The interpreter previously loaded it silently, staged the out-of-domain token, and fabricated a spurious `stringFalschesMuster` runtime error. **The fix:** `ModelLoader.requireConsistentEnumSValue` rejects a declared `sValue` not in the enum's `values` with a corrective `IllegalArgumentException` naming the field, the value, and `MVK_S_VALUE_INVALID` — the model-consistency sibling of IF145's prepared-boundary guard (loud, never silent).

*Locked:* (a) tri-engine `adapter.laws.StagedIndexDefaultInterpolationDiffTest` (absent + present-empty staged arms render `[]`, a non-staged EXPRESS baseline renders `[EXPRESS]`; dynamic-Groovy == java-static == interpreter) + multiplatform `interpreter.StagedIndexDefaultInterpolationMultiplatformTest` (JVM + Node). (b) `adapter.laws.OutOfDomainEnumSValueRejectionTest` (the kernel MVK stance via `RuntimeLaws.consistencyMessages` + the interpreter loader's matching loud refusal + an in-domain control) + multiplatform `interpreter.load.EnumSValueLoadTest` (JVM + Node). Fix in `commonMain` (`eval/Message.kt`, `eval/Document.kt` `messageDisplayValueAt`, `ValidationPass.callerDoc`, `DmInterpreter` message rendering; `load/ModelLoader.kt` `requireConsistentEnumSValue`) → JVM + JS. Kernel facts: KERNEL-FINDINGS §4/§12 "Two-corner supplement (IG104 probe)".

### IF223 — the model's configured condition language selects legal stored syntax and remains executable provenance through mounting (FIXED 2026-07-26; graduated from IG28) <a id="if223"></a>

IG28's premise was false: English storage in the German A12 Tools corpus is a serializer/workspace convention, not a legal-input restriction. An own-domain DM-JSON model with `content.modelConfig.conditionLanguage.code = de_DE` and German rule/computation fragments passes the real Kernel consistency gate and executes identically through dynamic Groovy and generated Java. The discriminating mounted Kernel fixture covers `Fuer`, `Mit`, localized predicates/functions, and the measured `Und`/`und`/`UND` plus `Oder`/`oder`/`ODER` families. The broader `@Von`, constants, and pattern/tolerance vocabulary is locked by the portable parser/remapper matrices rather than that Kernel fixture.

The common loader now carries `ConditionLanguage` on `ModelDef`, and every parser entry point selects canonical AST operators through that language. The bilingual identifier map is checked against the live operator catalog by localizing every catalog condition/computation example and requiring its German parse to equal the English canonical AST; every identifier keyword must occur in that population, with `RegelKontext` covered by one explicit grammar-only witness. Symbol operators and `In` remain language-independent. Recognition is language-exclusive: only a function head recognized in the selected language acquires a canonical evaluator identity, and the `@From` grammar token must be exactly `From` in English or `Von` in German. An English model may still have a path segment named `Fuer` or `Heute`, and a German model may have one named `For` or `Today`; the other language's vocabulary is not silently reserved.

Workspace preparation carries the source condition language on each mounted executable namespace, rather than inheriting it from an intermediate or final host while references are being moved. Reference scanning therefore recognizes only the source grammar's separators while remapping rule conditions and every computation fragment through nested mounts. The Java-reader parity path keeps an identity-keyed executable-language side table for the same remapping reason. One exact shared profile matrix snapshots both scanners' complete accepted separator, non-reference-atom, custom-condition, and `@From` vocabularies, so either implementation widening independently fails its guard.

An expanded model still has exactly one entry-level condition language. A German-localized provider mounted through English intermediate and entry models is therefore Kernel-invalid: `checkConsistency` rejects the expanded model with `MVK_UNEXPECTED_TOKEN`; the fixture does not claim one notification per retained executable. A provider whose configured language differs but whose retained fragment is language-neutral remains legal. The portable assembler requires the remapped fragment to parse to the same canonical AST under both languages and fails only the first class with one provenance-rich `INCOMPATIBLE_CONDITION_LANGUAGE` diagnostic per physical provider namespace; it does not reject configuration inequality alone.

*Locked:* real-Kernel consistency plus tri-engine execution in `adapter.laws.IncludeAssemblyStrategyDiffTest.germanSingleRootMountAgreesAcrossBothKernelStrategies`; the mixed localized rejection and language-neutral acceptance in `adapter.WorkspacePreparationFixtureGuardTest` plus multiplatform `interpreter … ReferenceAssemblyTest`; bilingual catalog canonicalization in `adapter.laws.ParserCorpusSweepTest.germanCatalogPopulationCanonicalizesToTheEnglishAst`; multiplatform AST, case-family, and cross-language-name locks in `interpreter.ConditionLanguageParseTest`; language-specific scanner collisions in `interpreter … ReferenceFragmentRemapperTest` and `core.IncludeReferenceRemapTest`. Kernel fact amended in place at KERNEL-FINDINGS "Stored conditions are usually canonical English; configured German storage is also kernel-legal".

### IF224 — one bounded portable workspace assembler now serves JVM, Node, Chrome, and dmtool (SHIPPED 2026-07-26; graduated from IG68) <a id="if224"></a>

The commonMain `ModelWorkspace` boundary is the project's single portable resolver for raw model workspaces. It admits a finite immutable source map or collects one discovered exact-id closure through `ModelSourceProvider`, then resolves imported typedefs, mounts includes, normalizes opaque ids, and remaps executable references before either returning the assembler-only expanded JSON or continuing through the strict interpreter loader. dmtool's `model expand` stops at the first seam; its runtime verbs use the same assembler and then cross the evaluator-specific gate. The JVM/Jackson `ModelReader` remains the published Java index/metadata implementation and a permissive Kernel-parity comparator, not a third dmtool runtime resolver.

The browser qualification closes the host residual rather than assuming commonMain portability. A fresh Vite project installs the actual packed TypeScript tarball and runs it in system Chrome under the repository-relative Pages base. It replays every one of the 33 clock-independent Kernel-grounded corpus cases with exact result multisets and no unsupported programs, and it prepares the exact four-file include/typedef fixture through both `ModelWorkspace.fromSources` and asynchronous `ModelWorkspace.collect`. The two routes request the same deterministic discovered closure, produce the same fingerprint, and execute the mounted rule plus imported type constraints. The sole explicit-clock corpus case remains outside the v1 exported façade because that façade intentionally does not yet project clock injection; this is an explicit capability boundary, not a browser discrepancy.

The same typed-authored baseline carries host-discriminating decimal, regex/grapheme, and Europe/Berlin spring-DST rules, so the Chrome run exercises the known JS-host seams rather than merely parsing a model. Resource, integrity, cancellation, snapshot, and no-retry provider laws remain locked in the common JVM/Node estate; the browser gate proves the packaging/host boundary needed by this finding without duplicating those common algorithms.

*Locked:* `interpreter/consumer-tests/browser/src/qualification.ts` through `:interpreter:verifyExternalInterpreterBrowserConsumer`; `adapter … WorkspacePreparationFixtureGuardTest` keeps the browser models generated from the typed own-domain author; `WorkspacePreparationBaselineTest` replays the host discriminators on JVM and Node; the existing workspace parity and collector laws own the algorithmic invariants. Red evidence: `build/R4.2-host-fixture-red.log` and `build/R4.2-browser-qualification-red.log`.

### IF225 — browser support has a packed-package semantic gate and a ratified 250 ms adaptive-median first-answer bar (SHIPPED 2026-07-27; graduated from IG86) <a id="if225"></a>

Browser support is qualified independently rather than inferred from Node. IF224 owns semantic equivalence: the actual packed TypeScript artifact replays the portable corpus, include/typedef workspace, and host-sensitive decimal, regex, grapheme, and timezone cases in system Chrome. The performance side uses one fixed release scenario: B1 with 10,000 rows and exactly 4,900 `HQ` findings. Workspace preparation and placement materialization occur before timing; the timed region starts at `createInterpreter()` and ends only after complete `validateFull` results have been checked.

Corrected by the owner on 2026-07-27: the release bar is median **≤250 ms**. A series begins with 10 fresh Chrome processes on the pinned Apple M1/Chrome runner and expands to 50 only when the observed range is greater than half its median; no sample is discarded. The policy rejects an unsupported sample count, non-finite/non-positive sample, unstable unextended series, or above-bar median. The report records the exact commit, browser, OS, architecture, CPU, scenario, estimator, samples, and ratification date. The earlier unconditional 50-process/p95 series remain historical calibration evidence rather than the active protocol.

The bar is intentionally scenario- and runner-specific, not a promise that every browser/device completes arbitrary models within 250 ms. Ordinary browser verification runs the semantic smoke and policy unit tests; the explicit collector normally stops after 10 processes and expands only when its noise rule fires. The first corrected-protocol series measured the representative 200-row, 23.1 kB, zero-finding Document at 10.2 ms median and the 10,000-row/4,900-finding stress Document at 177.1 ms median; both stopped after 10 stable fresh-process samples. The earlier one-time 50-process characterization measured 10.6 ms and 178.8 ms medians respectively. The public showcase displays the current scoped results and separately labels one live visitor-browser sample without presenting it as a distribution statistic.

*Locked:* the browser performance-policy test supplies the boundary, adaptive-sampling, negative, and sample-validity controls; the browser performance collector enforces the policy while collecting; `:interpreter:measureExternalInterpreterBrowserFirstAnswer` installs the packed artifact, runs the semantic preflight, and writes `build/reports/interpreter-browser-performance/r5.2.json`. Red evidence: `build/R4.2-browser-performance-bar-red.log` and `build/R5.2-adaptive-browser-performance-red.log`.

### IF226 — public delivery is proven only by anonymous consumers after immutable publication (SHIPPED 2026-07-27; graduated from IG85) <a id="if226"></a>

Local archives and staged repositories prove package construction, not public deliverability. Stable `v0.9.1` therefore crossed four distinct boundaries: an immutable source tag and root-aligned version, a public rebuild that generated the exact cumulative mirror and six checksummed assets, a draft-only Pages deployment followed by production URL checks, and fresh anonymous consumers that resolved the Maven and TypeScript artifacts from their public URLs with no project substitution. The public source tag resolves to `548973358769e60bb7099f141af60a703b3c30ea`; the generated public release tag and GitHub release `targetCommitish` both resolve to mirror commit `c64cee8d4be55bb279a0616e49207a0f37407c86`, authored and committed by `Martin Backschat <mbackschat@mail.de>`. The build workflow sets and verifies that release metadata only after the atomic mirror/tag push, because the draft necessarily exists before its final mirror commit.

GitHub's visibility boundary makes one narrow public window unavoidable: draft assets cannot be reacquired anonymously. The publisher consequently verifies every authenticated artifact and Pages route while the release is draft, publishes it as non-latest, anonymously reacquires and verifies the complete payload, runs the existing isolated Kotlin/JVM and TypeScript consumers, and only then marks a stable tag latest. The first anonymous JVM attempt hit a transient Plugin Portal resolution failure; the release correctly remained public but non-latest, and the resumable path repeated both clean consumers before promotion. This is why “latest” is an outcome of consumer proof rather than an input to publication.

A fresh public repository also has no Pages site merely because a deployment workflow exists. The production backend now makes that repository boundary explicit: before dispatch it creates a missing Pages site, or changes an existing legacy site, with `build_type=workflow`. The first attempted deployment failed safely at `actions/configure-pages` before release mutation; the next deployment succeeded after the site was enabled, and the permanent guard covers both create and update paths.

Post-publication user review found one presentation gap rather than an artifact failure: the Gradle repository prefix `/maven` resolved its exact POM and consumer correctly, but GitHub Pages does not synthesize directory listings, so opening that base URL returned 404. The cumulative assembler now generates `/maven/index.html` with the current coordinate, Gradle configuration, and exact POM link. Documentation-only refreshes of a published release are explicit workflow inputs and must prove every immutable versioned API and Maven path is byte-unchanged from the release tag before Pages deployment.

*Locked:* `InterpreterReleasePublisherRehearsalTest` owns the stable draft→Pages→non-latest→anonymous-consumers→latest state machine, the fresh-Pages bootstrap structure, release-target alignment, and immutable-path guard for a published Pages refresh; `InterpreterReleaseMirrorAssemblerTest` requires the rendered Maven landing page; `scripts/rehearse-interpreter-release.sh` exercises publication failure and resume against a disposable host. Production evidence is public build run `30301710222`, Pages deployment `30302937170`, the exact `SHA256SUMS`/`release-manifest.json` release assets, and the live Pages root, showcase, versioned/latest API references, and Maven POM. Red evidence: `build/R7.2-publisher-rehearsal-red.log`, `build/R7.2-resumable-promotion-red.log`, `build/R8.1-pages-enablement-red.log`, `build/R9.1-release-target-red.log`, `build/R9.1-maven-landing-red.log`, and `build/R9.1-published-pages-refresh-red.log`.

### IF227 — the public Document boundary reads and writes A12's own Document JSON; the invented placement transport is gone (SHIPPED 2026-07-28) <a id="if227"></a>

The interpreter's public Document boundary had shipped an **invented** shape — `DocumentInputV1{groups:[{path,coordinates}], fields:[{path,coordinates,raw}]}` — beside A12's real, kernel-serialized Document JSON. Every justification written into it turned out to be something A12 already does (KERNEL-FINDINGS "The canonical Document JSON and `DocumentPointer` string"). `PreparedModel.documents` now speaks the canonical shape in both directions and the placement transport is deleted with no deprecated bridge; `DocumentInputV1`, `GroupPlacementInput`, and `FieldPlacementInput` no longer exist on any target.

The read path is deliberately **exactly as permissive as A12's reader**, because a client-side refusal needs a measured kernel basis: `DocumentV2JsonReader.repetitionsFromJson` never consults repeatability, so an object at a repeating group is one row at repetition 1 and an **array at a non-repeating group is rows 1..n**. That keeps an over-repetition — including a non-repeatable group's coordinate 2, the input the old transport existed to carry — representable at the boundary, so the formal over-repetition gate stays reachable through the public API. A `null` field value keeps the cell **present with no value** (`FieldInstanceV2.ofValue(null)`, measured through `parseStringValueCommon`), which is `Document.emptyField`, never an omission; `""` reaches the same evaluation state while retaining its distinct raw text.

The write path is canonical instead: array versus object comes from *declared* repeatability, direct fields precede subgroups with each sorted by name (the kernel writer's key order), and a stored value is emitted as a **native JSON literal only when the text already is that literal** for the declared kind — otherwise as a JSON string, which is the kernel's own pass-through for a value it could not convert. That rule makes `read(encode(d))` reproduce `d` cell for cell while every emitted document stays inside A12's shape. The one document state A12's writer has no shape for — a non-repeatable group row above 1, where the kernel's own writer only asserts — raises `DocumentEncodeError` naming the canonical pointer rather than silently dropping the row. A field cell with a repetition above 1 is unreachable through this boundary and is an internal invariant, not a public error.

Two smells were removed with it rather than carried forward: `DocumentPlacementKind` duplicated the public `ModelEntityKind` (two enums, two conversion `when`s, one concept), and `maxCoordinatesPerPlacement` duplicated `maxModelNestingDepth` once coordinates became structural. The new `maxDocumentCodeUnits` closes a guarantee the ingress change would otherwise have weakened: the caller now supplies text, so the pre-parse size ceiling has to exist for the documented denial-of-service bound to stay true.

**The row ceiling had to be recalibrated, because the shape changed what "a row" costs to state.** The old envelope left ancestor rows *implicit* — `Document.field` auto-instantiated them **after** validation, so they never met `maxGroupPlacements`. In A12 Document JSON every row is explicit, so a document with N repetitions of one group presents N + ancestors rows: the project's own qualified 10,000-row browser stress document is **10,001**, which the former 10,000 default refused. Group rows and field cells now share one pair of ceilings (100,000 default / 1,000,000 hard), because a row ceiling below the cell ceiling refuses documents whose cells are admitted. Found by the browser gate, not by reasoning — the default was calibrated against a shape that no longer exists.

**Number I/O matches all three kernel stages, and the emitted form is the PLAIN one (owner directive 2026-07-28: adopt kernel JSON I/O behaviour exactly).** Reading needs no parse work — kotlinx retains the JSON literal verbatim, so the interpreter never sees a binary float and `250.00` survives where a default Jackson mapper would already have produced `250.0`. Writing did need work: A12 runs its generator with `WRITE_BIGDECIMAL_AS_PLAIN`, so it **never emits scientific notation**, and echoing the input literal diverged for `1e5` (kernel `100000`), `1.2e-3` (kernel `0.0012`), and `-0.0` (kernel `0.0`). `A12DocumentJson.plainNumberOrNull` reproduces `BigDecimal.toPlainString()` as a **purely lexical** transformation — deliberately not routed through the `Dec` evaluation type, whose rounding/precision model a transport must not impose. Text that is not a JSON number still writes as a string, the kernel's own pass-through, so a malformed value can never produce invalid JSON.

**The read side is value-normalizing too, and the target is the PLAIN form — corrected 2026-07-28 by kernel probe.** *This paragraph replaces an earlier one that named the `asText()` form (`1E+5`) as the faithful target and called the plain form a regression, on the reasoning that `BigDecimal("1E+5")` has scale −5 while `BigDecimal("100000")` has scale 0 and "scale is observable". That reasoning had an unmeasured link — which text the runtime actually validates — and the measurement refutes it.* The kernel's runtime does not read the stored `BigDecimal`'s scale; it reads a **string** rendered from it by `MetaField.convertFromBasicType` → `FormatDefinitionZahl`, which is `bd.setScale(max(bd.stripTrailingZeros().scale(), minFractionalDigits)).toPlainString()`. Discriminating cases, all probed through the kernel's real serializer + runtime (KERNEL-FINDINGS "The canonical Document JSON …"): on a `maxFractionalDigits: 0` field, `1e5` draws **no** error (so the runtime text is `100000`, not `1E+5`, which would be `zahlHatUngueltigeZeichen`), while `0.0000001` draws `zahlHatDezimalTrenner` (so it is plain, not `1E-7`). The `asText()` form would therefore have *created* an evaluation divergence rather than avoided one. And the plain form loses nothing: a negative scale is unobservable on both channels — the writer expands it (`WRITE_BIGDECIMAL_AS_PLAIN`) and the runtime floors it (`max(…, minFractionalDigits ≥ 0)`) — while a non-negative scale is preserved exactly by `toPlainString`.

So `readDocument` now normalizes **every typed kind** (`A12DocumentJson.cellText`, named `normalizedCellValue` until IF231 gave it the second rendering), each rule measured, not reasoned: NUMBER through `canonicalNumberOrNull` — the same lexical plain form over A12's own converter gate, `new BigDecimal(String)`, which is broader than JSON's grammar (`"+5"` → `5`, `"007"` → `7`, `1.5e1` → `15`) and skips only a `leadingZerosAllowed` field's leading-zero value, A12's one number case that stays text; BOOLEAN/CONFIRM case-insensitively (`"TRUE"` → `true`; a confirm has no false, so even a native `false` is a pass-through written back as `"false"`); `""` on any typed kind to the **empty cell**, written back as `null`; and the **date family and STRING/Enumeration not at all**. The date family was the one kind nobody had measured, and the measurement is decisive in the other direction: `ValidationDateParser` is non-lenient, so `2024-3-5`, `20240305`, `14:5:0`, `1.1.2024-31.12.2024` all *fail* conversion and are kept verbatim — there is nothing to normalize, because anything that parses already is the model format. The pass-through survives everywhere: a value the converter cannot parse stays verbatim ([A12K-3638]).

Three things this changed that are worth stating plainly. (1) It is a **convergence, not a trade**: `1e5`, `"+5"`, `"007"`, and `-0.0` each used to draw a formal error the kernel does not report (`zahlHatUngueltigeZeichen`, `zahlMitPlus`, `zahlHatFuehrendeNullen`, `zahlNullEnthaeltVorzeichen`), and now agree. (2) One residual was left standing: A12 renders one stored decimal *twice*, and a single stored text cannot satisfy both, so this step held the write-faithful one and left the eval side where it already was. *Closed 2026-07-29 by [IF231](#if231) — and the "structural, cannot be closed by a better normalization" framing this paragraph carried was wrong twice over: the cell already held two texts (`value`/`raw`, split by IF124), and the runtime rendering is derivable from the stored one, so no second text is stored. It was also only half the split — the runtime PADS as well as strips.* (3) A **denial-of-service hole was closed on the way**: `toPlainString` has no ceiling, so `1e999999999` — twelve characters of input — would allocate a billion; both lexical functions now refuse an expansion beyond the hard per-field ceiling and take the pass-through instead. That hole predated this change on the *write* path.

*Oracle:* `A12NumberPlainFormTest` compares **both** gates — the JSON-grammar write gate and the broader `new BigDecimal(String)` read gate — against `java.math.BigDecimal.toPlainString` itself, asserts the plain form is its own fixed point, never scientific, and numerically equal to its input, and pins the read gate to accept exactly what `BigDecimal` accepts. The oracle has now caught two hand-derivation errors: `BigDecimal` short-circuits zero at a negative scale (`-0e5` is `0`, not `000000`), and a leading zero is not part of the unscaled value (`007` is `7`, `00.50` is `0.50`). `adapter.laws.A12JsonIngressKernelTest` records the kernel's own ingress over a 55-row matrix (authored value → re-serialized JSON → fired codes), and `adapter.laws.A12JsonIngressDiffTest` runs the interpreter's ingress against it with an exhaustive, named residual set, so a new disagreement *and* a silently fixed one both fail. `DocumentTransportTest` locks the read normalization and the encode on JVM **and** Kotlin/JS.

**Two step-1 leftovers surfaced here and are fixed.** `A12PointerFormat` (the pointer utility, step 1 of the consolidation) was **broken on Kotlin/JS**: Kotlin/JS compiles a `Regex` in unicode (`u`) mode, where a lone unescaped `]` is a `SyntaxError`, so the pattern object never constructed and every call hit `undefined` — 9 of its 13 tests failed on the JS target while all 13 passed on the JVM. Escaping the bracket fixes it on both. The lesson is procedural: a `commonMain` addition is verified by `:interpreter:check` (which runs `jsNodeTest`), never by `jvmTest` alone. Step 1 had also left the ABI locks, `docs/SEMANTICS-MAP.md`, and the `llms.txt` type inventory unaware of its two new public types — the last because the build's `publicKlibTypeNames` regex omitted `object` while the test's twin included it, so a public Kotlin `object` silently dropped out of the generated inventory.

**Follow-up 2026-07-28 — the model-level codec pair, added so dmtool stops carrying its own (step 3 of the consolidation).** `PreparedModel.documents` is owner-bound and snapshot-returning, which is right for a library consumer but unreachable for one that works in `ModelDef`/`Document` — and dmtool's runtime verbs and `model seed` do, through the already public `ModelLoader.load` / `ModelDef.newDocument()` / `SeedGenerator`. Lacking a codec at that level, the CLI had written its own reader (an instance walker) and its own writer (a seed sink), and the adapter's performance-fixture guard a third, near-identical writer. Two functions close it — `ModelDef.readDocumentJson(json, limits = defaults())` and `Document.toDocumentJson(model)` — deliberately **two signatures rather than a public `A12DocumentJson` object** (owner directive: the module is a published library with reviewed ABI locks). They carry no document identity: a root `id` is still validated on read but nothing at this level can keep it. `toDocumentJson` is compact because A12 itself takes the pretty-printer as caller configuration.

What that consolidation *fixed*, measured through the `dmtool` binary before and after: the CLI's own walker had the A12 tree shape but not A12's value-normalizing ingress **behaviour**, so on a `maxFractionalDigits: 0` field `1e5` drew `zahlHatUngueltigeZeichen`, `"+5"` `zahlMitPlus`, and `"007"` `zahlHatFuehrendeNullen` — three formal errors the kernel does not report for those spellings (`A12JsonIngressKernelTest` rows). The interpreter had already converged on that (this finding, above); the CLI had not, because it was a second implementation. **The general lesson is the one the whole shape-consolidation program is about: matching a shape in a second implementation is not the same as matching its behaviour, and the only durable fix is to have one implementation.** `cli … DocumentInstanceIngressTest` locks each row end-to-end through the binary; `interpreter … DocumentJsonCodecTest` locks the pair itself.

*Locked:* `interpreter … DocumentTransportTest` (tree→rows/cells mapping, native-JSON scale survival, the `JsonNull` mapping, sparse `{}` rows, canonical key order and array-vs-object, the encode fixed point, aggregated diagnostics located by canonical pointer, the root `id`, the over-repetition read plus the non-repeatable encode refusal, every resource ceiling, owner identity); `PublicInterpreterApiTest`; `PublicPreparationApiSurfaceTest` (the public type inventory); the five consumer suites plus the browser qualification replay every corpus case through the new ingress — a replay the read normalization cannot perturb, because every committed corpus value is checked to be already canonical (plain numbers, plain strings, model-format dates).

### IF228 — A12's parse CONFIGURATION is part of its I/O behaviour: comments are legal, and one shared `A12_JSON` carries that at every A12-shape boundary (FIXED 2026-07-28) <a id="if228"></a>

Matching A12's Document JSON shape, then parsing it with a **default** parser, refuses documents A12 reads. `ObjectMapperProvider` builds both of A12's mappers with `JsonParser.Feature.ALLOW_COMMENTS` — `createObjectMapperForDocSerializer()` (document) and `createPreconfiguredObjectMapper()` (model) — so a commented model source or document is a **legal A12 artifact**, not input we may be generous about at our discretion. Neither mapper enables `ALLOW_TRAILING_COMMA`, so the parity is two-sided: comments in, trailing comma out. KERNEL-FINDINGS "The canonical Document JSON and `DocumentPointer` string" carries the source basis.

Every A12-shape parse in the module had been an independent `Json.parseToJsonElement` on the **default** instance, so all of them refused a comment with a corrective error — measured, not assumed: the document codec raised `DocumentInputError`, `ModelLoader` `IllegalArgumentException`, and workspace preparation `ModelPreparationError`. The fix is one `internal val A12_JSON` behind all six sites (the document codec, `ModelLoader`, `ModelCollection`, `WorkspaceAdmission.headerId`, `WorkspaceAssembly.parsed`, and the three DM-JSON parses in `CaseJson`), so the configuration is a **single decision** instead of a per-call-site accident. The conformance **case envelope** deliberately stays on the default instance: that is our own shape, and inheriting A12's leniency there would widen our format with no counterpart to cite.

**Corrected 2026-07-28, same day: the sweep was scoped to `:interpreter` and the class had a SEVENTH site.** `core.ModelReader` — the JVM model-read boundary behind every `dmtool` model verb, rulekit, modelkit, and `edit` — is a Jackson mapper that already carried A12's **number** configuration (`USE_BIG_DECIMAL_FOR_FLOATS` + `STRIP_TRAILING_BIGDECIMAL_ZEROES` disabled) and was missing the **comment** one, so a commented model was rejected on the JVM path after this finding had declared the class closed. Now `ALLOW_COMMENTS` too. The tell was visible in the file itself and is the reusable one: **a mapper matched to A12 for one concern is evidence that its OTHER concerns were never audited** — the configuration had been copied for the defect someone was chasing (scale), not derived from A12's factory as a whole. **Corrected again 2026-07-28, by independent review: the class was STILL open, four sites later.** `rulekit.validate.RuleValidator`, `rulekit.validate.ModelVersion`, `cli.ExportCommand`, and `core.ReferenceResolvers` each held their own default mapper, all reachable through the binary: a commented model made `rule add --dry-run` and `computation add --dry-run` fail as "the kernel could not process the candidate rule", `export rules`/`export card` exit 2, an included commented model vanish as `RK_UNRESOLVED_REFERENCE` while sitting on disk, and `model check` stop reporting version skew. The worst was `rule check`, which returned `valid:false` with `verification: "KERNEL_CONFIRMED"` on a model **the kernel had never been handed** — a false provenance claim caused by our own Jackson configuration. Now configured, and locked by role: `cli … CommentedModelSourceTest.everyVerbThatParsesTheModelReadsACommentedOne` uses the review's own discriminating cases and was verified to fail without the fix. **The repeated lesson, which two corrections did not learn:** the first pass scoped the sweep by *module*, the second re-scoped it to "the JVM model-read boundary" (one class, `ModelReader`) — still a *place*, not the *role*. A class defined by role is closed only by enumerating every site that performs the role and showing the enumeration.

**Corrected 2026-07-29 — the enumeration is now an executable build input, not another hand sweep.** `A12JsonConstructionGuardTest` scans the production sources of `core`, `modelkit`, `rulekit`, `edit`, `schemakit`, `cli`, `adapter`, and all three interpreter source sets for Jackson mapper construction and use of kotlinx's default `Json`. Its exact file/kind/ordinal allowlist carries a non-blank role basis for each of the 111 reviewed constructions; a new site fails closed and a removed site makes its row stale. The dedicated `jsonConstructionGuardTest` task declares those source trees as inputs and runs under the root test lifecycle. Running the enumeration found the remaining A12-role readers and writers instead of trusting the review's candidate list: raw reads in `LintBackstop`, `GitBaseline`, and `ProfileSynthesizeCommand` now go through `ModelDocument`; `InterpreterModelLoader`, `ModelTreeData`, and `JsonSchemaExporter` use explicit A12 configuration; the JSON-Schema importer's JSON **and YAML** readers preserve lexical decimal scale; and `CaseJson.write` now parses its embedded A12 `document` member with `A12_JSON`. That last site contradicted this finding's own shared-codec claim and the comment beside it: the case envelope remains deliberately strict/default, but its A12-owned member does not. `CaseJsonTest.writingACaseParsesItsDocumentMemberWithTheSharedA12Codec` locks the distinction.

The earlier lesson about the sweep: "every parse site" was scoped by *module* when the class is defined by *role* (parsing an A12-owned shape), and a class boundary drawn at a module edge will always miss the other side of it. Found by auditing the remaining boundary for step 5 of the shape consolidation, not by a failing test — nothing had covered it. Locked by `cli … CommentedModelSourceTest` end-to-end through the binary, including `model check`, so the comment is proven to survive into the **kernel's own** reader and not merely past ours.

Two things make this worth a numbered finding rather than a one-line fix.

**It is the shape/behaviour split again, one layer lower than IF227 found it.** IF227's lesson was that a second implementation can match a tree and diverge on value normalization; this is the same defect in the *parser configuration*, which is even easier to miss because it produces no different tree for any input either engine accepts — it only changes the **set of accepted inputs**. The plan document's step-3 survey had asserted "no walker rewrite is needed — it is already the A12 shape", and its mapper-configuration item was read as a *number* concern; both readings were true about the shape and blind to this.

**Half of it was a regression the consolidation introduced, and a deleted test had been the only thing holding it.** The retired `DocInstance` mapper did configure `ALLOW_COMMENTS`, locked by `DocInstanceNumberFidelityTest.commentsAreAcceptedBecauseA12AcceptsThem`. When that class was replaced by `DocumentInstanceIngressTest`, the number rows were faithfully re-expressed at the new boundary and this one row was not — so the capability left with the test that proved it. The procedural point: when a test class is deleted in a consolidation, the *inventory* of what it locked is the checklist, not the subset the change was about. The model-source half was pre-existing and undocumented.

*Locked:* `interpreter … A12JsonCommentToleranceTest` — a commented document reads to the identical canonical encoding, a commented model source loads identically through both `ModelLoader` and workspace preparation (whose admission/assembly/collection parses a per-call-site fix would have missed), and a trailing comma is still refused; run on JVM **and** Kotlin/JS. `cli … DocumentInstanceIngressTest.aCommentedInstanceDocumentIsAcceptedBecauseA12AcceptsOne` restores the end-to-end lock through the binary.

### IF229 — a malformed BOOLEAN / CONFIRM token is a formal error, and the kernel's check is case-SENSITIVE (FIXED 2026-07-28; graduated from IG84 — the *second* gap to carry that code, see the note below) <a id="if229"></a>

The interpreter had **no formal check for a boolean or confirm token at all**: `formalError`'s `Kind.BOOLEAN, Kind.CONFIRM -> null` arm returned clean, so `"yes"` on a boolean and `false` on a confirm evaluated as `false` rather than making the cell formally invalid (unknown, suppressing the referencing rule). The kernel reports `feldJaNeinFalsch` and `feldJaFalsch`. Fixed with two `FormalErrorCode`s carrying **our own** EN/DE text (the structural channels match the kernel; only the wording is ours — the license boundary, IF3).

**The measurement corrected the gap row that requested the fix.** IG84 recorded, as a checked-at-the-same-time aside, that "the evaluator is **case-insensitive** on boolean text (`typedValue`'s `raw.equals("true", ignoreCase = true)` … matching the kernel's `TRUE_STRING.equalsIgnoreCase`)" and concluded that storage normalization was therefore safe. That observation is about **reading a stored value**, and it does not transfer to the **formal check**, which the probe shows is case-**sensitive**:

| authored token (internal path) | kernel |
|---|---|
| boolean `true`, `false` | clean |
| boolean `TRUE`, `FALSE`, `True` | `feldJaNeinFalsch` |
| boolean `yes`, `1`, `0`, `wahr` | `feldJaNeinFalsch` |
| confirm `true` | clean |
| confirm `TRUE`, `false`, `FALSE`, `yes`, `1` | `feldJaFalsch` |

**Corrected 2026-07-29 — the third item was a source observation stated as full-engine behavior, and its reachability had not been measured.** Two runtime layers are established: (1) **JSON ingress** normalizes `"TRUE"` → `true` case-insensitively before storage, matching A12's converter (IF227); (2) the **formal check** on a stored token is case-sensitive, so a caller who fills a `Document` directly with `"TRUE"` gets `feldJaNeinFalsch`. `typedValue` does contain a case-insensitive comparison, but a discriminating full-engine differential shows the formal gate wins first on every plausible unguarded route tested: for stored `TRUE` and `True`, a direct `== True` rule and `NumberOfValueInFields(True In …)` are unknown/suppressed, while a bare boolean-copy computation clears its stale target and reports `feldJaNeinFalsch` in `formalErrorsInOperands`. Dynamic-Groovy, static-Java, and the interpreter agree. Therefore case-insensitive stored-value evaluation is **not a verified runtime tier**; it remains a lower-level implementation detail with no demonstrated reachability for a case variant. The general lesson is the one this program keeps re-learning at a new layer: *an observation about one layer carries no authority over another, and a source branch carries no reachability claim until the complete engine reaches it.*

**The condition tier is a separate live trap for the next reader:** in a *condition*, the comparison literal is the **capitalized** `True`/`False` (`[Active] == True`; a lowercase `true` is `MVK_UNEXPECTED_TOKEN`), which [`skill/SKILL.md`](../skill/SKILL.md) states correctly. An independent review executed every assertion in the skill's boolean/confirm bullet **and** its empty-string bullet through the current `dmtool rule check` binary; every accept/reject claim behaved as written, so the skill needed no edit. The previous basis here — "verified by reading the line" — did not meet the project's execution bar. The verified distinctions are now: condition literal `True`, stored token `true` (case-sensitive formal gate), and ingress normalization (case-insensitive); the case-insensitive lower-level stored-value comparison is explicitly source-only as above. KERNEL-SEMANTICS §1's stored-value paragraph names the distinction so the canonical doc cannot be misread the way the gap row was.

The gate IG84 set is met, with one correction to its own text: it asked for "the **two** rows removed from `A12JsonIngressDiffTest.KNOWN_DIVERGENCES`" — the set held **three** (`ExpressShipping="yes"`, `TermsConfirmed=false`, `TermsConfirmed="false"`), all now gone. Because that assertion is an exact set, a silently fixed residual fails it just as a new divergence does, which is what forced the count to be reconciled rather than assumed.

**Ledger-hygiene note: `IG84` names two different gaps in this repository's history.** IF137 ("the numerical NFR contract") also graduated from an IG84 — the earlier occupant of the code — and the boolean gap was later minted under the same number. Both citations are live in the docs, so a bare "IG84" is ambiguous: prefer the `IF` number. This is recorded rather than renumbered, because a finding is never renumbered and the gap rows themselves are deleted on completion.

**Amended 2026-07-29 — the token set is `true`/`false` for EVERY A12 model, not just a "default profile".** A review of `FormatDefinitionJaNein` (which `.equals()`-compares against `jaWert`/`neinWert`, overridable via `DarstellungsInformationIntern.getWahrWert()`) read this finding's "lowercase is literal" as over-generalized from one fixture, and raised our hard-coded check as a refusal broader than its measured basis — the second gap numbered IG88, now **withdrawn**. The discriminating case refutes it. On a model whose `BooleanType` declares `notInDCustomTrueValue: "Y"` / `notInDCustomFalseValue: "N"` and whose `ConfirmType` declares `"Y"`:

| authored token, on a model DECLARING `Y`/`N` | kernel |
|---|---|
| boolean `true`, `false` | clean |
| boolean `Y`, `N` | `feldJaNeinFalsch` |
| confirm `true` | clean |
| confirm `Y`, `false` | `feldJaFalsch` |

Byte-identical to a model that declares nothing — so implementing the gate IG88 asked for (load the tokens, compare against them) would have *introduced* a divergence, in the name of removing one. The mechanism, its three links, and the recall limit of the enumeration live in KERNEL-FINDINGS §2 "Value-token representation" (the kernel fact's home): the keys are `@NotInD` — "ignored while generating the validation code" — `TypeConverter` gates them behind a `considerNotInD` that only an out-of-tree ExtMP caller sets, and the runtime override lane has no channel on `DocumentProcessingConfig` and is explicitly nulled by the md-runtime service. **What made the wrong reading credible is worth keeping:** every individual source observation in it was correct. It failed as a *reachability* claim, because the links between "this comparison is configurable" and "a model can configure it" were never attacked — the oracle-by-claim-kind rule's last row, and the same shape as this finding's original layer-confusion lesson, one level up. Two near-misses in the measurement itself: the first probe run was **vacuous** (an injection that silently failed to match the pretty-printed model compared a model with itself and "confirmed" agreement), and a probe that only ran the kernel could not tell "the key is ignored by validation" from "the key was dropped by the reader" — both are now assertions in the lock rather than steps in a transcript.

*Locked:* `adapter.laws.BooleanTokenFormalDiffTest` — a 15-row token matrix on the **internal** path (programmatic `Document`), each row's kernel verdict compared against the interpreter's, with an empty divergence set so a new disagreement and a regression both fail; plus `tokensDeclaredByTheModelDoNotMoveTheFormalCheck`, the declared-token matrix over the `models/return-tokens.dm.json` fixture, which first proves the fixture's tokens survive A12's own model round trip (else the test is vacuous) and that the model is kernel-CONSISTENT, then pins the kernel's verdicts and compares the interpreter's. `adapter.laws.BooleanTokenEvaluationReachabilityDiffTest` attacks the direct-rule, aggregate, and computation routes in both kernel strategies and the interpreter, locking the reachability downgrade above. Verified load-bearing: teaching `formalError` to accept `Y`/`N` — the change IG88 requested — turns it red on the differential arm. The ingress path stays locked by `A12JsonIngressDiffTest`.

### IF230 — the conformance corpus carries A12 Document JSON, so the corpus finally tests the READER a reimplementation must get right (SHIPPED 2026-07-28) <a id="if230"></a>

Every committed case stored its instance as `placements[]` — an ordered list of `{kind, path, reps, value}` records, the serialized `PlacementSink` protocol, and the third of the three shapes this project had invented for a concept A12 already defines (KERNEL-FINDINGS "The canonical Document JSON …"). The consequence was worse than the duplication: a runner built the document through an **internal construction path**, so the corpus — the artifact a third-party reimplementation replays to demonstrate compatibility — exercised everything *except* the public document reader. The part most likely to be got wrong was the part the regression net did not cover.

Each case now carries `document`, the canonical shape, and the **interpreter** legs (JVM and Node) feed it to the public reader.

*Corrected 2026-07-28 by independent review:* this paragraph first said "**every** runner feeds it to its own public reader", which is false. The **kernel** legs do not: `CorpusReplay` converts the document back to placement records (`placementsForKernelLeg`) and builds a kernel document from those, so they run A12's V1 construction path over *our* reader's output rather than A12's own `deserializeV2`. The headline claim — that the corpus now tests the reader a reimplementation must get right — holds for the engine under test and not for the oracle. What keeps the net sound is that all three legs compare against the committed anchor rather than against each other, so leg independence survives; the overstatement was in the wording. The improvement is tracked in CONFORMANCE-CORPUS-SPEC §13.

**The migration went through the codec, not a converter.** Each case's placements were replayed into a `Document` on the internal path, then `toDocumentJson` produced the committed text — so the corpus holds exactly the bytes A12's own writer would produce, and nothing re-derived array-versus-object from row counts instead of from *declared* repeatability. Writing a second converter for the migration would have reproduced, one last time, the defect being removed.

**The measurement that made the step cheap.** The plan had specified a *holdout* mechanism — cases the canonical shape cannot address would keep `placements`, each recording its reason. A scan of all 34 cases found **zero** unaddressable coordinates (no repetition below 1, no `UNKNOWN_REP` sentinel), so no holdout exists and the mechanism was never built. The narrowing that made this possible was IF227's measurement that A12's reader takes an **array at a non-repeating group** as rows 1..n, which is what keeps an over-repetition representable.

**Two encodings were corrected on the way.** The browser qualification's transitional converter mapped an unfilled cell to `""`; A12's encoding is `null` (present with no value), and `""` is a *distinct raw text* that merely reaches the same evaluation state on a typed kind. The corpus now carries `null`. That converter is deleted with this step, along with the `CorpusPlacement` type — the reader under test receives the committed bytes verbatim.

**Where the retired protocol legitimately survives.** `PlacementSink` and the `Placement` record are *capture-side*: the generator builds a scenario before any case exists, and the resulting document is then serialized canonically. Keeping them there is not a residual — it is the correct boundary, and CONFORMANCE-CORPUS-SPEC §4 now says so.

**A permanent guard replaces the scaffolding.** `CorpusDocumentCanonicalTest` asserts every committed document is the codec's **fixed point**. It exists because A12's reader is deliberately permissive: a non-canonical encoding would still replay green, so drift would be invisible until someone diffed a regenerated file. The migration test itself was deleted — it was scaffolding, not a guard.

**One test lost coverage, deliberately and recorded.** `ComputeGranularityTest` used to wrap the placement **list** in a read-counting decorator and assert that preparation indexed each element exactly once with no further reads per projected result. `Document.placementsSnapshot()` offers no such interception seam, and the property is now structural (`prepare` fills a map once; `project` only reads it). The test asserts what stays observable — a prepared projection is reusable and order-independent — and says in-place that it was reduced and why, rather than being quietly deleted.

*Locked:* `interpreter … CorpusDocumentCanonicalTest` (canonical fixed point, JVM+JS), `CaseJsonTest` (the `document` round trip and the spec-shape fixture), `adapter … CorpusReplayTest` + `CorpusReplayCommonTest` (the replay itself, now through the public reader on JVM and Node), `CorpusSchemaGuardTest` (the schema's `document` member).

### IF231 — a NUMBER cell carries A12's TWO renderings, because the regime — not the channel — picks which text evaluation reads (FIXED 2026-07-29; graduated from IG85 + IG87) <a id="if231"></a>

A12 renders one stored decimal **twice**, and the split is real in both directions: the serializer writes `bd.toPlainString()` while the runtime hands evaluation `bd.setScale(max(bd.stripTrailingZeros().scale(), minFractionalDigits)).toPlainString()` (KERNEL-FINDINGS "The canonical Document JSON …"). IF227 stored the write-faithful text and ran the formal checks against it, which produced false errors on values a user plainly writes — `250.00` on a `maxFractionalDigits: 0` field drew `zahlHatDezimalTrenner`, and on a `minFractionalDigits: 2` field `250` drew `zahlOhneDezimalTrenner` while `250.0`/`250.5` drew `zahlFalscheAnzahlNachkommastellen`. All four are gone; so are two nobody had found, below.

**The mechanism is not "the formal check reads the wrong text" — it is that the text alone cannot say which rendering is due.** The regime is a property of the stored value's Java **type**, not of its spelling: a `BigDecimal`-valued cell (Document-JSON ingress) is converted by `ValidationData.getValue`, a `String`-valued cell (`createFieldInstance(path, reps, String)`) is returned **unchanged**. So the *same* text gets opposite verdicts, kernel-measured: `250.00` on `maxFrac 0` is `zahlHatDezimalTrenner` as a String and clean as a BigDecimal; `250` on `minFrac 2` is `zahlOhneDezimalTrenner` as a String and clean as a BigDecimal. This is why the tempting one-line fix — derive the rendering inside the NUMBER formal check — is **wrong**: it fixes the JSON arm by breaking the direct-fill arm.

**That refutation was EXECUTED, not reasoned** (the distinction this series exists to enforce — IG88 was refuted only when someone implemented its gate and watched the differential). The unconditional derive was applied literally inside `typeFormatErrorCached`'s `Kind.NUMBER` branch and the differential run:

| arm | result |
|---|---|
| `A12JsonIngressDiffTest` | **green** — the reported rows really do get fixed |
| `NumberRenderChannelDiffTest` | **red**: `V1 Weight=250.00` expected `[zahlHatDezimalTrenner]`, got `[]` |
| `OverRepetitionFormalGateDiffTest.malformedOverLimitDescendantExposesOverRepetitionNotFormat` | **red**: kernel `zahlOhneDezimalTrenner@/Policy[1]/Coverages[1]/Premium`, interpreter silent |

Exactly the signature the drill names: *the named arms go green and an unrelated, pre-existing lock reddens ⟹ the mechanism is wrong.* Had only the four reported rows been in view, this fix would have shipped looking correct. *(`build/refute.log`; the experiment was reverted, not committed.)*

**The fix needed no new cell state, because the cell already carried two texts.** `Placement` has held `value` (the evaluation text) beside `raw` (the stored text) since IF124 split them for CRLF→LF at eval ingestion. A12's number conversion is a second, independent reason for the same split, so `A12DocumentJson` — the one place that knows the value was converted — now emits both: `renderedField(path, evaluationValue, storedValue, …)`. The encoder and `cellStateAt` keep reading `raw`, so **read→write identity on a document A12 itself wrote is untouched**; every evaluation channel reads `value`; and a direct `Document.field(path, text)` fill sets both to the one text, which *is* A12's String-valued cell. Cost on the hot path: none — the derivation happens once per cell at ingress, where the model constraints are already in hand, and evaluation still reads one string.

`A12DocumentJson.runtimeNumberForm` is that derivation, **purely lexical** for the same reason `plainNumberOrNull` is: routing a transport value through `Dec` would impose the evaluation model's rounding on a rendering that must not have one. Nothing is ever rounded — the destination scale is by construction at least the value's own significant scale, so only trailing zeros are added or dropped.

**IG85's stated cost was false, and worth recording as a pattern.** The row said closing this "requires a cell-model change … in the interpreter's hottest data structure, which trades against the module's stated performance priority — an owner design call, not a local fix". Both halves were wrong: the two-text cell already existed, and one of the two renderings is *derivable*, so nothing extra is stored. The row had reasoned from "our cell holds one text" without checking whether it did. **A cost claim used to defer work needs the same oracle as any other claim** — here, reading the record it was about.

**Two divergences nobody had found, because no test could reach them.** `IG87` noted that `A12JsonIngressDiffTest.KNOWN_DIVERGENCES` called itself "the exhaustive, characterized residual" while being exhaustive only over its generator, whose model declared no `minFractionalDigits`. Adding `/Order/LineTotal` made the padding direction expressible. But the **length bounds** were outside *both* matrices: `min/maxLength` on a NUMBER count characters of the *rendered* text, so a stored `250.00` on a `maxFrac 2, minLength 4` field is `zahlZuKurz` in the kernel (rendered `250`, 3 chars) and was clean for us, and a stored `250` on a `minFrac 2, maxLength 5` field is `zahlZuLang` (rendered `250.00`, 6 chars) where we said `zahlOhneDezimalTrenner`. Those two are also the **discriminating cases for the `max(…, minFractionalDigits)` term itself**, which until now rested on a separator code: a bare `stripTrailingZeros()` predicts 3 characters where the kernel measures 6. An exhaustive set is only exhaustive over its generator — and a *second* generator with a different observable is what finds what the first could not express.

**What the channel sweep settled, so it is not re-investigated.** The question "which consumer needs the write-faithful text?" has the answer **none**: `ValidationData.getValue` renders once per regime and every channel reads that text. Measured per channel in KERNEL-FINDINGS: the formal check and `FieldValueAsString` read the regime's rendering identically; the semantic index, `uniqueIndex`, and `RepetitionNotUnique` are rendering-**insensitive** (the first two normalize both sides through the same `convertFromBasicType`, the third compares decimals); a `pattern` on a NUMBER field is not a channel at all — A12's model reader rejects the key. `$field.value$` interpolation is a **third** rendering (`getValueForDisplay` → `convertToUI`) and is deliberately left where it is: it reads `raw`, a pre-existing divergence on a different accessor, which this change neither creates nor fixes and which needs its own oracle.

*Locked:* `adapter.laws.NumberRenderChannelKernelTest` (the kernel's channel matrix over `models/decimal-render.dm.json`, both regimes, with the fixture's declarations proven to bind through A12's own model serializer) + `NumberRenderChannelDiffTest` (ours against it, including the V1 arm that refutes the unconditional fix and a byte-identical write-back); `A12JsonIngressKernelTest`/`A12JsonIngressDiffTest` (the padding direction now reachable; `KNOWN_DIVERGENCES` is **empty**, measured); `interpreter … A12RuntimeNumberFormTest` (the rendering against `java.math.BigDecimal.setScale` itself, at every declared minimum 0–4, plus the cases that must genuinely differ from the serializer's text — the sibling oracle to `A12NumberPlainFormTest`); `cli … DocumentInstanceIngressTest` (the boundary row now agrees with the kernel instead of being a characterized residual). Red-before-green: the three main sources stashed, `NumberRenderChannelDiffTest` 4/5 red and `A12JsonIngressDiffTest` red with all four rows named — `build/red1.log`.

### IF232 — `Time` construction is a zero-to-three-component prefix, and omitted trailing components are fixed zero (FIXED 2026-07-30; graduated from IG105, reconciles SPEC-2026-07-27-05) <a id="if232"></a>

The kernel accepts all four arities: `Time()` is 00:00:00, `Time("10")` is 10:00:00, `Time("10","30")` is 10:30:00, and the third component supplies seconds. This is a prefix contract, not three optional coordinates: minute cannot be supplied without hour. A quoted component has the clock-position range gate but no display-width gate, so hour `"1"` is accepted while `"24"` is refused.

The interpreter had two independent erasures of the shorter forms: the parser's general function-argument reader required one argument, and `evalTime` required exactly three. The fix makes only the canonical `Time` keyword admit an empty argument list and lets the existing zero-initialized component array supply absent trailing values; no other function silently gains zero arity. On the typed side, `TimeConstruction` now owns an immutable zero-to-three-element `components` list and `Dsl.time` exposes the four legal String/`FieldRef` overload families. The adapter retains the exact kernel arity on read-back instead of manufacturing absent operands.

*Locked:* `DateComponentConstantLawsTest.timeAcceptsZeroToThreePrefixComponents` and `aTimeConstantUsesTheClockRangeWithoutAWidthGate` settle admission against the kernel; `DateComponentConstantDiffTest.omittedTimeComponentsDefaultToZero` tri-checks dynamic Groovy, generated Java, and the interpreter; multiplatform `DateComponentConstantTest` pins JVM+Node; `DateTimeExprReadTest.roundTripsTimeAndDateTimeConstruction` and `DateConditionTest.rendersTimeAndDateTimeConstruction` cover typed read/render for all four arities.

### IF233 — partial DateType zero components form a monotone suffix; unknown month selects before day, unknown year stays non-relevant (FIXED 2026-07-30; graduated from IG106, reconciles SPEC-2026-07-27-03) <a id="if233"></a>

The kernel's four DateType precisions are a widening domain, not four unrelated formats. `FULL` admits only a concrete date; `DAY_OPTIONAL` also admits day `00`; `MONTH_OPTIONAL` also admits month `00` only after day `00`; `YEAR_OPTIONAL` also admits year `0000` only after both. Full and narrower values remain legal under every wider mode. An exact-format token with an illegal omission order is `datumFalsch`—a distinct formal class from wrong width, punctuation, or non-numeric text (`datumFormatFalsch`).

`ValueAsDate` chooses boundaries in component order. For `00.00.2024`, FirstDay selects January then day 1, while LastDay selects December then day 31. An unknown year exits as non-relevant before either choice: `ValueAsDate(00.00.0000, FirstDay) == "01.01.2000"` is UNKNOWN, and an `Or` discriminator separates it from an ordinary empty field. The year 2000 used internally by the formal checker never becomes an operation value.

The implementation extends the existing `DatePrecision` axis and keeps one owner for every stage: `ModelLoader` maps all four A12 enum values; `partialDateParts` retains and validates the suffix; `formalError` temporarily completes admitted unknowns for validation; `Document` keeps a partial direct value unknown; and `resolvePartialDate` alone selects `ValueAsDate` boundaries. The obsolete `UnsupportedDatePrecisionException` and preparation failure branch are gone; the public `UNSUPPORTED_DATE_PRECISION` diagnostic member remains for compatibility but has no current DateType producer.

*Locked:* `PartialDatePrecisionDiffTest` compares the legal suffixes, both month boundaries, known-year day omissions under both wider modes, unknown-year/empty/year-2000 separation, and both illegal mixed suffixes across dynamic Groovy, generated Java, and the interpreter. `PartialDatePrecisionTest`, `ModelLoaderTest`, and `PreparationApiTest` pin the same model/runtime contract on JVM and Node. Red-before-green: the new loader test failed on the explicit refusal; the runtime tests failed at the absent enum, and the first kernel discriminator corrected the expected illegal-suffix code from `datumFormatFalsch` to measured `datumFalsch`.

### IF234 — declared String and exact-year Date fields are temporal constructor components, converted only at that boundary (FIXED 2026-07-30; graduated from IG107, reconciles SPEC-2026-07-27-10/-08/-13) <a id="if234"></a>

The kernel's `Date(...)`/`Time(...)` component union is wider than the interpreter's stored value kinds. Its serializable declaration matrix admits bounded Number fields, exact-width digit-pattern String fields, and an exact-`yyyy` Date field only in complete Year; the precise positional gates and refusal separators are recorded in KERNEL-FINDINGS §6 and locked by `TemporalComponentDeclarationLawsTest`. At runtime the admitted String and exact-year Date sources contribute their displayed component digits, preserving leading zeroes until the constructor converts that text to an integer. Empty stays an empty component, a formal source remains unavailable, and an out-of-clock/calendar value stays present-but-unreal rather than being clamped.

The interpreter previously evaluated a component through the ordinary value-expression route. That made a String source nonnumeric and parsed an exact-`yyyy` Date source as though `FULL` always meant a complete calendar date; the formal pass therefore rejected a kernel-legal year before the constructor could see it. The correction is deliberately local: the exact `format == "yyyy"` Date declaration gets its year-fragment formal check and contributes its stored text only inside `analyzeDate`; ordinary Date consumers retain the existing full-date contract. This is the discriminating boundary—globally widening `FULL` Date parsing would make an incomplete date leak into comparisons and arithmetic the kernel does not admit.

The typed surface now names the constructor's closed source union as `TemporalComponent` (`FieldRef`, `NumberExpr`, or a lexical `TemporalComponentLiteral`) and leaves the declaration/position decision to kernel validation. `Dsl.temporalComponent("01")` preserves quoted digit text exactly, while matching extractor expressions compose through the `NumberExpr` arm. Kernel adapter read-back returns the same typed rendering.

*Locked:* `TemporalComponentDeclarationLawsTest` is the complete serializable static matrix; `TemporalComponentFieldDiffTest` covers typed authoring/read-back and compares leading zero, out-of-range, empty, and formal-source cases across dynamic Groovy, generated Java, and the JVM interpreter; `TemporalComponentFieldTest` pins the same evaluator contract on JVM and Node. `DslFacadeTest` and `DateConditionTest` guard the closed public union and bare-field rendering.

### IF235 — temporal constructor extractors preserve source reason and order; `TimeFromDateTime` projects only the wall clock (FIXED 2026-07-30; graduated from IG108, reconciles SPEC-2026-07-27-12/-09/-07) <a id="if235"></a>

A matching Date/Time component extractor is not a numeric expression evaluated in isolation. Its nested temporal source is evaluated first, then the component is selected, and the source's no-value reason remains load-bearing at the constructor boundary. An empty source contributes zero with missing provenance, so the constructor remains incomplete; a formal source stays non-relevant; a reached-but-valueless temporal expression contributes fixed zero. The discriminator is a domain-invalid sub-day shift: `HoursFromTime(AddHours(ScheduledAt, Quantity / 0))` supplies a fixed zero hour, while an empty `ScheduledAt` supplies a missing zero. These have the same number but opposite constructor behavior.

The interpreter's ordinary `dateComponent`/`timeComponent` route deliberately projects both an empty temporal value and a present valueless one to numeric zero for a direct numeric consumer. Reusing that route inside `Date(...)`/`Time(...)` erased the provenance too early: an empty Date extractor made `Invalid(Date(...))` VALUE instead of OMISSION, and an empty Time extractor manufactured midnight. The inverse defect was present too: a reached domain-failure shift became UNKNOWN instead of fixed zero. The root correction is one reason-bearing `temporalConstructionComponentInt` owner and a third `PRESENT_NO_VALUE` state. Direct extractor consumers still receive zero; constructors alone retain the distinction until their completeness/reality decision.

`TimeFromDateTime` maps a present source to its model-zone `HH:mm:ss` wall label and forwards a no-value reason unchanged. Its source date, offset, ambiguous-occurrence identity, and transport-only 1970 carrier never become Time semantics. `DateTime(ValueAsDate(partial, LastDay), TimeFromDateTime(source))` therefore resolves the Date first and composes the projected wall clock onto it; a formally unavailable Date side exits before evaluating the Time arm. Nested shifts and `Now` are evaluated before extraction, proven by the Berlin spring transition and a changing-clock control rather than inferred from a direct field case.

*Locked:* `TemporalExtractorComponentLawsTest` owns the matching-token, nonliteral-source, direct-literal, split-year, and complete-DateTime static gates. `TemporalExtractorComponentDiffTest` uses typed authoring and compares mixed Date components, empty/formal polarity, Berlin shift order, a domain-failure fixed zero, changing `Now`, partial-Date composition, empty `TimeFromDateTime`, and Date-side formal short-circuit across dynamic Groovy, generated Java, and the interpreter. `TemporalExtractorComponentTest` pins the same runtime matrix on JVM and Node. Existing `DateTimeExtractDiffTest`, `DstTimeZoneDiffTest`, and `TimeLiteralCarrierDiffTest` retain the ordinary, overlap-hour, and time-carrier controls.

### IF236 — String-pattern and requiredness messages are distinct field-owned template channels (FIXED 2026-07-30; graduated from IG109, reconciles EXP-2026-07-30-01) <a id="if236"></a>

A field-owned custom message is not a rule template. An `en_US` String-pattern message admits only the fixed lowercase `$field$` and `$field.value$` tokens; `$$` is rejected as an empty parameter, and inserted stored bytes are opaque. Requiredness is a second producer: the measured `Cost $$ $field$` form preserves the doubled pair and replaces only the field token. Dynamic Groovy and generated Java agree on every retained case under the same label-or-name provider.

The interpreter previously retained neither `StringType.errorMessage` nor `requirednessConfig.errorMessage`, so it emitted its own fallback text even where the model authored an exact message. `FieldDef` and `ModelLoader` now preserve the declarations as separate locale maps. A dedicated one-pass renderer owns each measured grammar; it never calls the rule/computation `renderMessage`, and unsupported producer forms or locales enter `ValidationOutcome.unsupported` rather than being guessed. `validateFull` therefore keeps its existing loud-refusal guarantee.

*Locked:* `FieldConstraintFormalDiffTest` retains the exact four-form static matrix, the `$$` rejection diagnostics, and the opaque-value dynamic/generated/interpreter comparison. `RequirednessDiffTest` compares the preserved-pair case across the same three engines. `FieldOwnedMessageTest` and `ModelLoaderTest.retainsPatternAndRequirednessMessagesAsDistinctChannels` pin rendering, refusal, and retention on JVM and Node. Existing rule/computation message locks remain unchanged.

### IF237 — the Date pre-1900 flag belongs to the shared basic check; partial Date computation targets are statically unreachable (FIXED 2026-07-30; reconciles SPEC-2026-07-27-01 by correction) <a id="if237"></a>

A12's `DateType.youngerThan1900Check` is independent of Date precision and the always-on Gregorian floor. When enabled, a concrete date strictly before 1900-01-01 is `datumFalsch`; 1900-01-01 is valid. The interpreter now retains the flag in `FieldDef`/`ModelLoader` and carries it through `FieldConstraints` into the one `typeFormatError` Date branch used by both full stored-input validation and computed-target classification. Partial-date completion still happens first, so an unknown year completes only for validation and is not misclassified as an ancient concrete value.

The motivating sync entry's runtime premise is false for kernel 30.8.1. Although every partial Date precision admits fully known stored values, a computation targeting such a field is rejected before execution: the generated implicit validation rule reads `[target]`, and a plain field-value operation over a partially-known Date is `MVK_INVALID_DATE_TYPE`. FULL is admitted; all three partial modes reject; the code cannot be suppressed. The interpreter therefore keeps its general formal owner truthful for foreign/direct models but does not claim kernel runtime reachability for partial computation targets.

*Locked:* `ComputedPartialDateTargetAdmissionDiffTest` is the terminal typed FULL/day/month/year target matrix plus suppression-defeat control. `FieldConfigExtrasTest` proves modelkit's emitted pre-1900 key is honored by the kernel, `DateBefore1900DiffTest` tri-checks the authorable FULL computed boundary, and `DateGregorianFloorFormalTest` plus `ModelLoaderTest.mapsDateBefore1900Check` pin the shared behavior and load on JVM and Node.

### IF238 — computed Time already retains exact zone-free clock identity; the kernel still classifies a source-identical clock as changed (VERIFIED 2026-07-30; reconciles SPEC-2026-07-29-02) <a id="if238"></a>

Both authorable producers retain the exact clock: `Time(Hour, Minute, Second)` and `TimeFromDateTime(2024-06-01T10:30:45)` compute `10:30:45` under UTC and Europe/Berlin. Applying either result and then evaluating typed Time comparisons still sees `10:30:45`, never the UTC decoding of the kernel V2 transport's 1970 model-zone `Instant`. Impossible or missing construction clears a stale target, and a formally invalid component remains in the formal operand channel and poisons the target to CLEARED. The interpreter already matched all value, poison, and application behavior; no production correction was needed.

The kernel result envelope adds a Time-specific classification: even when the source target already contains `10:30:45`, both dynamic Groovy and generated Java put the clean result in `getComputedFieldInstancesWithChanges`, so applying that already-classified result overwrites a different destination clock. `DmInterpreter.compute` deliberately remains report-all; IF239's public retained result now carries the source-relative action subset beside it, with Time always retained as VALUE. The exact reason the typed Time values fail the same-clock equality is not claimed: source inspection reaches `DocumentComputationResultImpl` and `FieldInstanceImpl.fieldValuesEqual`, but no discriminating case has selected the causal mechanism.

*Locked:* `ComputedTimeTargetDiffTest` owns exact constructed/projected clocks in UTC/Berlin, post-application Time identity, impossible/missing/formal clearing, both-strategy five-channel classification, and application to a separate destination. `ComputedTimeTargetTest` pins the evaluator and retained TIME action on JVM and Node. The generic unchanged control remains in `ComputeResultChannelsLawsTest`.

### IF239 — computation reports retain source-relative actions for immutable application to another owned Document (FIXED 2026-07-30; graduated from IG110, reconciles EXP-2026-07-30-02) <a id="if239"></a>

The report-all result and the application delta are different public observations. `ComputationReport.results` still contains every computed cell. Its immutable `actions` subset is classified once against the source: a typed-equal VALUE is omitted, a source-filled no-result target becomes CLEARED, ERRORED is retained unconditionally, and Time's measured same-clock exception remains VALUE. `applyTo(destination)` consumes those actions without recomputing on the destination. VALUE and CLEARED create the exactly addressed target and missing repeatable ancestry; ERRORED clears only an existing target; no action is a no-op.

Number equality uses the converted A12 `BigDecimal` identity, not the evaluation or serializer text: coefficient and scale must both agree. The Document ingress therefore retains that object identity beside its two existing renderings, including negative scale that plain-form JSON cannot reveal. This is transport identity only; no transport value is routed through the evaluator's rounded decimal type.

Compatibility uses the public API's existing owner invariant: a result can be applied only to a snapshot produced by the same `PreparedModel`, otherwise `ModelOwnerMismatchError` is thrown before mutation. This is an intentionally stronger library safety boundary, not an invented kernel rejection—the kernel leaves model compatibility as a caller precondition. An unsupported computation has no complete action set and applies atomically as a no-op. `Interpreter.applyComputations(source)` is exactly `compute(source).applyTo(source)`, so the same-document convenience and separate-destination route share one classification owner.

*Locked:* `SeparateDestinationClearDiffTest` runs the CLEARED/no-action/ERRORED/VALUE/filled-target discriminator through dynamic Groovy, generated Java, and the standalone interpreter route. `RetainedComputationResultTest` repeats that public contract, missing-row creation, source-relative no-recompute discriminator, owner refusal, immutability, and same-document composition on JVM and Node. `PublicInterpreterApiTest` pins coefficient-plus-scale Number identity, `ComputedTimeTargetTest` pins the same-clock Time action, `RetainedComputationFixtureGuardTest` keeps their shared model typed-authored, the packed TypeScript consumer exercises `actions`, `applyTo`, same-document composition, and owner refusal, and the staged Maven Java/Kotlin JVM plus Kotlin/JS consumers compile and execute both application forms.

### IF240 — a model-shaped starter is the existing Document seed preset, and its group tree must use the complete model inventory (FIXED 2026-07-31) <a id="if240"></a>

The browser showcase exposed an authoring problem rather than a rendering problem: after a user loaded an unfamiliar workspace, showing the raw DocumentModel JSON still left them to reconstruct the corresponding Document tree by hand. Making the four bundled examples larger helped only the known model. The reusable answer already existed as `SeedGenerator`, which backs `dmtool model seed`; the public prepared-model API simply lacked that capability.

`PreparedModel.documents.seedDocument()` is now the stable authoring preset: deterministic seed `0`, every declared group and field, and one row per repeatable group, returned as the same immutable owner-bound `DocumentSnapshot` as `readDocument`. `encodeDocument` writes it as canonical A12 Document JSON. Constraint-aware local values make the candidate useful, and an unconstructable local value remains a present-empty cell, but no claim is made that arbitrary cross-field rules accept it. The parameterized CLI remains `dmtool model seed --seed/--rows/--rows-default`; `profile` is disjoint because it describes or synthesizes a model performance workload rather than a Document.

Using the existing generator revealed a class defect in its tree construction. It derived groups from field ancestors plus repeatable groups, which omits a declared non-repeatable fieldless container. `ModelDef.groupPaths` is the already-established complete inventory, so the generator now builds from it and retains field/repeatable prefixes only as defensive closure. The fix is shared by the public API, dmtool, performance generation, and differential fuzzing.

The showcase treats the seed as authoring assistance, not destructive synchronization. Preparing a different model replaces only a stale bundled or previously seeded Document. A Document loaded from a file or edited in the textarea is preserved across preparation and loading-strategy changes. “Use model-shaped starter” is the explicit reset. The bundled scenarios themselves now show every field, leaving computation targets and unsupported safe samples as `null`; the self-contained permit model also declares the repeatable `SiteSections` group, and every bundled permit Document carries two populated rows while a generated starter carries one.

*Locked:* `SeedGeneratorTest.walksTheCompleteDeclaredGroupInventoryIncludingFieldlessGroups` is the second-instance root guard; `DocumentTransportTest.seedDocumentBuildsACompleteModelShapedCanonicalCandidate` pins all groups, all fields, one repeatable row, ownership, and canonical round-trip on JVM and Node; the packed TypeScript consumer checks the public bridge; the browser smoke gate checks complete built-in Documents, explicit regeneration, automatic uploaded-model replacement, preservation of the uploaded invalid Document across file-map/provider preparation, and the nested include/typedef model's complete effective field tree.

### IF241 — computation diagnostics share validation's public finding projection while retaining computation's two error channels (FIXED 2026-07-31; graduated from IAG9) <a id="if241"></a>

The public computation report previously preserved evaluation and application semantics but could not explain every unsuccessful result. A formally invalid operand could poison a reader to CLEARED without exposing the operand's error, and an ERRORED computed target carried its attempted value but not its target-owned error message. The report also omitted the Kernel's `noErrorOccurred` predicate.

The fix retains the Kernel's two distinct diagnostic channels. `ComputationReport.formalErrorsInOperands` is the eager checked-plan inventory: selected invalid ordinary operands, selected empty or duplicate indexes, and a computation-domain failure. An ERRORED `ComputationResult` carries its own `diagnostic`; a cause-blind poisoned dependent remains CLEARED without an invented target diagnostic. Both channels reuse the same detached `ValidationFinding` projection as validation, including code, formal owner, canonical error pointer, severity/type, field-owned rendered text, and immutable auxiliary path lists. The target check itself is shared with validation's basic formal checker, while computation deliberately excludes input-only text checks that the Kernel does not apply to computed values.

`noErrorOccurred` is true exactly when no result is ERRORED and `formalErrorsInOperands` is empty. `unsupported`/`fullySupported` remains independent, and computation continues after an eager operand error, so a report may contain a formal operand diagnostic, an errored target diagnostic, and successful unrelated results together. The `results`/`actions` distinction and retained-result application contract from IF239 are unchanged.
