*Locked:* `PublicComputationDiagnosticsDiffTest` compares dynamic Groovy, generated Java, and the public Kotlin/JVM report for clean computation, invalid ordinary operand, domain failure, invalid target, empty index, duplicate index, and both-channels-populated cases. `RetainedComputationResultTest` pins the public projection, validation/target diagnostic sharing, status predicate, and channel independence on JVM and Node. The staged Java, Kotlin/JVM, Kotlin/JS, and packed TypeScript consumers compile and execute the new getters/properties; the browser showcase smoke gate exercises both operand and target diagnostic scenarios and renders the complete report explicitly rather than spreading an opaque façade object.


### IF242 — the published pointer surface is TWO A12 types with FIVE domains, not one hybrid with one (FIXED 2026-08-01, corrected 2026-08-02; graduated from IAG10) <a id="if242"></a>

**The defect.** One published type, `A12Pointer`, carried the **union** of two A12 addressing domains and therefore belonged to neither. It documented itself as A12's exact `DocumentPointer` and enforced that type's last-part-only `0`, yet accepted `UNKNOWN_REPETITION` (`-5`) at any part — a value `DocumentPointerImpl`'s own part pattern (`([\w_-]+)(\[(\d+)?\])?`, unsigned) cannot read at all. Its *state* was equally invented: two parallel `names`/`repetitions` lists, a shape A12 uses only as factory arguments (`DocumentPointer.of(fullName, repetitions)`) and never as a pointer's state.

**Why the `-5` acceptance was not simply wrong, which is what made it durable.** The kernel really does emit `…/Demand[-5]/Headcount` on an unmatched parallel-iteration side, and a consumer really must read it back — so refusing `-5` outright would have been an over-rejection. That reasoning is sound and survived a review round as "deliberate and source-verified". What it missed is one layer up: A12 puts those values on a **different type**. `PartiallyKnownDocumentMultiPointer` is what all three of `IMessage`'s address accessors return and what `ICustomCondition` receives, its state *is* `fullName` + `repetitionIndexes`, and its conversion to an exact pointer is **partial** (`toDocumentPointer(): Optional<…>`). The right question was never "may an exact pointer carry `-5`" but "which A12 type is this channel".

**The shape.** Two nominally distinct types on every target, each matching its owner member for member — `A12Pointer` (`pathParts: List<A12PathPart>`, with `fullName`/`repetitionIndexes`/`size`/`partAtLevel` derived) and `A12PartiallyKnownMultiPointer` (`fullName` + `repetitionIndexes`, with `toExactPointer()`). The grammar is shared and single-sourced; only the repetition rule differs, and each type has exactly one enforcement site that all of its entry points call. Contract: [INTERPRETER-API-SPEC](INTERPRETER-API-SPEC.md) "The two pointer types". Kernel facts: [kf171](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf171).

**Three things worth carrying forward.**

1. **A `data class` cannot both validate and snapshot.** `init` cannot reassign a `val` constructor property, so a validating-and-snapshotting value object needs a private constructor plus a factory — which also removes the generated `copy` that would otherwise hand back an unchecked value. `A12PathPart` stays a `data class` precisely because it holds no collection: its `copy` re-runs the constructor and re-validates. The committed ABI dumps are what keep `A12Pointer`'s missing `copy` from coming back.
2. **Kotlin/JS erases `Int`, so the packed artifact is the only place a non-integer repetition is observable.** `Array<Int>` accepts `1.5`/`NaN`/`2^31` at runtime, and the A12 domain check reads `1.5 >= 1` as satisfied — it would emit `Order[1.5]`. The refusal belongs at the bridge, the layer where the erasure happens, and the test that proves it runs through the packed consumer, not through a Kotlin bridge call.
3. **Both over-rejections in this area were found by BUILDING, not by reading.** A bare `"/"` had been refused although `parseFullName` excludes exactly that string from its trailing-slash guard by name — and the way that surfaced was that the partially-known type's own `fullName()` for the root *is* `"/"` (its owner joins with a `"/"` prefix, where the exact type's yields `""`), so the shared splitter could not read back an accessor we had just written. Two source reads of the same method had missed it. That is the case for oracling a domain against the kernel's factories rather than against one's reading of them: `adapter … laws/A12PointerKernelDomainDiffTest` decides 25 cases on both sides, asserts the two deliberate over-strictnesses **as** divergences so a kernel upgrade that closes them fails the build, records whether `-ea` is actually in force (two of A12's own rules there are bare `assert`s), and carries a positive control proving its corpus discriminates the two domains.

**Corrected 2026-08-02, after an eight-row counterexample audit against the kernel factories.** The split above was right; five things about it were not, and every one was a claim reached by reading rather than by asking A12:

1. **One type does not mean one domain.** `[\w_-]+` went behind every entry point of both types. A12 puts it behind **one** — `DocumentPointer.of(String)` — while `PathPart` accepts any non-empty name, the part-list factory adds only the position rule, and the partially-known factory validates no names at all. Eight measured over-rejections, including on the read path. The grammar is now documented and enforced as the *string codec's* precondition; see [kf171](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf171)'s amendment.
2. **A `format`/`parse`/`render` trio for the partially-known type was an invented serialization.** A12 defines no pointer string for it — enumerated, not assumed. Deleted; the type publishes `of`, `WILDCARD`, `UNKNOWN`, `toExactPointer`. The dmtool-envelope exemption does not stretch to serializing an A12-owned domain object, and citing it was the error.
3. **`internal constructor` is not a closed constructor.** Kotlin mangles internal *functions*; a constructor is emitted as `ACC_PUBLIC` with no synthetic flag, and ordinary Java called it with `("anything at all", List.of(-7))` — no arity check, no domain check, no normalization. The ABI golden hides internal members, so it showed nothing either. Now private behind a factory, and `A12PointerConstructionClosedTest` asserts it against the **compiled** artifact with a positive control.
4. **"Nominally distinct on every target" was false for TypeScript.** Structural typing made an `A12Pointer` assignable to `A12PartiallyKnownMultiPointer` and passable to `toExactPointer`; only the reverse direction errored. Both types now carry a type-only `unique symbol` brand, with `@ts-expect-error` locks in the packed consumer — verified by deleting a brand and watching two directives go unused.
5. **The root conversion silently repaired a kernel edge.** `toDocumentPointer()` raises on an empty index list; ours returned the root pointer. Ours raises now, with the measurement in the message.

6. **And the message channel was never wired, while the API spec said it was.** `ValidationFinding.errorPath`, `referenced` and `fillToFix` still carried the interpreter's house-form `String`. A first attempt at this correction described that as blocked on a separate engine change and opened a gap for it; that was wrong twice — the wiring is the work IAG10 decided, and it is one commit, not two. **Fixed 2026-08-02 in the same correction:** the engine `Message` now *stores* `A12PartiallyKnownMultiPointer` for all three channels, built at the single shared `(path, reps)` source (`Document.messagePointer`, cached with the document index), with the house strings kept as **derived** projections for the conformance corpus, the `:adapter` differential and dmtool. The direction is forced rather than chosen: the house renderer writes an index on every segment **except the last**, so `[1,-5]` renders `/Plan[1]/Demand` and `[1,0]` renders `/Order[1]/Items` — both of this type's sentinels are exactly what the string drops, so a pointer can produce a string and a string cannot reproduce a pointer. That asymmetry is also why the CLI's exact-vs-house decision now reads the retained pointer instead of re-parsing the text: `/Plan[1]/Demand` parses cleanly as a *different* exact pointer, so a try-parse fallback silently promotes an unresolved address to a resolved-looking one. **Corrected again 2026-08-02 after the lock itself was reviewed:** `MessagePointerChannelTest` had constructed the already-public value directly, so it did not exercise the engine-to-public mapper it claimed to guard. It now sends terminal `3`, `0`, and `-5` through an actual engine `Message` and checks all three public channels. The packed TypeScript consumer holds the brand across a real finding and the browser corpus replay remains the end-to-end lock. The house projection stays tool-owned: an accidental root-package `MessagePointers` façade was removed from both Kotlin ABI goldens, and `InterpreterAbiGoldenDepthTest` sees the published formatter as its positive control before rejecting either house helper from the public ABI.

**The generalizable lesson, and it is not the one the first version drew.** That version's closing point was "both over-rejections were found by BUILDING, not reading" — true, and it still under-sold the mechanism. All five corrections above are the *same* error: a property was inferred from one entry point and applied to a family. Reading one factory tells you that factory's domain and nothing about its siblings, so the differential is now **partitioned by entry point** and forbidden from using one factory as another's oracle.

### IF243 — ABI-filter completeness must come from an unfiltered dump, not a second declaration model (FIXED 2026-08-02; correction to IRG3) <a id="if243"></a>

IRG3 correctly made both Kotlin goldens member-level, but its follow-up guard could not detect the second half of the defect it advertised. The JVM parser discarded every `$` type; the KLIB parser accepted only column-zero headers; and a hand-set floor of 50 let both inventories shrink together. The shipped files happened to contain the nested types, but the guard did not protect them.

The first replacement tried to reconstruct Kotlin's ABI from JVM reflection plus Kotlin metadata. Cold mutation review found a new omitted declaration class on every pass: KLIB accessors, then compiler-generated enum members, then JVM synthetic members. That was the same mechanism in a more elaborate form — a hand-built declaration model can only count the kinds its author remembered.

`InterpreterAbiGoldenDepthTest` now invokes Kotlin's own ABI engine a second time with **no filters**. For JVM it selects whole class blocks whose binary package is exactly the public root, so every member — including synthetic and future kinds — rides with the class. For KLIB it selects every engine-produced declaration line by the fully-qualified identity already printed in the dump, without parsing whether it is a type, function, property, accessor, enum entry, or a kind not yet known here. The configured golden must equal that mechanical projection after the authored non-contract root-name judgment. Engine subpackages are rejected by exact package identity, including future subpackages. One JVM wrinkle is made explicit rather than hidden: `Dec_jvmKt` is an owner-wide file-facade exclusion, so its **complete** unfiltered block has a separate reviewed lock; a new JVM-only top-level function in that file fails instead of silently inheriting the exclusion. Synthetic inputs containing unknown member/declaration syntax prove both projections are kind-agnostic. The old numeric floor and the reflection/metadata declaration census are gone.

### IF244 — packed TypeScript numeric validation is derived from caller-to-library declaration paths (FIXED 2026-08-02) <a id="if244"></a>

Kotlin and TypeScript compilation cannot prove the JavaScript values that reach an erased Kotlin/JS `Int`: a declared `number` may be `1.5`, `NaN`, infinity, or outside the signed 32-bit range. The packed consumer had cases for two pointer factories, one document value, and one coordinate, but nothing made a new numeric entry point inherit an obligation. Counting `number` lines in an authored ABI dump was rejected as the same shallow-denominator mechanism: aliases, nested options, callbacks, and reused types make lines neither entry points nor use sites.

The packed consumer now parses the declaration file installed from the real tarball with TypeScript's compiler API. It starts only from exported runtime values and traverses every callable and constructor parameter into arrays, aliases, unions, nested properties, recursive string indexes, and callback returns. Direction is explicit at every callable boundary: a caller-supplied callback's direct parameters are library-to-caller output, a package-owned method reached through that output reverses direction again for its arguments, and the callback's answer preserves caller-to-library direction. Ambient or inherited declarations and type-only exports do not become package entry points. The resulting 45 caller-to-library numeric use-site paths must reconcile exactly with an authored classification: 37 paths have hostile packed-artifact executions and eight are deliberately unvalidated data or package-owned opaque values. The hostile evidence set reconciles independently, so classifying a path as tested without executing it also fails.

The hostile families are domain-specific. Pointer repetitions cover fractional, non-finite, out-of-range, and invalid negative integers while retaining exact-pointer `0` and message-pointer `0`/`-5` controls. Model coordinates and both resource-limit routes add zero. Both document-read routes reject every native value JSON would drop, substitute, or fail to serialize while accepting a finite fractional number. Synthetic declaration controls cover nested optionals, arrays, recursive documents, callback direction, inherited `Error` members, missing and stale classifications, and missing executed evidence. This is explicitly a numeric-boundary guard, not a claim that arbitrary hostile values for every TypeScript type have been enumerated.

### IF245 — date-range bounds must resolve the polymorphic BaseYear source before ordinary range evaluation (FIXED 2026-08-02) <a id="if245"></a>

`StartOfDateRange(BaseYear)` and `EndOfDateRange(BaseYear)` are authorable and the kernel reads them as `01.01` and `31.12` of the configured reference year. The interpreter handled ordinary date-range fields but evaluated every bound source through `Val.Range`; `BaseYear` normally evaluates as its numeric form, so both legal expressions became unknown and never fired.

The bound evaluator now owns the polymorphic slot: `BaseYear` resolves directly to the first or last ISO calendar date, while every other source still has to resolve to a real range. The sibling endpoints share this one dispatch, so the fix cannot cover only the reported start case.

*Locked:* `BaseYearDiffTest.baseYearRangeBoundsAreTheFirstAndLastCalendarDays` compares dynamic Groovy, generated Java, and the interpreter at both endpoints plus a mid-year discriminator; `BaseYearRangeBoundTest` repeats the three outcomes on JVM and Node.

### IF246 — row-invariant starred aggregates fold once per validation call and star scope (FIXED 2026-08-03) <a id="if246"></a>

A per-row rule can read an aggregate whose domain does not change with the row, such as `[Amount] > Sum(Lines* / Amount)` while iterating `Lines`. The previous evaluator resolved and folded the complete star for every row. The diagnostic `latentQuadraticHunt` had exposed that cliff since 2026-06-25 and even named aggregate memoization as follow-up work, but it only printed a ratio: it was not a guard, not a live gap, and its comment's task number had no durable owner. The defect therefore remained green for more than five weeks. The release-time full sweep reproduced the consequence: 500→2,000 rows took 43.4→746.2 ms, 17.12× for 4× input.

The fix shares the result of an unfiltered, uncorrelated starred value aggregate across row interpreters only when their star-scope prefixes are identical. The cache key is the complete expression plus every operand's scope prefix, so separate parent rows cannot collide and multi-star operands retain all scope dimensions. It belongs to `ValidationPass`, not `EvalConfig` or `Document`: one validation call owns it, a reused configuration cannot leak values between documents, and a document cannot retain a model AST after validation. Compute mode deliberately receives no cache because its overlay mutates while target instances are evaluated, and memo eligibility independently refuses compute mode if a future caller supplies one. The shared mechanism covers `Sum`, `MaxValue`, `MinValue`, `NumberOfDifferentValues`, and `SumOfProducts`; filtered and `$`-correlated aggregates remain row-sensitive and bypass it.

**Measurement.** The final isolated tri-engine run at 500→2,000 rows measured generated Java at 29.66→433.80 ms (14.63×), dynamic Groovy at 35.23→475.94 ms (13.51×), and the repaired interpreter at 0.74→3.01 ms (4.07×). Both Kernel strategies therefore have the same optimization opportunity, although that does not weaken our own regression. **Mechanism basis.** The generated Java rule template evaluates `conditionBooleanCode` inside its `IterationState` loop, `RuntimeController.summe` directly invokes `combineFeldListe`, and `CalculationCache` stores computation results rather than validation-aggregate results.

*Locked:* `PerfGateTest.rowInvariantAggregateStaysSubQuadratic` is the does-fire regression guard (17.12× red, 4.51× final green); `AggregateMemoScopeTest` covers all five aggregate families, same-scope sharing, sibling-scope separation, and installs a shared memo in compute mode before mutating its overlay (stale `5` red, fresh `24` green) on JVM and Node; `KernelEdgeTest.rowInvariantAggregateKernelComparison` retains the opt-in tri-engine measurement and asserts result agreement before timing.

### IF247 — aggregate message operands retain the kernel's field-reference set, and filtered `FieldValuesNotUnique` uses TRUE_AF polarity (FIXED 2026-08-04) <a id="if247"></a>

The feedback discriminator was `FieldValuesNotUnique(/Order/Items*/Count)` with values `7, 5, 5`: both kernel strategies emit exactly one `VALUE_ERROR` at the first-row anchor `/Order[1]/Items[1]/Count`, referencing the wildcard operand `/Order[1]/Items[0]/Count`. The equivalent starred `Sum` comparison has the same one-message/wildcard-reference shape. `RepetitionNotUnique`, by contrast, is the per-row operator: the same duplicate cluster emits at the actual duplicate rows. dmtool converts the retained exact message address to canonical A12 exact syntax for output; that syntax has no leading slash and omits `[1]`, so the first-row pointer appears as `Order/Items/Count`. The interpreter's separate house form retains `/Order[1]/Items[1]/Count`. Neither spelling is a declaration-level address.

The interpreter already matched truth, message count, and error pointer after IF127, but its rich `referenced` channel was empty. `rowOutcomes` had reused `nonStarredRefs`, whose exclusion of aggregate stars is correct only for iteration and suppression, as the message-reference inventory. The root fix gives each prepared rule a separate message-reference plan. A starred operand contributes its `Having` fields and its value operand; repeatable levels at or below the star's bind depth become A12 wildcard coordinates, `$`-outer refs remain row-bound, and structural `CurrentRepetition` contributes no field pointer. A group-star recursively expands to contained fields because the kernel inventory is field-only. A filtered category `EntitySpec` traverses its source and `Having` operands instead of disappearing at the AST wrapper. RNU keeps its separate duplicate-cluster projection. The kernel exposes the resulting pointers as a set backed by incidental `HashSet` iteration order, so the contract is reference content, not list order.

The exact lock exposed a sibling polarity defect that the prior Boolean differential could not express. Adding a `Having` filter makes a firing `FieldValuesNotUnique` `OMISSION_ERROR`, even when every kept cell is filled. The generic TRUE_AF filter escalation existed but ran after `PredicateOp.polarity`, so this statically VALUE operator returned before reaching it. The measured exception now precedes static polarity only for filtered `FieldValuesNotUnique`; unfiltered uniqueness remains VALUE and the existing filtered fill-quantifier matrix remains unchanged. Narrowing this branch is load-bearing: the differential did not establish the same override for every statically typed predicate.

**Corrected 2026-08-05 ([IF249](#if249)).** The escalation shipped here was keyed on the operand list — a *sole* starred operand carrying a `Having` — because that was the only shape the differential then exercised. It is positional: what escalates is a filtered cell that yielded a compared value **before** the duplicate the scan returns at. The sole-starred results above are unchanged (the first kept value necessarily precedes the duplicate), so this correction widens the mechanism rather than overturning any measurement recorded here. The `predType` branch no longer inspects the argument list; it reads the polarity out of the same ordered scan that computes the truth.

*Locked:* `FieldValuesNotUniqueDiffTest.starredDuplicateEmitsOneFirstRowMessageWithItsWildcardReferenceInBothPlacements` compares dynamic Groovy, generated Java, and the interpreter at full message cardinality, error pointer, type, reference-set content, ordinary `Having` refs, structural `CurrentRepetition`, and wildcard pointers for both parent and repeated-group placement. `StarOnlyIterationScopeDiffTest.ownGroupStarOnlyCarriesItsWildcardReference` is the operator-independent `Sum` second instance; `OuterCorrelationDiffTest.outerCorrelationMessageReferencesKeepTheOuterRowAndWildcardTheInnerScope` proves `$`-outer/current-row and inner-wildcard operands coexist; `GroupStarValueAggregateDiffTest.validationMessageOverGroupStarRecursivelyReferencesExpandedFieldsNotGroups` locks recursive field-only expansion; `FirstFilledValueEntitySpecDiffTest.filteredCategoryEntitySpecRetainsItsHavingStarAndFallbackMessageReferences` locks the AST-wrapper sibling; and `HavingQuantifierPolarityDiffTest` retains the existing filtered-quantifier sibling class. Red-before-green: both exact aggregate differentials returned `referenced=[]`; the group-star emitted a group pointer; the `EntitySpec` operands disappeared; and after the pointer correction filtered uniqueness remained `VALUE_ERROR` against both kernels' `OMISSION_ERROR` until the operator-specific TRUE_AF branch was moved before static polarity.

### IF248 — an undeclared entity reference degrades three different confident ways; the runtime verbs now refuse it (FIXED 2026-08-04) <a id="if248"></a>

A condition or computation operation may name an entity the model does not declare. Such a model is invalid: `rule check --field /Policy/Coverages/CoverageCode --condition 'FieldNotFilled(NoSuchField)'` returns `valid:false` / `MVK_INVALID_ENTITY`, and injecting the same condition into a stored rule makes `model check` return the same code. There is therefore no legal input here and nothing to match — but the interpreter answered anyway, and its answer had three shapes, each locally reasonable and all three wrong to publish.

| operand position | what the engine reads | what the verb reported |
|---|---|---|
| presence (`FieldNotFilled(NoSuchField)`) | `filled(path)` is false for an absent path, so the predicate is TRUE | exit 0, `verdict:"fired"`, `type:"OMISSION_ERROR"`, `referenced:[]` |
| starred aggregate (`FieldValuesNotUnique(Items*/NoSuchField)`) | `starRows` over an undeclared group prefix yields an empty row domain, so the aggregate finds no duplicate | exit 0, `verdict:"passed"` — a clean bill of health |
| value (`[NoSuchNumber]` in a computation) | no empty reading exists; `ModelPathIndex` throws | exit 2, `computation failed: no kind declared for …` — no envelope, no code |

The empty `referenced` list beside a confident `OMISSION_ERROR` is the tell: the message names operands the engine never found. The two exit-0 rows are the dangerous half, and they are opposite in polarity, so no consumer-side heuristic can recover the truth from the answer.

**The reported scope was narrower than the class.** The gap was written up as candidate-only (`--condition`/`--field`), reasoning that dmtool's write paths gate every reference before storing — true, and irrelevant to a model that arrived from anywhere else. A stored rule carrying the same condition, injected into the model JSON, reproduced the identical false FIRED through both `rule eval <path>` and `model eval`. The gate therefore belongs at the evaluation entry, not behind the candidate flag.

**The fix is one value-independent query, and its risky half is breadth.** `DmInterpreter.undeclaredReferences()` (plus a candidate overload) walks every rule condition, computation guard, and computation operation and reports each named path that is not in the model's declared entity set. It is deliberately not folded into `supportAudit`: an undeclared reference is not a construct we cannot evaluate, and reporting it as `unsupported` would blame the engine for the model's defect while still exiting 0.

The walk is a new one rather than a reuse, because every existing reference walk omits paths on purpose — the scope view drops stars, the dependency view drops `CurrentRepetition`'s group operand and `EntitySpec` entirely, the message view drops a semantic-index target — and each omission would have become a reference the gate silently failed to check. Both `when`s are exhaustive over the sealed AST, so a new node cannot be added without deciding what it names. Two properties make the check no stricter than the kernel: the reference is already bound by the model's own rules before it is tested (declaring-group precedence, then the `fieldRefByShortNameAllowed` model-wide short name, IF119), and the parser stores a starred operand's path with its `*` markers already removed, so `Items* / Count` is tested as the declared `…/Items/Count`.

The CLI publishes it as `refused` / exit 2 / `RK_UNDECLARED_ENTITY_REFERENCE`, one diagnostic per reference so a consumer repairs all of them in one pass, on `rule eval`, `model eval`, and `model compute` alike. The interpreter's own library API is unchanged: it reports, and the caller decides.

**The query is offered at three scopes, because the whole-model scope is wrong for a focused verb.** The whole-model form forces every rule's AST, which would defeat `ParsedModel`'s parse-only-what-you-touch design — the property that lets `testRule` prepare exactly the rule it touches, and the reason the interpreter is on-par with the kernel at all rule counts. So `undeclaredReferences(rule)` answers for one condition, `undeclaredComputationReferences()` for the computations, and `undeclaredReferences()` for everything. Both bounds matter: too narrow lets a reached reference through, too wide refuses a focused question over an unrelated rule.

**Which halves a verb composes is decided by what it EVALUATES, and two verbs evaluate less than "rules + computations".** `model compute` validates nothing, so a stored rule's undeclared reference cannot reach its answer however broken it is — it takes the computation scope alone. A **focused** `rule eval --no-computations` skips the compute-and-apply step and then reaches only its own condition. Every other route composes both halves: a focused call *with* computations does too, because the form-engine flow applies them before validating, so a computation's reference is reached whichever rule the caller asked about; and `model eval --condition` takes the whole-model scope despite being a candidate route, since it appends the candidate and evaluates everything.

**Corrected 2026-08-04: `--no-computations` narrows a FOCUSED route only — whole-model `model eval --no-computations` still reaches the computations.** Narrowing it there was implemented first, on the reading that a skipped compute step puts the computations out of scope, and the run refuted it: the call died with `evaluation failed: document path is absent from the prepared model: /Subscription/Billing/NoSuchFee` — untyped, exactly the failure the gate exists to replace. The mechanism is that `validate` evaluates `parsedModel.implicitRules` — the implicit computation-validation rule per computation, `[F] != <operation>` (IF32/IF65) — *unconditionally*, so the operation is reached whether or not the compute-and-apply step ran; the flag governs `applyComputations`, not validation's rule set. The discriminating pair is in the lock: same model, same flag, the focused route passes and the whole-model route refuses, which no "the computations don't run" reading survives. `testRule`/`evalCandidate` look only in `model.rules`, which is why the focused route genuinely narrows.

*Locked:* `UndeclaredReferenceTest` (interpreter commonTest, JVM+JS) pins the three does-report faces plus twelve legal reference shapes as over-rejection controls — absolute, bare same-group, `..`-relative, the `..Name` turning point, `RuleGroup`, single and multi star, a whitespace-separated star, a group operand, both semantic-index key forms — and the `fieldRefByShortNameAllowed` on/off pair, where the same condition text must be accepted with the flag on and reported with it off. `UndeclaredEntityReferenceRefusalTest` (cli) locks the refusal on all five routes with its correctly-spelled control beside each, plus the four scope cases above (the two focused `--no-computations` routes and `model compute` must evaluate; whole-model `--no-computations` must still refuse); a mutant that makes the query return nothing fails 7 of its 10 refusal tests and leaves exactly the three controls green.

### IF249 — `FieldValuesNotUnique` is one ORDERED scan: the filter escalation is positional and an unconvertible cell is skipped, not a suppressor (FIXED 2026-08-05) <a id="if249"></a>

The reconciliation request is the lean project's `SPEC-2026-08-05-01`, and it named one of the two defects the probe found. Kernel side: [KF175](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf175).

**Two divergences, one root.** `fieldValuesNotUnique` collected every operand's values into a list and *then* scanned for a duplicate, which made both position-dependent facts unrepresentable:

- **Polarity.** [IF247](#if247)'s escalation branch tested `p.args.singleOrNull() as? Expr.Star` for a `Having`, so on a mixed direct-plus-starred list the interpreter reported `VALUE_ERROR` where both kernel strategies report `OMISSION_ERROR`.
- **Truth — the more serious half, which no ledger entry raised.** A filled-but-unconvertible operand returned `Tri.UNKNOWN`, suppressing the rule. The kernel skips that cell like an empty one, so the interpreter was **failing to fire rules the kernel fires** — a false negative in a validation result, on 4 of the 15 measured rows.

The fix is one function returning `Res`: walk the operands in authored order and the kept rows in row order; skip any cell with no comparable key (which is what an unconvertible value resolves to); set the escalation flag when a *keyed* cell came from a filtered operand; return `TRUE` with `OMISSION`/`VALUE` at the first repeated key. `evalPred` reads `.truth` and `predType` reads `.type` from the same scan — the shape the overlap predicates already used, so `predType` no longer inspects the argument list at all.

**Two rival readings were refuted rather than assumed away.** The kernel's runtime branches on whether the first operand is a direct Number field, which would predict stored-text comparison when a starred operand leads; measured both ways, `5` still duplicates `5.00`, so the interpreter's uniform numeric keying is not order-sensitive. And the skip is not temporal-specific — the Date rows behave as the Number rows, which is what licenses the kind-agnostic `distinctKeyOrNull(cell) ?: continue` instead of a Number-only carve-out.

**Coverage the admission correction opened.** [KF173](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf173) established that Time, DateTime, DateFragment and Custom operand lists are authorable, not just Date. Since the interpreter keys a compared value by Number/String/Boolean, a kind resolving to any other `Val` would silently never duplicate — and "kernel == interpreter" would be satisfied by two engines that both never compare it. All five kinds are therefore measured with an **equal/distinct pair**, the equal half asserted to fire: Date and DateTime inside the ordered-scan matrix, Time and DateFragment on the fixtures that already declare a same-format pair, and Custom on its own fixture with a validator registered on both sides. Custom adds a third row — two *rejected* equal values do not duplicate — which is the Custom analogue of the unconvertible-skip and confirms the rule is not kind-local.

*Locked:* `FieldValuesNotUniqueDiffTest.theOperandScanIsOrderedAndReturnsAtTheDuplicate` — 15 rows, each pinning the fired message type against dynamic Groovy and requiring the full `field|type|referenced` triple to agree across dynamic Groovy, generated Java, and the interpreter; the engine-agreement assertions deliberately run *before* the prediction pin, so a wrong prediction cannot mask a divergence. `everyAuthorableTemporalKindComparesOnItsValue` covers the Time and DateFragment kinds. Red-before-green: rows 2, 4, 5, 6 and 12 diverged before the fix — one polarity and four suppressions. The file's own `malformedOperandSuppresses` case was renamed, because its document has no duplicate once the unconvertible value leaves the comparison, so its boolean assertion held under both readings and could never have caught this.

### IF250 — a `DateType` whose declared FORMAT omits components resolves by fragment completion, not to Unknown (FIXED 2026-08-06, graduated from IG110) <a id="if250"></a>

The kernel admits `yyyy`, `yyyy-MM`, `yyyyMM` and `MM` as `DateType` formats and treats such a cell as a concrete date ([KF176](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf176)). The interpreter did neither half correctly: on `yyyy` the cell resolved to `Val.Unknown`, so **every referencing rule was silently suppressed** — the worst failure direction, a clean bill of health on a document the kernel has an opinion about — and on `yyyy-MM`, `yyyyMM` and `MM` the formal check emitted a **false `datumFormatFalsch`**, inventing an error the kernel does not report.

**The root cause was a half-applied exception, not a missing mechanism.** `SPEC-2026-07-27-13` (`Date(...)` admits a `yyyy` Year field) added an exact `fmt == "yyyy"` completion to `FormalError`'s FULL-precision branch, with a comment declining to infer the other incomplete formats — while `Document.resolveValue`'s FULL branch was left calling `parseDateToIso` alone. The two halves then disagreed *within this project*: the formal gate admitted a `yyyy` cell that resolution could not produce. Both are now `parseDateToIso(raw, fmt) ?: completeFragmentToIso(raw, fmt, baseYear)` — which is the shape the DATE_RANGE endpoint check beside it already used, so the fix is the house idiom rather than a new mechanism, and `completeFragmentToIso` needed no change: its `y = baseYear; month = 1; day = 1` defaults are exactly what the kernel does.

**The completion target was measured, and the year source discriminated.** The extractor for an omitted component is refused at codegen, so `MonthFromDate`/`DayFromDate` cannot read what a completion supplies — the target is only observable through comparison against a full-format sibling. Measured that way: `yyyy` `2020` → `2020-01-01` (not Dec 31, not mid-year), `yyyy-MM` `2020-03` → `2020-03-01` (not month-end), `yyyyMM` likewise, and `MM` `03` under a **non-default base year 2011** → `2011-03-01`, with 2000, 1970 and 2026 all failing. Without that non-default base year the row would have been satisfied by a hardcoded constant. A second, independent instance of the same mechanism: with no base year declared at all, comparing a `MM` field is refused outright at codegen (`MVK_INVALID_COMPARE_TO_DATE`), so the base year is a precondition and not merely a default.

**Sibling sweep.** The class is "a declared format that omits components, resolved by its kind's parser": `DATE_RANGE` already completed per endpoint (`parseRangeToIso`/`buildRangeEndpoint`), `DATE_FRAGMENT` already completed by construction, and TIME/DATE_TIME each admit exactly one kernel-legal format, so the two DATE sites above are the whole class rather than the two that were reported.

*Locked:* `adapter.laws.IncompleteDateFormatDiffTest` — the completion-target table across all four formats with its year-source discriminator, the admitted extractor still reading the stored component (so completion did not displace it), two-directional ordering plus presence on a `yyyy` cell, and a seven-row control that a value malformed *for its own incomplete format* (wrong width, out-of-range month, stray separator) is still `datumFormatFalsch` — the fix must not become a licence to accept anything. Every case asserts kernel/interpreter agreement **and** pins what the kernel itself reported, because agreement alone is satisfied by two equally silent engines, which is precisely the defect. The kernel-only admission half (the format whitelist, the per-format extractor matrix) is `validate.laws.IncompleteDateFormatAdmissionLawsTest`. Red-before-green: pre-fix, `yyyy` returned no message where the kernel fires, and the other three returned `datumFormatFalsch` where the kernel is silent.

### IF251 — model-static preparation is owned per MODEL, not per evaluator: the plans are configuration-independent and a concurrent service is the reason it matters (SHIPPED 2026-08-07) <a id="if251"></a>

`Interpreter` owned the whole of preparation — condition and operation ASTs, iteration scopes, `RepetitionNotUnique` plans, the model path index, the computation dependency order — while `PreparedModel`, the type whose KDoc calls itself "one model-static prepared owner domain", held only the `ModelDef`. So the P-SERVICE profile ([INTERPRETER-SPEC](INTERPRETER-SPEC.md) "The two consumer profiles", NFR-F3) could not be served: a bounded cache was keyed by **(model × configuration)**, and two tenants sharing a model each retained a full duplicate of every plan.

**The split is provable from source, not chosen by taste.** Every field of `PreparedRulePlan` — `ast`, `iteration`, `repetitionNotUnique`, `messageReferences`, `hasFilterConditions` — derives from the model and its AST alone. Configuration reaches rule preparation only through `auditCond`/`auditExpr`, which decide **acceptance** and contribute no plan content. `ParsedModel(model)`, `IterationScopes(model)`, `RepetitionNotUniqueEvaluator(model, scopes)` and `ModelPathIndex(model)` take no configuration at all, and `orderedFrom` needs only the model's computations and their parsed operations. So `ModelStaticPlans` owns the plans and `PreparedEvaluationPlan` is reduced to the per-evaluator audit verdict over them.

**Audit-before-build ordering is preserved deliberately.** Both halves can raise `UnsupportedConstructException` — the audit for a configuration reason, `repetitionNotUnique.prepare` for a model reason (more than one RNU in a condition) — and `validate` catches it per rule to exclude and report that rule. Building first would have changed which message an affected rule reports, so the verdict runs before the shared plan is forced.

**A warm shared instance cannot carry anything between evaluators.** None of the four shared collaborators (`ParsedModel`, `IterationScopes`, `RepetitionNotUniqueEvaluator`, `ModelPathIndex`) has class-level mutable state or any document- or configuration-derived state; their only `var`s are function-locals, and all six `LazyThreadSafetyMode.NONE` uses are call-local rather than shared. The one class-level `var` on the shared store itself is `ModelStaticPlans.preparationObserver`, an internal test seam absent from the public API and written only by the work-counter guards; it carries no evaluation state, but it IS shared between sibling evaluators, which the sibling guard relies on. `SharedPreparationIsolationTest` discriminates on the boundary the split created: two evaluators over one shared preparation differing only in whether a custom field type is registered, interleaved three times and each checked against a fresh-engine baseline, plus a document-crossover arm. It asserts the two configurations differ observably first, so it cannot pass vacuously.

**Concurrency is the stronger reason than reuse, and it is measured on the JVM.** `SharedPreparationConcurrencyTest` runs 8 threads × 25 requests over one shared prepared model through the public API on a 192-rule model: all 200 answers equal the sequential baseline, and a concurrent first touch builds **each** model-static plan exactly once, so the synchronized memos hold under race.

**Two consumer-facing facts fell out and belong to the contract.** A `PreparedModel`'s `fingerprint` cannot be a cache lookup key — obtaining it requires the preparation the cache exists to skip — and neither can a digest of the model body, because hashing requires fetching it. And content identity is not instance interchangeability: `ModelOwnerIdentity` is per instance and `requireOwned` compares by reference, so two prepared models over identical bytes share a fingerprint yet reject each other's objects with `ModelOwnerMismatchError`. That is why a request must resolve once and keep the instance it got, and it also makes eviction safe by construction, since an in-flight request's own reference keeps its entry alive.

**One observable message changed, and the owner ratified it (2026-08-07).** `orderedFrom` now consults the UNAUDITED parsed computation, because the dependency order is model-static. So a model carrying BOTH a dependency cycle and a configuration-unsupported computation now reports the cycle where it previously reported whichever audit failure the DFS reached first. The previous message was never a stable contract — it depended on visit order — and restoring it would put configuration back inside the shared model-static walk, defeating the split; a cyclic model is also rejected upstream by the kernel's consistency gate. Accepted rather than reverted.

**Corrected 2026-08-07, same day:** the resident-cost figures first recorded for this work were measured on the PRE-hoist ownership split and understated a served cache entry. Re-measured on the JVM afterwards, a warm entry is 5.8×–9.7× its expanded DM-JSON and grows on both the field and rule axes at roughly 1.6 kB each; the earlier "60–80 kB fixed floor" was the small model's own total, and its rules-only dose-response could not have supported a floor claim at all. A retained `Interpreter` now adds only 2–5% over a warm prepared model, where before the hoist it roughly doubled the cost — that delta is this change's effect, measured. Numbers and method: [INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE](INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE.md) "Per-model resident cost".

*Locked:* `PreparationWorkCounterTest` carries three arms. The hand-built arm proves a second evaluator over one shared preparation emits **zero** events while asserting that the FIRST evaluator exercised every `PreparationWork` class, so the denominator is not assumed; its positive control proves an unshared evaluator repeats exactly those classes; and a third arm runs the same property through the public `PreparedModel.createInterpreter()` boundary, attaching the observer BEFORE the first interpreter exists so the comparison cannot be empty-to-empty. A planted mutation making `PreparedModel.plans()` return a fresh store per call turns that third arm red and leaves the others green — the kernel differential cannot be this oracle, because moving ownership has no semantic effect. The pre-existing exact-count assertions in the same class still pass unchanged, so the hoist preserved the work counts. `ServiceCacheExample` in the staged-Maven external consumer executes the whole shape against the **published artifact**: 12 concurrent requests over 3 models cost 3 preparations when the cache fits; the same 12 requests run sequentially through a cache one entry too small cost 12 preparations and 10 evictions, and the cross-entry rejection is asserted rather than described.

*Corrected in place:* `PreparedModel`'s KDoc claimed "no mutable resolver state", which the shared memo makes false as written; it now states that nothing **request-scoped** is retained and that the model-static memos are lazy and thread-safe.

### IF252 — an `Interpreter` shared across threads now refuses loudly instead of leaking another lane's Document (SHIPPED 2026-08-07, graduated from IAG13) <a id="if252"></a>

`Interpreter.evaluate` guarded re-entrancy with two **unsynchronized** instance fields, and `adaptCondition` read one of them (`activeSnapshot`) to build a custom condition's invocation. Sharing one interpreter across request threads therefore had three failure modes, none of them loud: a consumer callback could receive **another thread's Document** — for per-tenant conditions, cross-tenant exposure — a peer's `finally` could clear the guard underneath an in-flight call, leaving it to fail as "custom condition invoked outside an evaluation transaction", and the check-then-set guard did not serialize, so the misuse was not even reliably rejected.

This surfaced while documenting the P-SERVICE profile ([IF251](#if251)), which is exactly the consumer that would hit it: a `PreparedModel` is safe to share, an `Interpreter` is not, and only the first half was enforced.

**The fix is a detector, not a mutex, and the distinction is the point.** The guard now records the *execution lane* that entered (`currentExecutionLane()`, an `expect`/`actual`: the thread id on JVM, a constant on JS where one isolate is one lane) in a `@Volatile` field, and refuses a second lane with `InterpreterIntegrationReason.CONCURRENT_USE` while a same-lane re-entry keeps reporting `REENTRANT_EVALUATION`. The interpreter does **not** become thread-safe; it becomes honest about not being thread-safe. Removing the field stash entirely — threading the snapshot through the call instead — is the better end state and remains open work.

*Locked:* `ConcurrentInterpreterMisuseTest` runs 8 threads × 60 evaluations through ONE interpreter and requires that every attempt either completes or is refused with `CONCURRENT_USE`, never lost and never some other error; its control runs the supported shape — an interpreter per thread over one shared `PreparedModel` — and requires that it is never refused, so the detector cannot pass by refusing everything.

### IF253 — a resident model costs 3.8–9.2× less than generated Java and 22–41× less than dynamic Groovy, and the difference is entirely the generated rule code (measured 2026-08-07) <a id="if253"></a>

Sizing guidance for the P-SERVICE profile ([IF251](#if251)) previously covered the interpreter alone, which cannot tell a service replacing kernel runtime services what it is trading. Measuring all three strategies on the same axis produced four facts worth keeping, two of them methodological.

**The generated rule/computation code is the whole difference; the Document Model is not.** The kernel's own deserialized+expanded `IDocumentModel` retains 19–186 kB across the five fixtures — comparable to a `PreparedModel`, and *smaller* than one on the rule-heavy shapes. Everything above that line is generated code and its loading machinery: totalled, a resident entry is **3.8×–9.2×** smaller than the generated-Java equivalent and **22.0×–41.0×** smaller than the dynamic-Groovy one. The naive expectation — that a kernel-free reimplementation trades memory for its independence — is inverted, and the reason is not a leaner model representation.

**A fifth to a half of the kernel's cost is invisible to a heap measurement, which makes the naive comparison wrong by construction.** Generated classes live in Metaspace and the disjoint Compressed Class Space, so `Runtime.totalMemory() - freeMemory()` — the settling method the interpreter-only section uses — cannot see 85 kB–4.9 MB of per-model cost, which is **19%–50%** of these totals. Any cross-strategy footprint claim must account class metadata separately. The interpreter arms load **zero** classes per model, which is what makes their metaspace column usable as the measurement's own noise floor (0–28 kB at twelve instances) and what makes heap the interpreter's true total.

**The JIT code cache does NOT belong in that account, and measuring it is what established this.** Attributing code-cache growth per instance yielded negative per-instance deltas and charged the interpreter — which generates no code whatsoever — 67 kB per model, because the pool tracks how hot code became during the run rather than how many models are resident; it is additionally globally bounded and JIT-evicted. So "full footprint per resident model" is heap plus class metadata, and the harness's KDoc records the refusal so it is not re-added. This is the narrower lesson behind the broader one: a memory pool belongs in a per-entry budget only if per-entry attribution is *measurably* stable, and the check is cheap.

**Dynamic Groovy defers nearly all of it to the first evaluation; static Java is eager.** A Groovy engine with its services created but nothing evaluated lands within 0.4% of the expanded model alone on all five fixtures, so `DocumentRtServiceFactory.createDocumentRtService` costs nothing and the first `validateFull` triggers the generation its `IModelCodeCache` then holds. `JavaKernelEngine` generates and compiles in its constructor. Both therefore need a *served* figure budgeted, the same conclusion the interpreter's lazy plans reach.

**Groovy's class count scales per rule; Java's does not** — ≈35 classes at zero rules rising to 639 at 192 (≈3.3/rule) against 12–16 for Java at every size, while generated *source* volume is near-identical between them (739,951 vs 736,274 bytes at 192 rules). So the divergence is in what compiling the source produces, not in how much is generated. Groovy's lowering of condition lines into per-closure classes past `NUM_CONDITIONLINES_PER_METHOD_GROOVY` is consistent with the slope and with the codegen behavior `RuntimeLaws.generatedGroovySource` already documents, but the slope is the measurement and the closure account stays its explanation, not a second observation.

**A resident model's cost has a three-term formula, and the missing third term invalidated the published rule of thumb.** Least squares over six models (5–79 fields, 3–29 groups, 0–192 rules) gives `1.41 kB × fields + 1.55 kB × rules + 2.30 kB × groups`, worst residual **3.9%**, and it predicts the *previous*, independently measured 40-instance run to within 2.4% out of sample. The `groups` term is load-bearing: dropping it moves the worst residual to 12.0%. The two-term "1.6 kB per field + 1.6 kB per rule" rule this supersedes was fitted on five fixtures that all carry 3–4 groups, so its sweep could not see a group axis at all, and it under-predicts a realistically structured model (29 groups) by ~18% — the general lesson being that a dose-response sweep can only discover the axes its fixtures vary. Weighting rules by mean boolean arity fits comparably (0.78 kB per boolean operand), so the per-rule term is really per-AST-node. On the kernel side dynamic Groovy fits `0.75 MB + 14.3 kB × fields + 58.3 kB × rules` within 7% and its generated class count fits `34 + 3.17 × rules` within 1.3%, while **generated Java resists these predictors because rule count is the wrong axis for it**: the 89-rule real-shape model costs *more* than the 192-rule fixture (1,283,876 vs 1,194,455 B), since 89 structurally varied rules over 79 fields and 29 groups compile to more program than 192 near-identical arity-2 rules over 5 fields, so the fitted slope was reading a proxy. Its measured range is quoted instead of a slope.

**The unattributed Java heap was the kernel's own map-heavy metadata, not the generated program — settled by a differential class histogram.** `jcmd GC.class_histogram` taken with no engines held and again with 12 held, per-class delta over 12, on `many-rules-192`: **44% of the generated-Java engine's heap is `HashMap`/`LinkedHashMap` infrastructure** (575,823 B of 1,298,802 B) belonging to the expanded model plus the runtime service's derived metadata — the kernel's analogue of the interpreter's plans — with the kernel's `Rt*` identifier/template machinery at 102,528 B, per-rule objects (`Regel_t`, `Rule`, `MetaRegel`) at 176 B/rule, and the compiled bytecode inside the 360,746 B `[B` row. Dynamic Groovy carries all of that **plus ~2 MB of metaclass, call-site and reflection machinery** (including **6,029 `java.lang.reflect.Method` instances**, 530,552 B) that a static strategy never needs; its `[B` row independently confirms the retained `generatedPlainCode` by measurement, 736,274 of 1,028,103 bytes. The histogram is authoritative for **composition only**: its total runs ~4% above the settled-heap figure for the same arm (and ran 46% above it before the leak fix below), and the Java arm has ~8% run-to-run variance on the largest model against the interpreter's ~0.1%.

*Found by that histogram:* `RuntimeLaws.compileInMemory` never closed its `StandardJavaFileManager`, leaving a zip filesystem open per classpath jar (~56 kB of zipfs index nodes per compiled model). Now closed once the compile task finishes, since the returned loader reads only the in-memory byte map. Closing it removed 396 kB per engine from the histogram but did **not** move the settled-heap Java figures by that amount — an unresolved discrepancy between the two measurement methods, recorded rather than explained away; the published totals remain the footprint harness's.

**Two retention facts decide which arm may be adjusted, and getting the asymmetry wrong overstated the result once.** (a) The Java arm's measured heap included every generated class's **bytecode**, because `RuntimeLaws.compileInMemory` defines classes lazily from a `Map<String, ByteArrayOutputStream>` captured by the returned ClassLoader — 56–271 kB per model that a deployment loading those classes from a jar never pays. That is the harness, so it is subtracted (`RuntimeLaws.compiledJavaBytecodeBytes` reports it deterministically from compilation rather than GC sampling). *Corrected 2026-08-07: the first published version of this finding carried the unadjusted figures and claimed 4.7–10.3× rather than 3.8–9.2×.* (b) The Groovy arm gets **no** such adjustment, because there the retention is the kernel's own: `ModelGroovyCode` holds `private final String generatedPlainCode`, non-transient, beside the `transient GroovyObject` parsed from it, so the generated Groovy text really is resident per model (~740 kB of `many-rules-192`'s 7.1 MB heap). The same class creates one ClassLoader per model, commented *"to enable the deletion of corresponding class definitions from metaspace"* — independent confirmation from the kernel's own source that metaspace is the axis the generated code occupies. The general lesson: before subtracting anything from a cross-implementation measurement, establish whose design the retention belongs to; "it looks like measurement overhead" is not the test.

*Measured by:* `EngineFootprintReportTest` (`:adapter`, opt-in `-Da12.engine.footprint=true` under `-PrunPerf`; prints, asserts nothing beyond its own validity gate). It lives in `:adapter` because `:interpreter` is kernel-free in test scope. **Its sixth model is derived from a real one** rather than hand-authored, because the five committed fixtures top out at 63 kB of DM-JSON with 3–4 groups while real models reach 281 kB across 79 fields, 29 groups and 89 rules: `dmtool profile extract` reduces a real model to the identifier-free artifact [MODEL-PROFILE-SPEC](MODEL-PROFILE-SPEC.md) defines (verified empirically — the committed profile contains no string values at all), and `ModelSynthesizer.synthesize(profile, seed = 42)` regenerates an own-domain model, so the fixture is realistic in shape without committing anyone's model. Fidelity is exact on field/group/rule counts and partial on operator mix and iteration capacity (45 of 140 axes differ), which is acceptable precisely because those axes drive evaluation work rather than retained size — it is a footprint fixture, not a performance clone. A profile carries no localized text, so the derived row under-represents what a text-heavy real model retains for messages. Two properties make the numbers usable rather than suggestive: the interpreter arm **reproduces** the independently written 40-instance `ModelFootprintReportTest` to within 1% on every fixture, and an **equivalent-work gate** runs before any arm is measured — the three engines validate the identical document, their message-code multisets must agree, and the interpreter must report nothing unsupported. That gate paid for itself on first run by catching the harness binding `order-aggregates`' `CustomCondition` on neither engine (the kernel fires an unbound condition; the interpreter reports the rule unsupported), which would otherwise have compared two different computations. Full tables: [INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE.md](INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE.md) "The same axis against both kernel strategies".

### IF254 — half the interpreter's evaluation entry points had no timing test, because coverage was never asked against a derived list (2026-08-07) <a id="if254"></a>

`PerfOp` models two operations, `validate` and `compute`, so every gate and benchmark times one of those. `DmInterpreter` exposes **eight** public methods that evaluate over a `Document`; the other four — `testRule`, `evalCandidate`, `computeField`, `applyComputations` — had no timing test at all, and `validatePart` had none until the day before.

**The gap is the expensive kind: the missing measurement is also the invisible one.** Partial validation was O(N²) for its whole life (`relevanceOf` linear-scanned the relevant set per field instance, and a concrete enumeration grows that set with the document). Twenty sub-quadratic growth guards ran at every landing and none could see it, because no scenario called `validatePart`. It surfaced only when the [IF42](#if42) correctness fix made the path do work it had been skipping — the quadratic was hidden behind a second bug.

**The method was at fault, not the diligence.** Each of the twenty guards targets a mechanism that had actually regressed once; the estate grew a guard per incident. No step in that process enumerates the surface and asks what is unmeasured, so an entry point that never had an incident never acquires a guard however central it is. Counting guards answers "what have we protected"; only a denominator answers "what is unprotected".

The denominator is therefore **derived by reflection** (`InterpreterPerfCoverageTest`): every public `DmInterpreter` method taking a `Document` must have a guard that times it. Untagged, so the question is answered on every build rather than only under `-PrunPerf`. **Amended 2026-08-08 by [IF263](#if263):** the initial implementation kept guard ownership in a side map; coverage is now independently derived from annotations on JUnit-discovered timing tests. The published `Interpreter` facade is a **second** denominator the first reflection cannot see (its methods take `DocumentSnapshot`); it is enumerated and dispositioned as delegating rather than timed.

The four new guards reuse the reviewed one-rule B1 and one-computation B7 scenarios, where the kernel's whole-document call is the slice the entry point computes — asserted, so a scenario growing a second construct fails rather than silently charging the kernel for extra work. Measured advantages over dynamic Groovy, as the **envelope across five runs** rather than one: `testRule` 7.8–19.0×, `evalCandidate` 6.7–20.3×, `computeField` 2.8–5.9×, `applyComputations` 2.9–6.4×; growth 1.9–4.3× for 4× rows. The spread is wide because each call is single-digit milliseconds, which is exactly why the guard asserts a floor and a growth shape rather than a number — quoting one run's figures as *the* result would misrepresent a measurement this noisy, and an independent reviewer duly failed to reproduce the first version of this line. No defect found; the deliverable is the guard.

*Locked by:* `InterpreterPerfCoverageTest` (denominator, ordinary lane), `EntryPointPerfTest` (`@Tag("perf")`), `PartialSumOfProductsPerfTest` (`validatePart`). The trip line is `PerfBounds.MIN_KERNEL_ADVANTAGE` via `KernelAdvantage` — a conservative floor against dynamic Groovy, not a target, superseding nothing; its polarity inverts the neighbouring `RATIO_CEILING`.

### IF255 — the partial-relevance rewrite carried three defects, all of them a bound or a shape ASSERTED instead of measured (2026-08-08) <a id="if255"></a>

The [IF42](#if42) completeness fix and the relevance index that followed were both green on their own differentials and both wrong. A cold review of that work, plus the differential written to lock its findings, produced four divergences with one shared mechanism — a quantity reasoned about rather than measured, then written into a comment that afterwards read as evidence.

**Exponential in depth, and silently wrong past 31.** Answering "is this cell relevant" by trying every wildcard/concrete combination of the path's levels is `2^depth` probes; the code said so and justified it as *"depth is the model's nesting depth (single digits)"*. `ModelPreparation` admits **64** by default, 128 hard — the bound was a guess about fixtures. Measured: **4.7 s per partial validation** of a one-field depth-24 document, and at depth 31 `1 shl depth` overflows `Int` negative, so the probe loop never runs, every query answers "not relevant", and messages disappear. Fixed by probing **one key per distinct wildcard pattern the caller supplied** — equivalent, since an entity with pattern `p` matches iff its indices equal the query's repetitions with `ALL` at `p`'s wildcard positions — which bounds cost by the relevant set instead of the depth.

**Completeness read per LEVEL, when it is a property of the TUPLES.** Discriminator: relevance at `Outer[1]/Inner[1]` + `Outer[2]/Inner[2]` covers `{1,2}` at both levels while covering half the rows. One repeatable level cannot separate the two readings, which is why the originating probe missed it. Now requires the full cross-product of the declared repetitions.

**Completeness is per ITERATION ROW.** Not in the review — the differential written to pin the diagonal found it. With the rule iterating `Outer`, a relevant set completing `Outer[1]` but not `Outer[2]` makes the kernel **fire once**; a predicate keyed on the star path alone has no row to decide against and stayed silent for both. The gate now takes the row's bound repetition prefix.

**Two method notes, both of which changed an outcome here.** *The oracle for an optimization is the thing it replaced* — the first depth test failed at depth 3, far below any boundary; restoring the linear scan under the same test failed identically, proving the test's relevant set inadequate rather than the index broken. Fixing the index to satisfy it would have been a wrong fix chasing a wrong test. *A defect's cost can hide the defect* — the natural probe depth for the `Int` boundary is 31, where the shallowest match sits at depth 30 and the old code spends ~10⁹ probes, so the correctness test hung for ten minutes instead of failing; depths **32+** are fast *and* wrong because the shift wraps, and that is what the guard uses.

**A fourth defect was in the guard, not the code.** `PartialSumOfProductsPerfTest` timed the interpreter against `RuntimeLaws.messagesPart`, which at that revision expanded the model, built both service factories, and converted V1→V2 **inside every call**, while the interpreter reused its prepared model. It reported **19.57×** at 1000 rows; built once on both sides the honest figures are **5.40×** at 250 rows and **7.17×** at 1000, growth 3.92× for 4× rows. The conclusion held and only the magnitude was wrong — and note the direction on the *before* number: an inflated kernel baseline made the pre-fix 0.98× an **over**statement of where the interpreter stood. The helper now reuses a bounded exact-model engine, but still converts the document per call; symmetric timing continues to use the explicit build-once engine and prepared instance. No published surface was affected (they cite the inventory sweep, which builds its kernel once); method rule and basis in [INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE.md](INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE.md) "Checkpoint 2026-08-08".

*Locked by:* `PartialRelevanceDepthTest` (`:interpreter`, kernel-free — the index against the scan it replaced, plus a cost bound a depth-enumerating query cannot pass) and `laws/PartialNestedAggregateDiffTest` (`:adapter` — wildcard / full cross-product / diagonal / anti-diagonal / one-row-complete / mixed-shape, every reading taken from the kernel).

**Corrected 2026-08-08:** the case listed here as "mixed-shape" was not one — `[*,*,1]` and `[*,*,2]` share a wildcard pattern and differ only in a concrete coordinate, so the cross-product check saw them together and the case never exercised coverage spread across shapes. The genuinely mixed reading is [IF260](#if260), where the per-pattern decision turned out to suppress a complete union. The case is kept, relabelled for what it does pin.

### IF256 — the published façade allocated quadratically by defensively copying a list the engine had shared, and a TIMING guard could not see it (2026-08-08) <a id="if256"></a>

[IF191](#if191) makes the engine hand every finding of a dense `RepetitionNotUnique` cluster **one shared pointer-list instance** rather than a private copy each. The public `Interpreter` façade then projected each engine message into a `ValidationFinding` and ran `immutableListSnapshot` on that list **per finding** — copying one N-element list N times, and cancelling the optimization at exactly the boundary a library consumer calls. Fixed by reusing the snapshot when the source list is the same instance (`SharedPointerSnapshots`, one slot per channel; a miss simply falls back to the copy, so it is a fast path and never a correctness dependency).

**Corrected 2026-08-08:** one slot per channel held for a single duplicate cluster and missed on *every* message of two interleaved ones, restoring the full quadratic — see [IF261](#if261). The copy is now removed rather than cached: the engine publishes an immutable snapshot and the boundary recognizes it. The measurement in this finding stands; the remedy it describes was replaced.

**The instrument matters more than the finding.** The cost here is **allocation, not arithmetic**, and the two are not interchangeable:

| scale | timing guard (4× rows) | what actually happened |
|---|---|---|
| 500 → 2,000 | 2.68× — **passes** | defect fully present |
| 1,000 → 4,000 | 3.80× against a 5.4× trip line — **passes** | defect fully present |
| 8,000 | never reports | `OutOfMemoryError: Java heap space` |

`arraycopy` is fast, so N² copies barely move a clock; they move the heap. At 8,000 duplicate rows the façade produced ~64 million pointer references and killed the test JVM on a document the engine handles comfortably. **A timing proxy for an allocation defect reports green while the process is one scale away from dying** — and a growth guard tuned to a scale that fits in memory is guaranteed to sit in the range where the defect is invisible.

So the guard asserts the invariant **directly**: findings the engine gave the same list must come out sharing one list (`assertSame`). Deterministic, scale-free, 40 rows, no clock — and it runs in the ordinary lane rather than behind `-PrunPerf`, because a consumer-visible memory blow-up should not wait for someone to opt into the perf tier. Verified non-vacuous: removing the sharing fails it, where the timing version passed.

**How it shipped: a disposition reasoned from one line of a function.** The façade's four snapshot-taking methods were dispositioned in [IF254](#if254) as "delegates to a timed core call, adds an O(1)-per-finding projection, so the worst case is a constant factor". That was reached by reading `ModelAddressValidation.entityKind` — two set lookups, correct in itself — while missing the pointer-list copy on the adjacent line. Reading part of a function is not a measurement of the function, and the reasoning was recorded confidently enough that only an external reviewer's execution overturned it.

*Locked by:* `FacadeProjectionSharingTest` (`:adapter`, ordinary lane). Found by cold review (C-F5); the reviewer's 8.56×-growth reading did not reproduce at that ratio here, but the underlying defect did, and its real signature is the heap rather than the clock.

### IF257 — the wildcard-only gate was row-blind too, the sibling site of IF255, and it UNDER-fired (2026-08-08) <a id="if257"></a>

[IF255](#if255) fixed `SumOfProducts`' completeness gate, which decided globally what the kernel decides per iteration row. `starRelevant` — the wildcard-only gate `Sum`/`MaxValue`/`MinValue`/`NumberOfDifferentValues` share ([IF42](#if42)) — was keyed on the star path in exactly the same way, so the root-cause rule required sweeping it rather than assuming it was fine. It diverged.

**Discriminator:** two nested repeatable levels, with the inner level wildcarded for ONE outer row and enumerated concretely for the other — `Outer[1]/Inner[*]` + `Outer[2]/Inner[1]` + `Outer[2]/Inner[2]`. Kernel fires **1×** (the wildcarded row). Interpreter fired **0×**.

**Note the direction, because it is the opposite of the sibling and of the prediction.** The old predicate required *every* repeatable level of some entity to be `ALL`, so `[ALL, 1, ALL]` — outer row 1, all its inner rows — failed the test and no entity qualified: silent everywhere. The expectation before running it was over-firing, on the reasoning that a path-keyed gate would answer "some entity wildcards this star" and admit both rows. Reasoning about a defect's direction is worth exactly nothing; the differential took a minute and settled it.

Cell relevance cannot mask this one: every cell of `Outer[2]` **is** relevant in the discriminating case, and only its wildcard-ness differs — which is why the case had to nest two repeatable levels to exist at all.

**The fix removes the duplication rather than repeating it.** Both gates now share the row scoping — filter entities to the current row's bound prefix, then look at the repeatable levels left concrete below it; the wildcard-only gate requires none, `SumOfProducts` requires them fully enumerated. The row scoping was the part that was wrong twice, so it exists once.

**Corrected 2026-08-08:** the shared helper derived the bound prefix from the star path's *deepest* repeatable level, which is wrong for a multi-star operand — see [IF259](#if259), where consolidating the computation made one wrong derivation authoritative for both gates. The consolidation stands; the derivation inside it did not.

*Locked by:* `laws/PartialNestedWildcardScopeDiffTest` (`:adapter` — both-wildcarded, both-concrete, and the mixed discriminator, each read from the kernel and compared as a firing COUNT so one-row-versus-two is visible rather than collapsed to a set).

### IF258 — four guards asserted their own source text or a name's existence rather than the behaviour they advertised (2026-08-08) <a id="if258"></a>

A cold review of the release and perf work found four guards that would pass while the invariant they name was violated. None had ever failed; three had never been able to.

| guard | what it asserted | how it passed with the defect present |
|---|---|---|
| capture-stamp portability | no `System.getenv` **inside** the digest closure | bind the environment to a file-scope name and hash the name |
| native provenance input | the build file *contains* `inputs.file(generateVersionProperties` | a commented-out line carrying the same text |
| perf coverage map | the named test **method exists** | point an entry point at any unrelated method, including one inherited from `Object` |
| entry-point compute preflight | the result is **non-empty** | a wrong-but-nonempty result is timed happily |

The first two are now comment-stripped and identifier-aware: the stamp guard additionally collects every file-scope name bound from `System.getenv` and refuses to find any of them in the digest, which closes the hoisting form (mutant-verified — it fires naming the hoisted variable). The compute preflights compare **instance counts against the kernel** instead of asserting non-emptiness.

**The coverage-map guard was only partly closable, and that was stated rather than papered over.** It required the named method to carry `@Test`, which ruled out a pointer to a helper or an `Object` method but left the ownership claim in a separate map. **Amended 2026-08-08 by [IF263](#if263):** the map is gone; typed coverage declarations now live on JUnit-discovered, perf-tagged timing tests.

**The pattern worth keeping.** All four are *source-text* or *name-existence* proxies for a *behavioural* claim, and a proxy is only as good as the distance between it and the thing. The distance is invisible while the invariant holds, which is why none of these ever failed — and why the check that matters is not "does the guard pass" but "can I make the defect and watch it pass". The stamp guard's hoisting form was mutated and fires naming the hoisted variable.

**Strengthening a guard is a code change and can itself be wrong.** The first version of the `applyComputations` count compared the applied and source cell lists **positionally**; this scenario's seed leaves the computed field empty, so the source list is empty, the zip produced no pairs, and the guard failed claiming 0 written cells where 150 were written. Keyed by coordinates it is right, and an absent source cell correctly counts as written. A preflight that fails on a correct implementation is not a stricter guard, it is a broken one — worth stating because the whole finding is about guards that do not check what they claim.

*Locked by:* `BuildPerformancePolicyTest` (the two build guards, root `:test`), `InterpreterPerfCoverageTest` and `EntryPointPerfTest` (`:adapter`).

**Extended 2026-08-08, after a second review found four more of the same shape** — three of them in the strengthening above, which is the finding's own lesson arriving on its own fix:

| guard | what still passed | now |
|---|---|---|
| capture-stamp portability | the hoist sweep knew one spelling (`def x = … System.getenv`); a method wrapper, an `ext` property, and Gradle's own `providers.environmentVariable` all passed | six ambient-read forms × four binding forms, with a positive control asserting the detector finds each |
| native provenance input | the declaration could sit in a dead branch and the test named a *rebuild* it never observes | renamed to what it checks (`nativeCompileDeclaresTheGeneratedProvenanceAsAnInput`); the behavioural claim rests on the 2026-08-07 measurement, and the name no longer overstates it |
| perf coverage denominator | both denominators key on method NAME, so a second `validatePart(Document, …)` overload would leave the discovered set unchanged and go untimed | a duplicate name fails loudly and demands its own disposition |
| native resource globs | any own-prefix glob containing `*` was skipped, so `schema/no-such-*.json` was accepted — the exact dead weight, spelled with a star | wildcards resolve against the shipped tree, with a positive control on a matching and an empty glob |

**The bound is stated rather than papered over.** Text scanning cannot cover a dynamically composed `System."${'get' + 'env'}"`; the guard catches every form anyone would write on purpose and the stamp gate fails closed on CI behind it. **Amended 2026-08-08:** the coverage-map residual graduated from IG111 to [IF263](#if263); the claim now lives on the timing guard and the side pointer no longer exists.

### IF259 — the row-scoping helper bound a MULTI-star operand at the wrong level, and the shared helper spread it to both gates (2026-08-08) <a id="if259"></a>

[IF257](#if257) fixed both starred-aggregate gates by giving them one `rowScope` helper, which derived the current row's bound coordinates from the star path's **deepest repeatable level**. That derivation is right for a single star and wrong for a multi-star operand, where [IF94](#if94) already pins the kernel's rule: binding stops at the levels strictly above the **first** `*`-marked group, so `Outer* then Inner* then Qty` still spans every outer row from any one of them.

**Discriminator:** `Sum(Outer* then Inner* then Qty) > 25` on `Outer[2]/Inner[2]`, all `Qty = 10`, with the relevant set pinning `Outer` to one row and wildcarding `Inner` under it. Kernel **0×**, interpreter **1×** — it fires on an operand reaching rows the caller never made relevant. Pinning both outer rows separately reads the same way: kernel **0×**, interpreter **2×**.

The threshold is the positive control and is why the reading means anything: 25 sits between one outer row's subtotal (20) and the cross-row total (40), so full validation firing **2×** in both engines proves the operand really does span both outer rows. A silent kernel in a partial case is otherwise indistinguishable from an unsupported shape, and every "agreement" underneath would be two kinds of nothing.

**`bindDepth` was already on the AST and already correct** ([IF93](#if93)/IF94, threaded through the whole `EvalContext` star family). Only the relevance gates re-derived it from the path — one of them via a helper introduced *to stop the row scoping being derived twice*. Consolidating a computation is not the same as getting it right: it made one wrong derivation authoritative for both gates, and the sibling site in the value-list quantifier dropped the same parameter independently.

**The fix** threads `bindDepth` into both gates and splits the star path into what the row binds and what the star iterates (`starScope`). A hand-built node (−1) keeps the leaf-parent default, where the two coincide by definition.

*Locked by:* `laws/PartialMultiStarScopeDiffTest` (`:adapter` — the full-validation positive control plus four partial readings, each compared as a firing count).

### IF260 — completeness decided PER WILDCARD PATTERN suppressed a relevant set whose union is complete (2026-08-08) <a id="if260"></a>

[IF255](#if255)'s cross-product completeness test grouped the relevant set by (path, wildcard pattern) so that tuples were comparable, then asked whether **any one group** covered everything. Coverage spread across genuinely different patterns therefore read as incomplete. That direction was recorded in the source as deliberate-but-unmeasured; it is measured now, and it is wrong.

**Discriminator:** `Inner[1]` relevant for every outer row (`[*,*,1]`) plus `Inner[2]` pinned per outer row (`[*,1,2]`, `[*,2,2]`). The union is all four pairs; neither pattern is complete alone. Kernel fires **2×**, interpreter fired **0×**. The same reading with the shape difference at the root coordinate rather than the outer one (`[*,*,1]` against `[1,*,2]`) behaves identically, so it is not an artefact of which level carries the difference.
