Over 322 observations in three sets (108 local in-sample, 108 local out-of-sample against a block they did not author, 106 hosted) the worst is `1.352`, so `1.65` keeps `1.22×` of margin and **nothing breaches**. *(Corrected the same day after a cold review: this first concluded the tightening was refused, by comparing the ratio itself — `1.421` worst — against candidate factors, and cited a lane that `1.45` "would have red-lined" when its ceiling at `1.45` in fact clears its worst observation by 11 %.)* What does not survive contact with the same data is a **per-class** tolerance: the local class is the noisier of the two on both quantities, so one number serves both. A median-of-N anchor narrows where the bar sits without bounding the next run at all, exactly as the third bullet below warned.

**Three things about an absolute bar that are true regardless of the machine, all three learned while trying to arm this one on a hosted runner** (the dated hosted measurements themselves are INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE's "Checkpoint 2026-08-11"):

- **Normalizing an observation by an in-run reference is disqualified, not merely weak.** It divides out a genuine uniform slowdown along with the noise, which is the precise blindness this bar exists to remove — the same reason a growth ratio cannot see one. So a noisy environment cannot be rescued by normalization; only by a wider tolerance, more sampling, or leaving the class unanchored.
- **A finer clock is not a substitute for isolation.** These are already CPU-clock medians, and cross-tenant cache and memory-bandwidth interference is spent in **user-mode** cycles; `getCurrentThreadUserTime` would drop only the kernel half, so it cannot remove a common-mode level. The measurable signature of such a level is that dividing it out changes the one-sided exceedance while leaving the multiplicative spread untouched — spread is invariant under any single multiplicative normalizer, so only the exceedance carries information.
- **A median-of-N anchor narrows a noisy class but bounds nothing about the next run.** It sets the anchor at the middle of the observed levels; it says nothing about a level that was not sampled, so it is a complement to a measured tolerance and never a replacement for one.

Separately, the lane stopped being `failFast`: the 2026-08-11 nightly reported 21 of 26 rows because one breach aborted the run, and a lane whose purpose is measurement must not discard measurements (`GateTierWiringTest` locks it).

*Locked by:* `PerfAnchorBudgetTest` on scripted observations — the uniform-slowdown case that no growth ratio can see, the tolerance boundary, the absolute-slack boundary on a tiny lane, one-low-outlier-cannot-clear-a-real-slowdown, bootstrap versus missing-key-in-an-anchored-class in both required modes, and that a changed fingerprint or a different lane drops the anchor rather than inheriting one. `PerfAnchorScriptTest` **executes** `scripts/perf-anchor.sh` against a temporary anchor (`ANCHOR_FILE`) for each of its refusals — an incomplete input for a new runner class, the `ALLOW_REMOVALS` escape, inputs from two runner classes, `show` writing nothing — and for the median-of-several-runs, don't-touch-the-other-block, and machine-identity properties, which were claims in prose until then — including the probe's failure sentinel, whose refusal the test pins through `MachineIdentity.UNKNOWN_MODEL` itself, so the cross-language pairing cannot drift. `PerfHistoryScriptTest` covers the renderer's four: the header names the machine, two named machines are disclosed once, one machine is not reported as a mix, and an unrecorded machine is not evidence of a different one. End-to-end: with the anchor authored from one full run, two further independent full runs held all 36 observations inside the tolerance (no false red), and mutating one committed key made the assertion fire. `GateTierWiringTest` covers the `failFast` half and that the required flag is not CI-gated.

### IF281: only the cross-engine comparison survives shared hardware, so it is the gate and everything self-referential is a flag (measured; two dated checkpoints) <a id="if281"></a>

**The interpreter and both kernel strategies execute in the same JVM, on the same vCPUs, in the same run, so a comparison between them is dimensionless.** Machine speed, VM generation, JDK patch level, co-tenant interference and thermal state divide out because both engines experience them. A bar that judges this run's numbers against a constant or against a number recorded earlier has no such protection: it is a property of the machine as much as of the code. That asymmetry, not a statistical argument, is why the two kinds of bar get different treatment.

The numbers are dated evidence and live in INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE's results log, not here: the hosted margins and the dispatch method error in "Checkpoint 2026-08-11", the 34-row envelope after the bar was extended to every gated scenario in "Checkpoint 2026-08-12". Three of their conclusions are what this finding rests on.

**(1) The margin is comfortable almost everywhere and thin in one place.** The interpreter wins all 34 rows; **32** of them by `2.5×` or more. The exceptions are both scales of the compute-overlay `$`-correlation path against generated Java, at `1.99×` and `2.49×` (re-measured 2026-08-12 over three isolated runs; the row's own ratio spread is `1.036×`). *(Corrected 2026-08-12 twice, in place. The margin was first held against `1.78×`, a figure measured on different scenarios on a different machine class — borrowing a noise figure across scenarios is the same error as borrowing one across quantities. Measuring that row's own noise then made it worse, not better: `1.90×` spread and a worst observation leaving only `1.35×`, so the noise exceeded the margin. Both readings were taken on a **cold first pair** and neither number survives [IF282](#if282): warm, the row's spread is `1.07×` and its margin `1.97×`, and the worst observation across all 34 rows and three runs leaves `1.95×`.)* **A crude bar is therefore not sufficient by itself** — which is the correction to this finding's first version, written when the bar existed on two scenarios and the observed envelope was `5×–17×`. A margin measured on the two scenarios that had a bar is not the estate's margin, and only extending the bar could show that. What makes the bar safe is not the margin but the **re-measurement**: a comparative breach is re-run and accepted on the median of the attempts, exactly as the growth floor has been since 2026-08-08.

**(2) Every false red the nightly produced came from a self-referential bar** with no kernel in it, sitting a few percent from its trip line while the comparative bars sat several hundred percent from theirs. For `CharsetPerfTest` the contention explanation has a discriminator — the same class passed immediately on re-run in isolation. For the two hosted growth reds it does not: the evidence is one commit passing one night and failing the next two, with dispatch pattern confounded with time, so *that* attribution remains a hypothesis and is recorded as one.

**(3) The bar existed on two scenarios out of seventeen.** `requireInterpreterBeatsBoth` and `PairedAcceptance` are reachable from exactly one production path, `PerfHarness.pairedScenarioGrowth`, and until 2026-08-12 only the two `Number-index` lanes called it — and those are not growth-gated, so **all 17** growth-gated scenarios were uncovered, not fifteen. Their only relative bar was `RATIO_CEILING`, `interp/groovy < 5.0`, which tolerates the interpreter being **five times slower**, with nothing at all against generated Java. Any of them could have lost to the kernel with a green build. `KernelParityGateTest` now runs the bar on all 17 at roughly a tenth of the growth lanes' scale, because a per-unit cost needs two scales rather than large ones; `KernelParityCoverageGuardTest` holds the set difference, since the cheap way to add a lane is to copy a growth lane and a growth lane carries no kernel.

**Per-unit cost is the cross-engine scaling quantity, not the growth factor.** Over a 4× step a linear engine's measured growth factor is `4 − 3f` in the fixed cost's share `f`, so comparing growth factors ranks engines by fixed-cost share and the leanest engine loses deterministically ([IF279](#if279)). The gate is `(t_large − t_small)/(n_large − n_small)`, per row × scale × strategy, decided from medians measured in one run — **no aggregation across rows**, because the invariant is universally quantified and one row losing is a loss.

**What follows for enforcement** is `TimingEnforcement` (INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE, "Which bars may FAIL depends on where they run"): the cross-engine family gates everywhere; the growth floors and the absolute anchor gate on a dedicated machine and **report** on a hosted one. The self-referential bars were **not** retired, which an intermediate design proposed: retiring them would strictly loosen coverage, because the parity gate is only a `10.4×–13.1×` equivalent growth bar. They keep their full strength where the measurement supports them.

**The COLD lane is the one place where a flag is a real loss.** Its unique coverage is the super-linear index build that the warm harness structurally cannot see (optimization #7), and it is the estate's only wall-clock lane — run 2 of the three hosted runs recorded 34 of 36 observations precisely because both its scales dropped out. If that needs a *gate* on shared hardware rather than a flag, the honest instrument is a **deterministic work counter** over the index build on the existing seams (`PreparationObserver`/`PreparationWork`, the eval-time `CacheObserver`), which is a pure function of the input and cannot false-positive. Timing one cold call and dividing it by another cannot be made reliable.

**Recorded so they are not re-proposed**, each with what falsified it:

| idea | status | basis |
|---|---|---|
| an absolute growth floor is a usable gate on a shared runner | **retired there.** A few percent of margin against 2× noise; both nightly reds came from here | Checkpoint 2026-08-11 |
| gate the cross-engine invariant on an aggregate | **wrong in kind.** A universally quantified invariant cannot be aggregated | review finding |
| a growth **factor** compares engines fairly | **false.** `4 − 3f` ranks by fixed-cost share; the leanest engine loses | [IF279](#if279) |
| the CPU clock removes contention | **false as stated.** Cache and bandwidth interference inflate cycles, which thread CPU time counts | IF280, the hosted runs |
| GC/JIT activity is a valid sample-rejection predicate | **no.** Post-treatment: an allocation regression causes GC, so rejecting GC windows censors the evidence | review finding |
| `δ=0` for a self-comparison | **retracted by its author.** At a true ratio of `1.00` the interval always straddles the boundary, so the verdict is permanently inconclusive | measured half-widths: 1.9 % at `σ=0.02, n=24` |
| "add forks while the interval crosses the boundary" | **retracted by its author.** Optional stopping without alpha spending (Lan-DeMets O'Brien-Fleming) | — |
| a cross-engine ratio is inherently steadier than an absolute | **contingent, currently false** at `warmup=5, iters=5` (`1.15×`/`2.16×` against `1.24×`/`2.08×`); irrelevant at these margins | measured |
| `min`-of-N is the least-polluted estimator | **contingent, currently false** at `N=5` (`1.55×`); legitimate at large fixed `N` | measured |
| retry-and-take-the-median is no defense | **overstated.** `3q²−2q³` under independence, a 556× reduction at `q=6e-4`; the real objection is same-VM correlation | verified arithmetic |
| a fresh-runner confirmation squares the false-alarm rate | **not automatic.** Runners share image, provider epoch, CPU class and temporal load | review finding |
| normalizing by another interpreter scenario | **excluded.** It divides out a genuine uniform slowdown. A reference that does not move when we regress — the kernel, a previous release, a validated canary — is not covered by this exclusion | IF280 |
| a fixed runner solves the problem | **repeatability, not portability.** It cannot see an architecture-specific regression | review finding |

**The trigger that would make this insufficient.** Should any row's ratio climb past `0.5` — `CO-correlation` already sits there — that row stops being a crude comparison and becomes a near-boundary decision needing a real statistical treatment **for that row only**: a `1 + δ` boundary for any self-comparison with `δ` above the interval half-width plus a pinned epoch carrying the cumulative bound; power specified at an alternative beyond the boundary and reported rather than promised; Holm within the night at a per-night familywise budget of `0.01/30 = 3.33e-4` for a 0.99 clean 30-night period (Holm needs no independence assumption, which matters because the lanes share a whole-run level); sample size quadratic in the within-fork paired `σ` and only logarithmic in `α` (`σ=0.02` ≈ 6 forks per cell, `σ=0.04` ≈ 24, `σ=0.06` ≈ 53); and the replication unit is the **fork**, not the inner iteration, with engine order counterbalanced across forks. None of it is built, deliberately.

*Locked by:* `GrowthFloorRetryTest` pins both enforcement rules on scripted inputs and all three outcomes of the growth floor; `PairedAcceptanceTest` pins the per-unit-cost comparison, including that a quadratic interpreter still has the lowest per-unit cost — which is why the floor stays a separate check; `KernelParityCoverageGuardTest` pins the scenario coverage. Dated evidence: INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE "Checkpoint 2026-08-11" (hosted margins, the dispatch error) and "Checkpoint 2026-08-12" (the 34-row envelope).

### IF282: a cross-engine ratio needs every engine at steady state, because a growth factor's cancellation does not apply to it (FIXED 2026-08-12) <a id="if282"></a>

**A cold JVM largely cancels out of one engine's N/4N growth factor and does not cancel out of a two-engine ratio at all.** The growth factor divides one engine's own two scales, so an under-warmed run inflates numerator and denominator together; a parity row divides two *different* engines, which warm along *different* curves, so the ratio reports where on each curve the sample landed. The estate had carried the first case's intuition into the second: the parity path took its first timed observation after three warm runs, a count chosen as "enough for the Groovy validator to JIT-warm" and never measured.

Measured, it is not. Tracing every per-pair observation on `CO-correlation` (`-Dperf.trace=true`), **all eighteen** engine-scale series across three sequential isolated runs were slower in pair 0 than in pair 2 — Groovy at N=800 by `1.51×`–`1.70×`, the interpreter at N=200 by `1.88×`–`2.34×`. All eighteen above 1.0 is the discriminator that separates this from contention, which is symmetric and would put about half below. The numbers and the before/after comparison are dated evidence in INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE, "Checkpoint 2026-08-12 — the first pair was measured cold, on every engine".

Three things follow, and the first two are what generalize past this estate:

- **The reduction hides the mechanism, so the instrument has to survive it.** A row's medians cannot distinguish a cold first pair from a contended one — both leave the same spread — and every attempt to reason about that spread from the reduced numbers reached a wrong mechanism. The per-pair trace is kept for that reason, not as debugging residue.
- **Warm adaptively, not to a bigger constant.** The scenarios span `0.2 ms` to `100 ms` per evaluation, so no single warm count is adequate for the cheap ones without being wasteful for the dear ones, and a per-observation warmup is paid again by every pair while a pre-warm is paid once per instance. The stop rule is "two consecutive flat probes", capped: one flat probe stopped the warm after ~8 runs and left generated Java still falling `1.19×` across the pairs, so a single flat observation is exactly as unreliable here as a single breach is for the comparative bars, and gets the same confirmation.
- **Warm LARGE first — reasoned, not measured.** JIT compilation is per method and both scales run the same methods, so the large instance should warm them with four times fewer evaluations, leaving the small one to settle in the two probes its own rule needs. This is a **hypothesis about cost, recorded as one**: the rival reading is that two differently-shaped documents pollute each other's call-site profiles, which predicts the opposite ordering. Nothing observable rests on it — the order changes what the warm COSTS, never what is measured — and one run settles it whenever it matters: warm small-first and compare the six per-instance warm-run counts.

The honest ratio can be *worse* than the cold one — `CO-correlation @200` reads `0.509` warm against `0.460` cold, because generated Java was the engine furthest from steady state and the cold figure credited the interpreter with part of the kernel's warm-up. Which direction a row moves depends on which engine was more under-warmed, which is the whole reason a cold ratio means nothing.

**The time cap took two measurements to set, and the second one moved it off a boundary (2026-08-12; closes IG123).** At 1 s exactly one instance ended on the cap rather than by flattening — `CO-correlation`'s dynamic-Groovy arm at N=800, after 9 runs — and the bias is not neutral: a kernel measurement still falling reads higher than its settled cost, so `interp/groovy` reads lower and the tightest row's margin looks better than it is. At 4 s that arm flattened and a *different* instance took its place in one of three runs: `Number index validate`'s Groovy arm at N=2,000, the dearest in the estate at ~266 ms per evaluation, capping after 15 runs while flattening inside the budget in the other two. A quantity that sits exactly on a boundary is answered with a factor, not with another boundary value, so the cap is **8 s**; three consecutive full gate runs then reported no cap stop at all. *(The basis for that last claim is the runs' own logs, which is weaker than it should be: the warm outcome qualifies every ratio in its lane and is **not** in the committed record, so a returning cap stop fails nothing and leaves no trace a later run can compare against. Recorded as part of IG121, whose complaint this is.)* The predicted direction of the bias did **not** materialize for the tightest row — its margin read `1.97×` before and `1.99×` after, inside its own `1.036×` spread — so the mechanism was real and its magnitude, for that row, was immaterial. The parity tier costs *less* than before (71.5 s against 82 s), because the same commit also made the interpreter faster ([IF284](#if284)).

*Locked by:* `SteadyStateWarmUpTest` pins the stop rule on scripted batch medians (flattening needs confirming, a worse batch counts as flat, each cap fires on the shape it guards); `ParityTraceTest` pins that the trace keeps every observation the medians reduce away. Dated evidence: INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE "Checkpoint 2026-08-12 — the first pair was measured cold, on every engine".

### IF283: a second row-context implementation inherits the shape of the first but not its caches (FIXED 2026-08-12) <a id="if283"></a>

**`RowContext` and `ParallelRowContext` answer the same questions about a path — which group owns it, what its repetition indices are, what placement it resolves to — and only the first memoized them.** The memos in `RowContext` carry a comment naming the profile that motivated them and the invariant that permits them: the answers are deterministic in the path plus the context's immutable fields, the same path is asked for many times per row, and the document's structure is fixed for the length of a call. Every word of that applies unchanged to the parallel variant, which was written later for the outer-join iteration and got none of it.

Nothing flagged the omission. The two classes share an interface and no supertype where a cache could live, the parallel one is only reached by scenarios using parallel iteration, and its cost is invisible to a growth floor — re-deriving a pure function per access is a **constant factor**, so the lane stayed comfortably linear while paying it. It took a cross-engine ratio thin enough to be worth profiling, and then a profile of the *right* scenario: the estate's tightest row is a COMPUTE one that never constructs a parallel context, so the earlier profile could not have seen this. The measurements are in INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE, "Checkpoint 2026-08-12 — the parallel row context, profiled and memoized", and the fix is catalog #12.

**What generalizes.** A constant-factor omission is exactly what this estate's instruments are weakest at: a growth floor divides it out, a per-unit-cost comparison tolerates it while the engine still wins, and a differential is indifferent to it. What found it was the cross-engine LEVEL bar, which is the only one of the three that a constant factor moves — and it only found it after being extended past the two scenarios that had carried it ([IF281](#if281)). When a class of context, cache or resolver gains a second implementation, the question to ask is not whether it is correct but whether it kept the first one's *optimizations*, because correctness has an oracle here and that does not.

*Locked by:* the existing parallel-iteration differential estate (10 classes, 30 cases; an 11th, `SiblingGroupFuzzDiffTest`, is `@Tag("fuzz")` and outside the default run) covers the behaviour, which is unchanged by construction — the memos hold a pure function's result. The effect is held by `KernelParityGateTest`'s `PF-framed` and `PI-parallel` rows, which is the bar that exposed it.

### IF284: a memo keyed by the CELL costs more than the value-shaped work it caches (FIXED 2026-08-12; graduated from IG122) <a id="if284"></a>

**Three memos were stacked on one another, each keyed by `(path, reps: List<Int>, raw)`, to protect a check that a fourth one had already cached.** `FormalResultCache` wrapped `AutoChecks.computeFormalResultAt`, which consulted `ValueAdmissionCache`, which wrapped `computeValueAdmissionAt`, whose expensive step — the format/charset/scale check — goes through the value-shaped `formalErrorCached`. Profiled on `complexRealWorld`, 47.1 % of samples were inside `resultAt` and its leaves broke down as `HashMap` get/hash/put/resize **35.2 %**, `ArrayList` hashCode/equals **21.2 %**, the data-class `Key`'s own `hashCode`/`equals` **10.2 %** and `Intrinsics.areEqual` **12.5 %**, against **2.6 %** in the check being cached. A repetition list in a key is walked to hash it and walked again to compare it, while the two String components have their hashes cached by the platform.

**The fix is to key each memo by the shape of what it actually computes, which is not the cell.** A value's admission is a function of the field and the value, so one entry serves every cell holding that value. Three parts of a formal result are *not*, and each got its own treatment rather than a place in the key:

- the **over-repetition gate** depends on the coordinates; it is a walk over the path's declared bounds, so it moved *outside* the memo (`AutoChecks.overRepetitionResultAt`) — which also removed a duplicate: the outer function and the inner one were both performing it;
- **duplicate-index membership** depends on which siblings a cell has, and is a set lookup;
- a **code-based `CustomFieldType`** is a consumer callback whose invocation count is part of the contract (one per cell, IF150/IF152), so those fields keep the cell-shaped key in a second map. **The kernel agrees, measured 2026-08-12** (closes IG125): one value in two in-cap rows reaches the validator **twice** under dynamic-Groovy *and* under generated Java. So the exception is not a self-imposed cost to protect our own lock — a value-shaped key here would diverge from the kernel. Five doc summaries of IF152 had drifted to "once per relevant concrete value", which is what made the contract look self-imposed; IF152's own text always said *cell*, and the summaries are corrected.

That last one is the trap, and it is the reason this needs stating: keying purely by value is *invisible* in every outcome — the messages, codes and pointers are identical — and shows up only as a stateful validator being consulted once where the contract says twice. `CustomFieldTypeMultiplatformTest` measures exactly that, with a validator that appends to a list, and it is what failed on the first attempt. **A memo's key must cover every input its computation is allowed to observe, including how many times it is called.**

Two cheaper roots came with it: `path to reps in unavailable` and `path to reps in dup` allocate a `Pair` and hash the repetition list *before* an empty set can answer "no", and both sets are empty on the ordinary path (outside partial validation, absent a duplicated index), so the emptiness is now checked first.

**What it bought, measured:** 34 of the 36 anchored growth observations fell, seven below `0.6×` of their previous ceiling — `Berlin DATE_TIME @20000` 157.37 → 73.45 ms (`0.47×`), `B1 @20000` 49.27 → 27.28, `semantic-index @20000` 60.22 → 35.77, `complexRealWorld` `0.65×`/`0.67×`. The rows that did not move are the compute-overlay ones (`CO-correlation` `0.96×`/`0.98×`), which is what the mechanism predicts: a per-cell formal cache is hit by validate paths, not by overlay arithmetic. `CO-index` read `1.04×`/`1.11×`, i.e. marginally slower, which is inside this machine's measured `1.42×` run-to-run band and is recorded rather than claimed as zero.

*Locked by:* `FormalCacheCellIdentityMultiplatformTest` (JVM + JS) holds the two invariants a cheaper key could break. The **first** — the same value in an in-cap and an over-limit row must not share a formal result — is the positive control for this change: it fails against the naive value-only key, which is how it was checked, and the pre-existing `OverRepetitionFormalGateMultiplatformTest` could not have caught the fold because it uses different values in its two rows. The **second** — a duplicate index value must not spread to another parent's row — passes both before and after, because duplicate membership was never inside the admission memo; it guards the rival keys IG122 listed (`(context, path, raw)`, or a value-keyed outer cache) rather than this diff, and is kept for that. `CustomFieldTypeMultiplatformTest` holds the invocation count against the interpreter, and `CustomFieldTypeContextDiffTest.theSameValueInTwoCellsReachesTheValidatorOncePerCell` holds it against the **real kernel** on both strategies — with its own positive control, since routing the code-based branch through the value-shaped map turns exactly that test red at `expected: <2> but was: <1>`. The whole `adapter/laws` differential tier and `:interpreter:check` (1,200 JVM + 1,177 JS) cover the semantics. Dated evidence: INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE "Checkpoint 2026-08-12 — the stacked formal-result memos".

### IF285: the list-in-key sweep — a fixed mechanism's siblings are found by profiling, not by pattern-matching the source (SWEPT 2026-08-12; graduated from IG126) <a id="if285"></a>

[IF284](#if284) fixed one memo whose key was a repetition list and named five siblings with the same shape. The sweep the root-cause rule requires was then run, and it inverted the list: **the class's two live members were not among the five.** Numbers, method and conditions: INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE "Checkpoint 2026-08-12 — the list-in-key sweep".

**Corrected 2026-08-12, by a cold review of this very finding: the sweep did NOT establish that the five are cheap.** It first claimed "all five named memos are cold". One of them was measured — `AggregateMemoKey` is a `data class`, so its `hashCode`/`equals` appear as frames and a hot one would be visible. The other four read 0 because **the code that owns them never executed** in either profiled scenario: `ValidationPass.indexColumns` documents itself as "Empty and unread in a FULL validation" and both profiles are full validations, so its 0 was guaranteed by construction rather than measured. They are also *fields* rather than classes, so no frame carries their name even when hot. IG126 is reopened for them with a per-memo oracle. **This is the same defect the finding below diagnoses in IF284's sibling list, committed while writing the diagnosis:** a completeness claim resting on an oracle that structurally cannot see the thing it is about.

**Enumerating by shape over-collects; the discriminator is call multiplicity.** Most sites carrying this shape are *accumulators* — one entry per computed cell, written about once per unit of work, where key hashing is proportional to the work rather than multiplied by it. Only a *memo consulted repeatedly per unit of work* pays IF284's cost. The count is a judgement call (one query yields 22 declarations, a looser one 74), so the checkpoint publishes the query rather than a number. `AggregateMemoKey` deserves a separate note: it is gated to unfiltered starred aggregates outside compute mode, so it rarely engages, but its key holds an `Expr.Func` — a `data class`, so a lookup hashes the **entire expression subtree**, an unbounded structure where IF284's was a handful of ints. That is a latent, not a measured, cost; the sound fix if it ever surfaces is identity rather than structural equality, since the AST node is shared across rows within a call and a distinct-but-equal instance would only miss the memo.

**The two live members are different mechanisms, and neither is "a list in a key" as stated.** One is an **unmemoized prelude**: `ValueAdmissionCache.resultAt` runs `overRepetitionResultAt` before consulting its memo, so IF284's own decision to move that walk *out* of the key left it running on every consultation by every consumer — it is now larger than the memoized work it guards. The other, `OverlayContext.stateOf`, is IF284's shape exactly, but the cost is the **number of lookups** rather than the key: three collections keyed by the same pair mean the repetition list is walked three times for one logical read. Its own KDoc records an earlier round of this fix that removed three key *constructions* and left the three *hashes*.

**The generalizable lesson is about the sweep, not the memos — and it applies twice over.** IF284's sibling list was assembled by pattern-matching the source for the shape that had just been fixed, which is how the two real costs were missed: one had a different shape and the other a different multiplier. Then this sweep read a 0 from a scenario that cannot execute four of the five and called it cold, which is the mirror error: the first trusted a query it never measured, the second trusted a measurement whose denominator it never checked. A profile attributes a key mechanic to the frame that built the key, so it finds members by their cost instead of by their spelling — and the hottest member of this whole class, `Document.Index.valueByKey`, turns out to have been fixed by exactly IF284's mechanism long before IF284, with the reason written in its own comment. **Enumerate a class by measuring it, then read the source to explain what measurement found.**

*Instrument:* `scripts/perf-keys.sh <validate|compute|recording.jfr> [frame …]` — the committed sweep, so the numbers are reproducible and the two follow-up fixes have a before/after. It reads stacks rather than self time, because this class's cost lands in `java.util.*` frames attributed to nobody.

### IF286: one entry per cell — the cascade overlay's read cost was the NUMBER of lookups, not the key (FIXED 2026-08-12; closes IG127's second item) <a id="if286"></a>

`OverlayContext` answered every read by probing **three** collections — `overlay: Map<Pair<String, List<Int>>, Val>`, `cleared` and `poisoned` as `Set<Pair<…>>` — under one key. A `Pair<String, List<Int>>` hashes by walking the repetition list, so one logical read walked it three times, and `starCells`/`starScanStates` walked it three times *per row*. Measured: INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE "Checkpoint 2026-08-12 — the cascade overlay's three hashes of one key" (the heading that pointer names was briefly destroyed by a later edit to the same region and has been restored).

**This is [IF284](#if284)'s shape with a different multiplier, which is why the sibling sweep missed it** ([IF285](#if285)). IF284's cost was one key that was too wide; here each key was the same width and simply asked three times. An earlier round had already removed three key *constructions* from this exact function and left the three *hashes* — the KDoc recorded the improvement and the residual went unnoticed, because a profile attributes it to `java.util.*` rather than to the function.

**[CascadeOverlay] holds one entry per cell, nested `path → reps → entry`** — the same fix, and the same reason, as `Document.Index.valueByKey`'s. Two properties do the work: the outer `String` lookup is cheap and platform-hash-cached, so **a path the cascade never touched exits without hashing a list or even calling `repsOf`** (itself a map lookup the flat form had to make before it could build a key); and a column-wide read resolves `rowsAt(path)` **once** and then probes per row, so a star read over an untouched column costs nothing at all.

**Collapsing three collections into one entry moves precedence from the read to the write, and that is the part that could have broken silently.** The flat form applied `poisoned > cleared > overlaid` at read time and never removed a poisoned cell from `overlay`, so a later overlay write could not un-poison a cell — the read simply kept answering POISONED. With one entry per cell the last write wins, so **poison is absorbing on write**. That is enforced locally rather than argued away: a poisoned cell is also marked `decided`, which the cascade skips, but that is an invariant in another file, and a guarantee that depends on a caller's ordering is one refactor away from being false. Cause-blindness is unchanged (SPEC-2026-07-26-04) — `Entry.Poisoned` carries identity only.

*Locked by:* `CascadeOverlayPrecedenceTest` (JVM + JS) holds the write-side precedence, including the case the read-time form made impossible — an overlay and a clear arriving *after* a poison must both be ignored. The semantics themselves are held by the existing cascade estate: the full `adapter/laws` differential tier (333 classes, 1,906 cases against both kernel strategies) and `:interpreter:check`, with `BareGroupComputeExactnessTest` exercising a hand-built overlay directly.

### IF287: `FieldValuesNotUnique` missed a duplicate inside a GROUP operand — a kernel-legal shape the evaluator answered FALSE (FIXED 2026-08-12; closes DG33's blocker) <a id="if287"></a>

The kernel admits a **group-scope operand** here (KERNEL-FINDINGS [`kf173`](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf173)) and expands it to its contained fields, exactly as it does for the value aggregates. Both kernel strategies then **fire** on a duplicate among the expanded cells; this evaluator returned **FALSE**. For a uniqueness rule that is the worst polarity of divergence — a duplicate goes unreported, and nothing signals it, because the rule evaluates cleanly rather than degrading.

**Two distinct causes, one per operand shape**, which is why a single-shape probe would have half-fixed it:

| authored operand | what the evaluator did | why |
|---|---|---|
| `FieldValuesNotUnique(/Shipment/Origin)` — an unstarred group | contributed **no** cells | `pathOf` + `ctx.filled` ask about a *group* path, where no field is declared, so the operand read as empty |
| `FieldValuesNotUnique(/Shipment/Legs*)` — a starred group | contributed **no** cells | `filteredStarCells` reads cells at the starred path and, unlike [`starOperandVals`](#if49), does not expand a group into its contained fields |

**This is the `Sum(Lines*)` defect one operator over, and the reason it survived the fix that found it.** `GroupStarValueAggregateDiffTest` records the same mistake — reading cells at a group path and computing the empty selection — fixed in `starOperandVals` by the `declaredKind == null → containedFields` recursion. Its own note says the aggregate siblings and a validation-side rule ride that chokepoint, and `FieldValuesNotUnique` *looks* like it should: it consumes starred operands and honours a `Having`. It simply routes through a different collector. **A shared-chokepoint claim is about the call graph, not about the family**; the members that route elsewhere are exactly the ones a source reading places inside the fix and a differential places outside it.

**The expansion was deliberately NOT pushed into `filteredStarCells`.** Its other caller is the date-range overlap operator, whose behaviour over a group operand is **unmeasured** — widening a shared path past its measured basis is the defect class CLAUDE.md's "a client-side refusal needs a measured kernel basis" rule names, in its mirror form. The new `comparedCells` recursion lives in the operator that measurement covers, and preserves the star's `Having` and binding onto each expanded field ([IF99](#if99)) because the filter belongs to the authored operand rather than to one of its expanded cells.

**A THIRD cause, found 2026-08-13 by measuring the one extent this entry left open — and it was the same silent polarity.** The 2026-08-12 handover to a12-kernel-lean listed nesting as unmeasured: no row covered a group operand containing a nested *repeatable* subgroup. Measured, the kernel expands recursively and **crosses that subgroup's rows** with no star written (KERNEL-FINDINGS [`kf173`](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf173)), while this evaluator read the nested repetition at a **single row-1 coordinate** — so a duplicate pairing a direct field with nested row 2, and one lying between two nested rows, both went unreported.

The mechanism is confirmed by a discriminator rather than by the observation it explains: pairing the direct field with nested **row 2** fails under "reads only row 1" and *succeeds* under the rival "expands every row but compares them only against the direct fields", and it was missed — so the rival is falsified. The cause is that the recursion chose `Expr.Star` vs `Expr.Field` from the **operand's** shape, so a non-starred group operand turned a field living under a repeatable subgroup into a plain field ref, which resolves at the ambient coordinate. Both arms now expand to a starred field, and the star form iterates every repeatable level below the evaluating row while a field directly in a non-repeatable group degenerates to the single row the plain ref used to read.

*Mechanism corrected 2026-08-13, same day, after the extent above was measured at two levels.* This paragraph first said the default `bindDepth = -1` "binds above the leaf group's parent, so the nested repetition iterates fully", crediting the iteration to that default. That is wrong, and it was a source reading dressed as the mechanism — precisely what CLAUDE.md's oracle-by-claim-kind rule forbids. `Document.groupScopePrefix` computes `rowReps.take(minOf(commonPrefixLen(starParentParts, contextGroupParts), cap))`, where `cap` is what `bindDepth` sets: **`bindDepth` is only an upper bound, and the binding that actually applies is the shared named ancestry with the EVALUATING context** ([IF93](#if93)/[IF94](#if94)). Every case here authors its rule on the shallow root group, so `commonPrefixLen` is 1, the cap never binds, and all levels below iterate — which is also why two stacked repeatable levels needed no further fix. The prediction derived from the wrong reading (that `-1` would pin the outer repetition of a two-level path) was falsified by the measurement it was written to explain.

**That correction surfaced a third extent, and it too is now MEASURED and agrees.** A group operand on a rule authored at a **deeper iterating group** — ordinary per-row validation, and the placement all the earlier rows omit — was the case the corrected mechanism said should behave differently. With the rule on `/Shipment/Depot/Handovers` and the operand naming its ancestor `/Shipment/Depot`, a duplicate spanning two different `Handovers` rows **fires**, on all three arms: the compared set is bounded by the **operand**, not by the rule's own row. Its control (a duplicate inside one row, which fires under either scope) confirms the rule evaluates.

**The MECHANISM here is deliberately left unestablished, and that is the finding worth carrying.** Three successive readings of `Document.groupScopePrefix` produced three predictions — that `-1` drives the nested iteration, that two stacked levels would pin the outer one, that a deeper rule group would scope the operand to its own row — and the differential falsified **all three**. The last one is the sharpest: `commonPrefixLen(starParent, contextGroup)` is 3 here and the cap is 3, which reads as a pin, and it does not pin. So the behaviour of this function under a converted group-expansion star is **not derivable from reading it**, at least not by the reading applied three times, and anything asserted about it belongs behind a differential. What IS established is the observable contract, on both kernel strategies and every arm: the compared set is every `(row × field)` cell reachable below the operand, independent of depth and independent of where the rule sits.

**The class is NOT shared, and that was measured before the fix rather than argued.** `starOperandVals` — which this expansion is modelled on — handles nesting correctly: `Sum(Charges*)` over two `Extras` rows under one `Charges` row plus one under another totals `7.50` on both engines. Its existing lock had the identical blind spot for a different reason, placing exactly **one** nested row, where "every row" and "row 1 only" predict the same total; the sibling row is now in `GroupStarValueAggregateDiffTest`, so the lock states the extent instead of being consistent with either reading.

**Both extents this entry recorded as unmeasured are now measured, and both AGREE (2026-08-13).** Two stacked repeatable levels below the operand fire on a duplicate pairing the direct field with `Handovers[2]/Scans[2]` — neither level at row 1 — and on one spanning two different `Handovers` rows' `Scans`; an **authored** star over a repeatable group that itself holds a repeatable subgroup fires both within one outer row's later nested row and across two outer rows' nested subgroups. Each has a negative control on the same fixture, so a pass is not silence. No code change was needed for either, which is the outcome the corrected mechanism above predicts and the original reading did not.

**Message MULTIPLICITY and ANCHOR, which every row above was structurally unable to answer (added 2026-08-14).** All nineteen rows assert `any { code }` — a **boolean** — so one message and two were indistinguishable throughout, and the extent findings said nothing about how many messages an extent produces or where they attach. The lean project named the gap and its discriminator in its 2026-08-14 disposition. Measured, on all three arms: a group operand reports **exactly one message**, anchored at the error field's **concrete first row**, and neither number nor address depends on where the duplicate lies. Three cases separate that from the rivals it would otherwise be confused with — a duplicate spanning two outer rows under a root-placed rule gives one message at `/Shipment[1]/Reference`; the same duplicate under a rule authored on the **repeatable** group the operand names still gives one, which alone is ambiguous because that error path resolves above the group so both rows address the same cell; moving the error field **inside** the rule's own group gives per-row addresses to report at, and it is still one message, at `Consignments[1]/Ref`. The fourth places the duplicate **wholly inside row 2** and the anchor stays at row 1, which is what separates "anchored at the first row" from "anchored where the duplicate is". This matches what the lean spec already records for the starred *field* form, so the group form needs no separate clause on this axis.

**A THIRD silent row-1 pin, in the reference channel this time, found by widening the assertion rather than by adding a case (2026-08-14).** The multiplicity rows above first asserted `errorPath|type`; adding `referenced` to the same comparison immediately failed on the **unstarred** arm and only there. The kernel writes a **wildcard** coordinate at each repeatable level below the operand — `/Shipment[1]/Carrier[1]/Handoffs[0]/Site` — and the interpreter wrote the concrete `Handoffs[1]/Site`. **Root cause, one layer up from the symptom:** `ModelStaticPlans.expandMessageReference` expanded a group reference into its contained fields while *preserving the reference kind*, so an `Ordinary` group reference produced `Ordinary` field references, which `messagePointer` resolves through `ctx.repsOf` — a concrete row. The starred arm was correct only because its reference already arrived as a `Wildcard`. The fix binds an expanded field at the **group's own group-depth**, so the operand's own level and everything above it stay concrete while levels strictly below it wildcard iff repeatable; a group with no repeatable level below it therefore expands exactly as before and needs no special case.

**The fix's first form was too broad, and a kernel-locked test caught it.** Reading the `ordinaryErrorReference` decision off the *expanded* list conflated two different questions and changed which rules take the semantic-index error-context path — `FirstFilledValueEntitySpecDiffTest` failed on its semantic-group pointers. The two questions are now asked separately: *how the condition names the error path* is answered before expansion (a group expansion is **not** an authored star, so it still counts as ordinary there), while *how a pointer renders* is answered after it. Recorded because the coupling is invisible at the call site — one list served two consumers with opposite needs.

**The scope correction that prompted all of this.** The four multiplicity rows all authored `FieldValuesNotUnique(/Shipment/Consignments*)`, so "the group operand reports once at the first row" was measured for the **starred** form only, and the entry said it without qualification. The a12-kernel-lean project flagged it. The unstarred arm is now measured on the `Carrier` fixture and **agrees**: one message, anchored at the error field's concrete first row, including when the duplicate lies wholly in nested rows 2 and 3 while row 1 is distinct.

**A FOURTH pin, predicted from source and then measured — and it is the one that shows the two channels had diverged (2026-08-14).** An audit of the class asked where else an expansion takes a coordinate from the ambient context, and named this: the non-starred arm of `Interpreter.comparedCells` converted its expanded field to `Expr.Star(field, …)` **without a bindDepth**, taking the `-1` default. `-1` means "the leaf group's parent", which iterates a nested repetition fully *only while nothing else anchors an intermediate level* — and iteration here is reference-driven, so a sibling reference can anchor one. The discriminator, which the existing deeper-rule-group row could not reach because every reference in it is non-repeatable and the plan is therefore `Once`: guard on a direct field of the middle repeatable level (`FieldFilled(…/Handovers/Marker)`), operand naming its ancestor `/Shipment/Depot`, duplicate spanning **two** `Handovers` rows' `Scans` with the direct field distinct. Both kernel strategies fire; the interpreter returned **FALSE**. Fixed by binding the expanded field at the **operand's own group depth**, so every level strictly below the operand stays free whatever the rule iterates.

**This is the same rule the reference channel already used, and that is the point.** After the third fix, `expandMessageReference` bound at the operand's depth while `comparedCells` bound at `-1`, so one operand's two channels disagreed about its own extent — the reference set said "all `Handovers` rows" while the compared set read one. Two channels of one operator disagreeing is what the third defect looked like inverted, and it is the cheapest available signal that one of them is wrong. **The authored-star arm was measured on the same question and is correct unchanged** (guarding on `Consignments/Ref` anchors iteration inside the starred group; the cross-row duplicate still fires), so the fix is bounded to the conversion that had no depth to carry.

**The class is NOT confined to this operator — a fifth instance, in `NumberOfFilledFields` (2026-08-14).** The audit's ranking put it second, on a structural argument rather than a suspicion: the `Star` branch of `filledFieldsValue` has **no group check while the `else` branch of the same function does**. A function that disagrees with itself about whether a group needs expanding is the shape the whole class shares, and it is the more useful signal than any individual site. Measured: `NumberOfFilledFields(/Order/Lines*) >= 3` over two fully-filled rows fires on both kernel strategies and the interpreter returned **false**, because for a group path `ctx.star` resolves through the group's *parent* rows and reads a value at the group path, where no field is declared — count **0**. Fixed by expanding a starred group operand to its contained fields at entry, each carrying the authored operand's `having`/`outer`/`bindDepth`. **The star is the only legal spelling here** — a repeatable group operand unstarred is rejected `MVK_NO_WILDCARD` — so the branch that could not expand was the only one a real author can reach. *(Locked by `adapter.laws.GroupStarFilledFieldsDiffTest`: the `>= 3` bar is reachable only by counting across both rows, so a per-row count and an unexpanded count both predict "does not fire"; plus a does-not-overcount control, an empty-group control, and a cell that exists only in row 2.)*

**A sixth instance, in the value aggregates, and it makes the "disagrees with a sibling" tell three-for-three (2026-08-14).** `starOperandVals` carries the group branch; its file-mate `aggOperandVals` did not, and its `else` arm decides an operand by `ctx.filled(pathOf(arg))` — false for **every** group path, since a group holds no value of its own. The operand was therefore dropped and the aggregate folded to its empty identity: a silent `Sum` of `0`. Measured, `Sum(/Order/Billing) > 5` over `3 + 4` fires on both kernel strategies and returned false here. Fixed by expanding a group operand to its contained fields, bound at the operand's own depth. Again the unstarred spelling is the only legal one for a non-repeatable group, so the arm that could not expand was the reachable one. *(Locked by `adapter.laws.UnstarredGroupSumOperandDiffTest`, whose lock **asserts kernel admission first**: `kf173` measured the star gate under `Sum` and the unstarred admission under a different carrier, so a rejection here would have silenced both engines and passed every row while measuring nothing. `minMaxOperandVals` has the same shape and is NOT fixed — it is unmeasured, and stays in IG136 rather than being widened on symmetry.)*

**A seventh instance, and the worst-shaped one: a validation/compute MODE SPLIT (2026-08-14).** `scanFilled`'s bare-group arm expanded and its starred arm did not, so every fill quantifier over a starred group was correct on the validation path — which dispatches on `fillScope(isGroup = …)` — and read **zero cells** on the compute path. A mode split is the nastiest member of the class because the operator is demonstrably right wherever the estate pairs it and wrong on the path nobody paired. **The evidence is a pair that fails in OPPOSITE directions**, which is stronger than either case alone: with every cell filled, `NoFieldFilled(Charges*)` answered TRUE and `AtLeastOneFieldFilled(Charges*)` answered FALSE — that is the signature of a quantifier over an EMPTY selection, not of a wrong verdict. `NoFieldFilled` is also the only usable polarity here: `AllFieldsFilled` answers vacuously TRUE over zero cells, which is the same verdict a correct implementation gives on a fully-filled document, so it cannot separate the readings. *(Locked by `adapter.laws.GroupStarFillComputeModeDiffTest`, with the positive control that proves the precondition evaluated at all.)*

**This fix is the first step of the ROOT cause rather than another instance.** The expansion now has **one owner** — `expandGroupStars` — which both the filled-field count and the compute-path fill scan call, instead of each site re-deriving it. That is the shape the class needs everywhere: while expanding a group is a step an operator must remember, fixing instances cannot close it.

**An eighth instance, in `NumberOfValueInFields`, and it came with an authoring gap attached (2026-08-14).** Neither arm expanded: the starred arm took the group's PARENT rows and read a value at the group path, the plain arm asked `filled` of a path that holds no value, so both counted `0`. Because our DSL has no `GroupListRef` overload here, the shape is **type-level unrepresentable** — the quiet refusal shape DG33 was — so the lock measures ADMISSION first and records the kernel's verdict as its own test. The kernel **admits** the operand and counts one match per row across two rows; the interpreter returned false. So this site carried two defects at once: an unbased client-side refusal (now [DG34](DEV-API-GAPS.md)) and a wrong runtime answer. *(Locked by `adapter.laws.GroupStarValueInFieldsDiffTest`, whose admission test states in its own failure message that a rejection would be the finding rather than a defect.)*

**A predicted ninth instance that turned out to be a REFUSAL, and the negative is worth as much as the fixes (2026-08-14).** A second audit predicted that a *filtered* group star (`AtLeastOneFieldFilled(G* Having …)`) diverges between the paths: validation's group-aware scope arm is gated on `having == null`, so a filtered group star falls to the naive star tally and reads at the group path, while the compute fix above expands it and carries the filter through. The code reading is correct and the paths really do disagree — but the kernel **REFUSES the shape**: `MVK_NO_GROUPS_ALLOWED`, "At 'Having': It is not allowed to use a group", thrown at code generation rather than reported as a diagnostic. So the divergence is unreachable by authoring, and the `having` our shared expansion carries onto each expanded field is **dead for the group case** — unreachable rather than untested, which is why no test can pin what duplicating a filter across N expanded fields would mean. *(Locked by `adapter.laws.FilteredGroupStarFillDiffTest` on both quantifier polarities, with a control proving the same filter over a starred FIELD list of the same group IS admitted — otherwise the refusal could be read as the kernel disliking the fixture.)* **The lesson is the ordering:** admission is a question no amount of source reading answers, and it is what separated a defect from a non-issue here. Four of this session's nine predictions were settled only by asking the kernel first.

**A NINTH instance, in the POLARITY channel, and a cold review predicted it from the fix that made it reachable (2026-08-14).** The value channel's group expansion (sixth instance) turned a dormant defect live: `PolarityWalk.aggregateFillState`'s `Expr.Field` arm returned `null` for a group path, so `numericAggregateFill` fell to `legacyStarFill`, whose `singleOrNull() as? Expr.Star` is null for a `Field`, and the polarity came from `leafOmission(Expr.Field(groupPath))` — where `!ctx.filled(groupPath)` is TRUE for every group, since a group holds no value of its own. The result is a symmetric fill state and an **OMISSION** where the kernel answers **VALUE**. Measured: `Sum(/Order/Billing) > 5` over `3 + 4` gives `VALUE_ERROR` on both kernel strategies and gave `OMISSION_ERROR` here. Its own `Expr.Star` arm already expanded — the sibling tell, for the fourth time. Fixed by expanding a group operand in that arm too.

**Two things about this one are the session's most transferable lessons.** First, **the lock for the sixth instance could not see it**: that row asserted `any { code }`, and a message of the wrong TYPE reads as agreement, so the fix and a fresh defect shipped in one commit under a green test. Widening the same row's projection to `field|type` exposed it with no new fixture — the third time in this class that widening an assertion beat adding a case. Second, **a fix can MAKE a parked defect reachable**: before the value channel expanded, the operand was dropped, `Sum` folded to `0` and the rule never fired, so the mistyping was unobservable. IG136 had already named `PolarityWalk.legacyStarFill` as an unmeasured locus — so a known-parked defect went live in the same commit that added a lock unable to detect it. When a fix makes a previously-dead path live, the parked findings that mention that path are the first place to look.

**The predicted SIBLING of that polarity defect does NOT manifest — measured, not assumed (2026-08-14).** The same review flagged `countFill` (`PolarityWalk.kt:311-328`) as the identical shape one operator over: it skips a star with no `Having` outright (`st.having ?: continue`) and falls back to a leaf-omission reading of the group path. It also correctly noted why no existing row could see it — every filled-count bar in the estate is `>=`, which can only grow more true as cells fill, so a correct and a group-blind polarity read both answer VALUE. Measured at a **shrink** bar, `NumberOfFilledFields(/Order/Lines*) < 5` over four filled cells: the interpreter agrees with both kernel strategies on the message type. Recorded as a measured negative so the next reader does not re-open it from the same correct code reading — the fourth time this session that a structurally sound prediction turned out not to manifest.

**A TENTH instance, and the only one that CRASHES instead of answering wrongly (2026-08-14).** `dateAggregate` decides whether a min/max rides the kernel's date machinery by asking `declaredKind` of each operand — null for a group — so a group of DATE fields took the NUMERIC branch. Its own file-mate `firstFilledEntityKind` already implements the correct fallback (`declaredKind(path) ?: containedFields(path).firstNotNullOfOrNull(…)`): the sibling tell, for the fifth time. Reading the code **bounds the damage to exactly two consequences**, which is worth more than the instance itself: `Aggregates.min`/`max` use the empty identity only as a fallback (`values.minWithOrNull(ORDER) ?: empty`) and never as a seed, so a non-empty date selection folds correctly either way. What is left is (1) `minMaxEmpty` handing back `Val.Num(0)` where the date branch hands back `Val.Unknown`, and (2) `producesDate` routing through the same predicate, so a counterpart string literal stops parsing as a German date. Both measured: `MinValue(<date group>)` over an all-empty selection threw **`IllegalStateException: incomparable values Num(0) vs Txt(2026-06-15)`** while both kernel strategies simply do not fire, and `MinValue(<date group>) < "01.07.2026"` fired on both strategies and returned false here. **The crash is the severity finding:** the degrade net catches only `UnsupportedConstructException`, so this propagates and sinks the whole model evaluation rather than one rule — the same mechanism as the F4 crash earlier in this class, reached this time from a *legal* model and a *legal* document. One edit fixes both consequences because the literal coercion consults the same predicate.

*Locked by* `adapter.laws.DateGroupExtremaDiffTest`, whose two PASSING rows are what make the two failing ones mean something: the group operand's answer and the single-field control's answer are arranged to fall on **opposite sides** of the compared date, so a group that silently read only its first declared field would have agreed with the control instead of contradicting it — and the polarity row asserts `field|type` rather than a boolean. Each group condition is *derived from its control by substituting the operand alone*, with `assertOperandSubstituted` failing if that substitution misses, because a missed substitution would compare the control to itself and pass.

**An ELEVENTH instance, in the value-list quantifiers, and this one got the ROOT treatment rather than a twelfth private copy (2026-08-14).** `vlqOperands` had no group check in either arm: its `Star` arm calls `starCells` at the starred path, of which a group path has none, and its `Field` arm takes `presenceTri`, FALSE for a group — so the quantifier ran over an EMPTY selection and still returned a well-formed verdict. Measured on a document holding a listed value: `AtLeastOne` returned false and `No` returned true, the **opposite-direction signature of an empty selection** rather than of a wrong verdict, which is the same evidence shape that settled the seventh instance. The kernel admits all three plural forms over both group shapes ([KF187](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf187)).

**The fix gave the expansion RULE one owner instead of an eleventh site.** `expandOneGroupOperand` is now the single place that knows *how* a group operand expands — recursively to the declared fields below it, each carrying the operand's `Having`, `$`-marking, and a bind depth resolved to the operand's own level, and yielding the operand unchanged when the group holds no field. Two entry points select *which shapes* a carrier expands: `expandGroupStars` (starred only, for the callers whose unstarred handling is separately measured) and `expandGroupOperands` (both shapes). That split is deliberate and is the "measured basis" rule applied to our own refactor — collapsing the two would widen three already-measured call sites past what any row covers, which is the mirror image of the defect being fixed.

**A cold review found two false claims in the admission sweep this instance rests on, and both were in a published finding.** [KF187](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf187) asserted `Min`/`Max` and `MinValue`/`MaxValue` verdicts in both repetition shapes while two of the four carriers had been measured in only one, and its lock note claimed every rejection carried an admitted field control when two did not. Corrected in place, with the missing rows measured. The re-run then produced three facts the first pass had missed: the kernel **type-checks the expansion** (a DATE group against a string value list is refused, so ACCEPT genuinely means "expanded"), **arity does not** count it (a single-field group passes the param law that same field alone fails), and the `$` refusal is a **universal filter-only gate** that says nothing about aggregates — a bare `[$…] > 0` comparison draws the identical code. That last one had been used to close a defect, so the correction reopened it.

**A TWELFTH instance, and it is the class's other mode split — a DIVERTED dispatch rather than a validate/compute one (2026-08-14).** A field-fill quantifier is routed onto the group/starred-scope path as soon as **any** operand qualifies (`hasFillScopeOperand`: a bare group, or a star with no `Having`), and that path called `EvalContext.fillScope` for **every** operand — a function that takes no `having` parameter at all, so a filter could not be honoured there even in principle. The victim and the trigger therefore have to be *different operands*: a filtered star alone in its list diverts nothing, takes the ordinary tally path, and is filtered correctly. `AtLeastOneFieldFilled(/Inv/Charges*/Amount, /Inv/Lines*/Amount Having …)` is the reachable shape, the kernel admits it ([KF187](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf187)), and the estate contained no rule of that shape.

Measured on a document whose only filled cell sits in the row the filter DROPS: `NoFieldFilled` returned false where both kernel strategies fire, and `AtLeastOneFieldFilled` returned true where they do not — **opposite directions**, so the evidence is about the dropped filter rather than about either quantifier's verdict. The same document with only the filtered operand in the list agrees on every arm, which localizes the defect to the sibling's mere presence.

**The declared-range half needed its own discriminator, and the first two attempts at it were vacuous.** `AllFieldsFilled` is the one quantifier whose scope-path answer comes from the declared range rather than the instantiated tally, so what a filter means for it is a kernel question. Two rows asserting it passed — and passed for the wrong reason: the *sibling's* cells were empty, which makes the quantifier false whichever way the filter is read. Filling every sibling cell so it cannot decide split the readings, and the kernel fires. The fix therefore derives the declared flags from the same kept-row scan, with the one interaction left explicitly unmeasured: a filter is a predicate over a row, so an un-instantiated declared repetition has no kept-or-dropped answer, and every measured row has its full declared range instantiated.

**A THIRTEENTH instance, in the polarity channel again, and it was PREDICTED by the review of the tenth (2026-08-14).** A min/max over a group of DATE fields answered **OMISSION** where both kernel strategies answer **VALUE**, on a document with every cell filled — so nothing was missing and the message type was simply wrong. Both repetition shapes, and for two different reasons that meet at the same place. `aggregateFillState` rejects a non-NUMBER expansion, so a date group falls through to `legacyStarFill`; there, the **starred** group's `dateLike(declaredKind(path))` test is FALSE because a group path has no declared kind, sending a date group down the *numeric* branches to read `starOmission`/`star`/`signed` at a path where nothing is stored — while the **unstarred** group is not an `Expr.Star` at all, so `singleOrNull() as? Expr.Star` is null and the polarity came from `leafOmission`, whose `!filled(groupPath)` is TRUE for every group. Fixed by resolving the operand's kind the way its `ExprEval` sibling already does and OR-ing the omission across the expansion; scoped to the DATE family, because the numeric group operand is already handled and a group of some other non-numeric kind reaching a min/max is unmeasured.

**The lock that measured it had the right assertion pointed at the wrong condition, which is the ninth instance's lesson recurring one level up.** The tenth instance's own polarity row asserted `field|type` — correctly widened — on the single-field CONTROL, the one condition whose polarity was never in question, so the group operand's polarity went unmeasured inside the lock added to measure it. A cold review caught that. Two further self-inflicted vacuities were caught by the assertion itself once it pointed at the right condition: the first attempt reused the *discriminator* document, which is built so the group form does NOT fire, and a row that does not fire measures no polarity at all — `assertSamePolarity` now refuses a silent kernel outright rather than comparing two empty lists.

**A FOURTEENTH instance, and it is the class's tell in its purest form — one expression consulting two different contexts (2026-08-14).** `aggOperandVals` decided an operand's presence with `ctx.filled(path)`, the AMBIENT context, while the line beside it and `evalExpr(arg)` both resolve through `ctxOf(arg)` — which returns the **outer** context for a `$`-marked node. So the fill test and the value read consulted different rows, and the sibling `starOperandVals` uses `ctxOf` throughout. Measured with the rule authored ON a repeatable group, so `$` addresses the rule's row while the plain reference addresses the filtered row: the kernel fires on row 2 and the interpreter returned **nothing**. Confirmed load-bearing by mutation — restoring `ctx.filled` turns exactly that row red and leaves the two controls green.

**This one was CLOSED as unreachable earlier the same day, on a measurement that measured something else.** A first sweep put `$` under `Sum` at the top level, read `MVK_INVALID_OUTER_ITERATION`, and concluded an aggregate cannot carry a `$` operand. A cold review traced that code to its single locus — `CheckEntitySpecImpl.checkOuter`, gated on `ctx.outer != null && !isInFilterCondition()`, which names no carrier — and to a KERNEL-FINDINGS line that already said `$` is **filter-only**. The probe had re-measured a documented universal gate and the "control" could not discriminate, because the marker is refused everywhere at top level. Probed where `$` is legal, inside a filter, the shape is admitted for a field operand list *and* for a group operand. **The lesson is narrower than "measure admission": a refusal only closes a question when the refusal is ABOUT the thing you are asking. A universal gate answers nothing about any particular carrier**, and the tell was available for free — the control refused too.

**A FIFTEENTH instance, in the date-range overlap predicate — the one this entry had explicitly declined to fix, and the reason it gave turns out to be half wrong (2026-08-14).** This entry records that the expansion was deliberately NOT pushed into the shared `filteredStarCells` because "its other caller is the date-range overlap operator, whose behaviour over a group operand is **unmeasured**" — the right instinct, applied to an unmeasured premise. Measured: `DateRangesOverlap(<group>)` is indeed refused `MVK_NO_GROUPS_ALLOWED` in both repetition shapes, but the plural `AtLeastOneDateRangeOverlaps(<scalar> In <group>)` is **ADMITTED** ([KF187](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf187)). One predicate family, two forms, opposite verdicts — so half the reason held and half did not, and the shape was authorable the whole time.

**Both arms of `overlapCells` were blind, by different mechanisms, and the unstarred one CRASHED.** The starred arm read `star` at the group path — zero cells, a silent non-overlap where the kernel fires. The unstarred arm asked `evalExpr` for a `Val.Range` at a path that holds no value and threw **`IllegalStateException: no kind declared`**, which the degrade net does not catch, so it sank the whole evaluation from a legal model and a legal document. That is the second crash-class member of this class, after the tenth instance, and both were reached the same way: a shape the kernel admits, on a path our code had only ever seen carrying a declared field.

*Locked by* `adapter.laws.GroupOperandDateRangeOverlapDiffTest`, whose discriminator is unreachable by a wrong expansion in **two** independent ways at once — the only overlapping cell is `Periods[2]/Grace`, so a row-1 pin misses it *and* an expansion reaching only the group's first declared field misses it — with the two per-field controls (`Grace` fires, `Window` does not) pinning which failure a divergence would have been. The fixture carries a DATERANGE scalar at the ROOT specifically so the unstarred arm is probeable: the operator's first slot refuses a group and any wildcard, so without a scalar outside the group under test there is no legal left-hand operand, which is why the admission sweep could not reach that arm.

**The generalization worth carrying: the correct behavior is OPT-IN at every one of these sites, and the default is wrong.** Five instances, and in three of them the code disagrees with a sibling that got it right — `filledFieldsValue`'s two branches, `starOperandVals` versus `aggOperandVals` in one file, validation's `fillScope(isGroup = …)` versus compute's `starScanCells`. Group expansion is a step each operator must remember rather than a chokepoint it cannot bypass, which is why fixing instances does not stop the class. The remaining audited candidates are recorded in [INTERPRETER-GAPS](INTERPRETER-GAPS.md) IG136 rather than fixed on the strength of a source read.

*Locked by:* `adapter.laws.FieldValuesNotUniqueGroupOperandDiffTest` — twenty-eight tri-checked cases (groovy-dynamic == java-static == interpreter) over both group shapes and a nesting fixture, each paired with the equivalent explicit field list so a disagreement cannot be read as a fixture defect, plus the discriminator that places the duplicate **inside one row across two fields**, which an implementation expanding per row and comparing within a field alone would miss, the nested row-2 discriminator above, and a positive control naming the nested field's own star so a `false` elsewhere cannot mean "the rule never evaluated". The authoring side this unblocked is `Dsl.fieldValuesNotUnique(GroupListRef, …)` (DG33), measured against the kernel in `validate.laws.FieldValuesNotUniqueAdmissionLawsTest`.

### IF288: a rare-case gate belongs behind a document-level precondition, not behind a cheaper key (FIXED 2026-08-13; closes IG127) <a id="if288"></a>

The over-repetition gate is coordinate-shaped, so [IF284](#if284) moved it OUT of the value-shaped admission memo and left it as an unmemoized prelude that every consumer re-answers for the same cell. Measured with `scripts/perf-keys.sh validate`, the `HashMap<String, PathInfo>` probe behind it was **10.33 %** of a full validation. Three shapes of fix were closed off before this one: putting the walk back in the key reinstates the list-in-key IF284 removed; a per-path memo of any shape **is** the same probe (built, measured at 11.36 % residual, reverted); and a cheaper packed cell key would destroy the value-sharing the memo exists for, since two cells holding one value must share an entry (except the code-based validator, [IF150](#if150)).

**The lever is not to ask per cell.** `AutoChecks.overRepetitionGateNeeded` answers ONCE per call whether the gate has to run for this document at all; when it does not — every ordinary document — the per-cell gate is skipped outright. It is named for the question it answers rather than for the fact it usually reports, because the two differ: a model whose declared maximum is below 1 needs the gate even for an empty document with no addresses in it. It is the third member of a family the same code already guards this way: `duplicates` and `unavailable` are coordinate-keyed sets that are empty in the ordinary case, and both are tested with `isNotEmpty()` first, on the path every call takes. Over-limit was the member without the guard, and finding it that way is the general form: **when a check is expensive because it is asked per cell, look for the sibling checks that are asked the same way and see which ones already have a precondition.**

**Soundness rests on scanning supersets of the emitter's own domains, not on an argument joining different ones.** The scan walks `Document.placedAddresses` (which carries present-but-empty cells, the addresses no valued placement holds and exactly what the empty branch consults) and the instantiated rows of every declared entity (the emitter iterates only BOUNDED groups). Scanning more addresses can only make a `false` answer stronger. Two links are closed rather than argued: a **maximum below 1** refuses the fast path outright, so the all-1s address `mandatoryErrors` synthesises for an absolutely-required field needs no reasoning about which branch it takes; and both scans resolve the path through the same `info`, so its **undeclared-path assertion keeps firing** — once per document instead of once per consultation. Skipping a gate silently skips whatever the gate asserted, which would have made a malformed placement pass on a clean document and still throw on an over-limit one. **A cold review caught this claim being false in the shipped draft:** a `maximums.isEmpty()` early return, added because it looked free, returned before either scan and so dropped the assertion for every model declaring no bounds — reachable through the public `ModelDef`, whose `groupPaths` defaults to empty. The lesson generalizes past this fix: **an early return added for speed silently discards every side effect the code after it was carrying**, and a precondition scan that doubles as an assertion has one.

**A precondition is a document-derived fact, so it takes the storage discipline this path already uses.** The *gate* was model-static-plus-arguments and held no memo, but the code around it is full of document-derived state — `Document.Index`, `ValueAdmissionCache.byValue`/`byCell`, `FormalResultCache.results`, `ValidationPass.aggregateMemo` — and every one of them is safe by one of two rules: it dies with the call, or it dies with the index on mutation. The flag takes the first: it lives on the per-call `ValueAdmissionCache`, never on the call-invariant `AutoChecks` or the interpreter, and is computed on the **staged** document `validate` builds rather than the caller's, a distinction no constructor could make whatever an interpreter costs to instantiate. Nothing here is a new hazard class; the point is that it is not exempt from the existing one.

**The path-major scan is loop-invariant hoisting, not the memo that failed.** A first version probed per cell and cost 2.16 % of the profile itself; `ModelPathIndex.anyExceedsBound` resolves one path's bounds once and then compares coordinates as integers. The falsified memo cached a handle ACROSS calls that arrive path-interleaved; a caller holding every coordinate of one path has no such miss.

**What this does NOT fix, since the gap row is deleted and nothing else would record it:** the prelude is now CONDITIONAL, not cheap. A document carrying even one over-limit address answers `true` and pays the full unmemoized per-cell probe on every consultation, exactly as before. That is the malformed-document case and it was judged not worth further machinery, but the deleted row's own closing step — resolving bounds once per cell on the record the formal walk already iterates — remains untaken and is the lever if that case ever matters.

*Locked by:* `OverLimitAddressPresenceMultiplatformTest` (JVM+JS) — the well-formed control that proves the fast path is taken at all, an unbounded repeatable row under an over-limit ancestor, a maximum below 1 on an EMPTY document (the case every contents-only formulation misclassifies), the staged-copy recompute, and the undeclared-path fail-fast. The does-fire tier is unchanged and still governs: `adapter.laws.OverRepetitionDiffTest`, `OverRepetitionFormalGateDiffTest`, `OverLimitParallelIterationDiffTest`. Numbers and their conditions: INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE "Checkpoint 2026-08-13".

### IF289: the relevance query's cost was rebuilding path strings, not the key it hashed (FIXED 2026-08-13; closes IG128) <a id="if289"></a>

`EffectiveRelevanceIndex.isRelevantWithin` is asked per cell in a partial validation. IG128 attributed its cost to the throwaway `List<Int>` it built per `(depth, pattern)` to probe a `HashSet<List<Int>>` — [IF284](#if284)'s shape with another multiplier. **The attribution was wrong about which member dominated**, and the profile that settles it is unambiguous: `StringBuilder.append` was **16.95 %** of a partial-validation profile with the `joinToString` family ~10 % more, against **~2.8 %** for the list-key mechanics the row named.

The mechanism: the query walked `depth` prefixes of its path and rebuilt each one as a String — `"/" + parts.take(depth).joinToString("/")` — then used that String twice as a `HashMap` key to resolve the level's buckets. Per relevance question that is one `pathParts` split, and per depth a `take`, a `joinToString`, and two map probes, all to recompute a **model-static** fact. The list key was real but small.
