**Why the misattribution was structural, not careless.** `perf-keys.sh` reports the nearest interpreter caller of a `java.util.*` **key mechanic**, so it sees hash probes and is blind to string building by construction. `isRelevantWithin` topped that view at 12 %, which is a true statement about hash probes and reads as a statement about the function's cost. The general lesson: **an instrument that filters by mechanism ranks callers WITHIN that mechanism, never against the ones it filters out** — so a hot frame it surfaces still needs a plain self-time profile before its cost is explained.

The fix memoizes, per queried path, one entry per ancestor level carrying that level's buckets — model-static data keyed by the path `String`, whose hash the platform has cached. That is emphatically not the defect IF284 removed, which put a repetition **list** in a key so that hashing walked it on every lookup; it is the same shape as `Document.partsByPath`. The probe key also dropped from two allocations to one (`take` then `mapIndexed` became a single `List(levels)` build).

Measured on the `validate-partial` driver, both arms without JFR: **3,329 → 11,108 `validatePart` calls in 20 s (3.34×)**. `isRelevantWithin`'s whole subtree fell from **72.84 % to 33.79 %** of the profile.

**The second half, landed the same day — the probe key IG128 originally named.** Removing the string building promoted it to the top frame (`projectedKey` **8.23 %**, `ArrayList` hash/equals ~7 % behind), so it was real, just not dominant. The tuple is now an `IntArray`-backed `RepKey` instead of a `List<Int>`, and the probe instance is **reused** across lookups, which is sound for one reason worth stating: a `Set.contains` never retains the key it is given. Cumulative throughput **3,329 → 11,880 calls in 20 s (3.57x)**; `projectedKey` and both `ArrayList` mechanics leave the top-18 self frames entirely, replaced by `RepKey.reset` + `RepKey.hashCode` at **6.57 %** combined against the **15.47 %** the three of them cost together.

A reused mutable key needs a discriminator that a single query cannot supply, because a leak or a stale field changes a LATER answer rather than its own. `theReusableProbeKeyDoesNotDisturbLaterQueries` interleaves a hit, a miss, a **shorter** probe, then repeats the first two — and it does fail on the obvious mutant (`length` updated only when it grows), which also takes two neighbouring tests with it.

*Locked by:* `EffectiveRelevanceIndexTest` — the existing readings plus `eachAncestorLevelIsMatchedAgainstItsOwnTuples`, which puts buckets at two levels of one path and decides three queries at different levels, so a plan that collapsed or mis-indexed them cannot produce the expected triple.

### IF290: the last three list-in-key memos, measured on a scenario built to execute them (2026-08-13; closes IG126) <a id="if290"></a>

IG126 was reopened because four of five memos read 0 on scenarios that structurally could not run them, and a 0 from unreachable code is not evidence of cheapness. `indexColumns` closed on the partial driver; the other three needed constructs no scenario carried. `PerfCatalog.partialMemoProbe` supplies them — a **starred** aggregate and a **`RepetitionNotUnique`** rule, evaluated through `validatePart`, with `Amount` duplicated in pairs so RNU builds real clusters rather than finding none and returning early. Driver `EvalProfileTest.loopPartialMemoProbeForProfiler`, reachable as `scripts/perf-keys.sh validate-memos`; 1,297 `ExecutionSample`s.

| memo | its enclosing frame | reading |
|---|---|---|
| `DmInterpreter.starRelevantOf`'s `decided` | **5 samples, 0.39 %** | **measured cold.** The frame is present through two distinct callers (`evalFunc`, `validatePart`), which is what the previous 0 could not establish |
| `groupRelevanceOf`'s `HashMap<String, HashMap<List<Int>, …>>` | **1 sample, 0.08 %** | **measured cold**, frame present |
| `RepetitionNotUniqueEvaluator`'s row cache | **534 samples, 41.17 %** | **measured HOT** — see below |

**The denominator matters and is stated rather than buried:** this scenario carries two rules and is built to stress RNU, so 41 % is a share of a probe, not of a realistic workload. What the probe establishes is the *shape* of RNU's cost, which no proportion of a mixed model would isolate.

**Inside that 41 %, two distinct mechanisms, and only one of them is the class IG126 was tracking.** `ArrayList.hashCodeRange` is the immediately-enclosing frame for **16.11 %** of all samples — the `groupBy { it.errorReps.take(scopeLen) }` scope key and the `groupBy { it.key }` cluster key, each a fresh list hashed by walking it. Separately, `MutableBigInteger.divideOneWord` + `divideKnuth` + `BigDecimal.<init>` account for **~14 %**: `rnuNumericKey` re-normalizes every NUMBER key cell through `BigDecimal` on every call. The second is not a key mechanic at all and would never have surfaced in the view that opened this class.

**The disposition is deliberate.** IG126 asked whether these memos had ever been measured on code that runs them; that question is now answered for all five, so it closes. The RNU cost is **newly discovered work with its own mechanisms**, not a leftover of IG126, so it opens its own row (IG129) with a completion gate rather than riding out as a sentence in a closing finding. Note for whoever takes it: `outcomes` runs ONCE per pass and its O(rows) shape is necessary work, so the avoidable part is the key hashing, and the two `groupBy` keys are not equally tractable — the scope prefix is a coordinate tuple, while the cluster key is a `List<KeyAtom?>` whose comparison is the operator's semantics. Split them before optimizing either.

### IF291: RNU's cost is proportional to rows, not multiplied per consultation — and a value memo makes it worse (2026-08-13; closes IG129) <a id="if291"></a>

`RepetitionNotUniqueEvaluator` is present in **41.17 %** of the probe scenario built to run it ([IF290](#if290)), which looked like the list-in-key family's next member. It is not, and the distinction is the one that decides whether a cost is worth attacking.

**The family this class tracks is a cost MULTIPLIED per consultation** — [IF284](#if284)'s memo was re-probed by every consumer of every cell, [IF289](#if289)'s relevance query is asked per cell, and [IF288](#if288)'s gate was re-answered by four consumers. RNU is the opposite shape: `outcomes` runs **once per pass** and hashes each participant row's two `groupBy` keys **once each**. Two hashes per row is *proportional to the work*, which is the accumulator classification IF285 already established for most of the class's declaration sites. A profile share says nothing about which of the two shapes you are looking at, and that is exactly why the 41 % needed a mechanism reading rather than a ranking.

**The `BigDecimal` half was attacked and the attempt was FALSIFIED, which is the useful part.** `rnuNumericKey` (`decOrNull(raw)?.scale19()`) measured ~14 % of the probe, and it is a pure function of the raw text, so a call-local `raw → Dec` memo is the obvious move. Measured on the probe's own throughput: **9,921 → 9,666 `validatePart` calls in 20 s**, i.e. slightly WORSE. The reason generalizes and is why this should not be retried: **RNU exists to check UNIQUENESS, so its key columns are mostly distinct by construction** — a value memo therefore misses on nearly every row and adds a `HashMap` write to each. The probe is already unrealistically duplicate-heavy (every value appears exactly twice) and even there the memo does not pay. The change was reverted rather than banked, on the same rule that reverted the one-slot path memo.

What is left is a floor rather than a defect: each distinct key value must be parsed once, and each row's key must be hashed once. Reducing the constant would mean a primitive-backed key for the scope tuple, worth roughly half of the smaller of the two mechanisms, on a path whose share of a realistic multi-rule document is a fraction of this probe's 41 %.

*Measured with:* `scripts/perf-keys.sh validate-memos`; numbers and the denominator caveat in INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE "Checkpoint 2026-08-13 — the last three list-in-key memos".

### IF292: the cross-engine early-warning band — per-row, sized from the row's own history, and warn-only by measurement (2026-08-13; closes IG121) <a id="if292"></a>

The comparative gates fire only at **parity**: the interpreter must beat both kernel strategies. So a row whose margin falls from `21x` to `1.1x` passes every night, and the estate learns nothing until the catastrophe. IG121's first piece was a band between the two, and it took three attempts because the first two were built before the quantity they needed existed.

**What was falsified, in order, and why each looked right.** A **flat constant** (`0.5`) rested on margins of `5x`–`17x` measured on the two scenarios that carried a bar at the time; once the bar reached all seventeen, the tightest row sat at `0.52` and a flat band would have fired on it from the first run. A floor derived from **the spread between a run's interleaved pairs** was the obvious repair and is the wrong quantity: a pair ratio divides two three-iteration medians while the reported row divides two nine-sample medians, so it is dispersed by construction — `FVNU-uniqueness @250` spread `3.21x` between its pairs against a run-to-run spread near `1.3x`. Both were removed rather than kept.

**What works is `k` robust deviations below the row's own median margin, over the committed series' CURRENT level** — which only became computable once `perf-trend.sh` could find each row's change point and report the noise of the level *after* it. A fraction of the median cannot substitute: it gives two rows with equal medians the same floor however differently they wander, so on the real record a `0.50x` fraction tripped only the two noisiest rows. In deviations, a quiet ±5 % row is guarded just under its median and a ±25 % row far below.

**The band warns and never fails, and that is a measured conclusion.** Backtesting over 27 runs and 68 rows: `0.80x` of the median would have fired 72 times, `0.45x` three times, `3σ` nineteen, `6σ` five, `8σ` twice. **No candidate reaches zero**, because the record contains genuinely contended runs whose *kernel* arms rose 18–39 %, and a margin cannot separate "the interpreter regressed" from "this run was contended". A band that failed the build would therefore violate the standing rule that the nightly must never fail on a false positive. Shipped at **`8σ`**: 62 of 68 rows banded, two historical firings.

**Six rows are deliberately absent, and absence means "not banded" rather than "banded at zero".** Their floor would land at or below parity — `CO-correlation @200` vs Java has a median margin of `2.05x` and a spread of ±13.8 %, so `8σ` below it is `0.73x`, a warning the parity gate makes unreachable. The emitter refuses those rows and says so on stderr; `MarginBand` reports them `unbanded`, never `ok`, because a refreshed scenario fingerprint changes the key and a band that quietly passes a row it no longer covers is how this bar would disappear.

*Locked by:* `PerfMarginBandTest` (the decision on scripted numbers — including that parity is judged BEFORE the floor, which only an input under both can discriminate) and `PerformanceRecordShapeGuardTest` (the `margin` row's shape, its status vocabulary, and the floor-present-iff-not-unbanded pairing). Authored by `EMIT_FLOORS=1 BAND_SIGMA=8 scripts/perf-trend.sh --ratio`, the same computation the backtest prints, so what ships is what was measured. First run carrying the rows: [`2026-08-13T005257Z`](../perf-results/darwin-aarch64/2026-08-13T005257Z.jsonl) — 62 `ok`, 6 `unbanded`, tightest at `1.13x` of its floor.

### IF293: a hosted absolute anchor is a trend line, not a bar — its runner class is noisier than any tolerance worth setting (2026-08-13; graduated from IG124) <a id="if293"></a>

The hosted block was authored from runs of `1845f8af` and the code moved 2–5× past it, so its ceilings described nothing. Re-authoring it needed the revision on the remote, which is why the row outlived its usefulness. Refreshed from two sequential runs of `15b3ed0`; the dated measurements are in INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE's "Checkpoint 2026-08-13 — three sequential hosted runs". What the exercise established is worth more than the refresh.

**The runner class spans CPU models within one hour, and it does not matter.** Three sequential dispatches of one revision landed on `AMD EPYC 7763` twice and `AMD EPYC 9V74` once, so `perf-anchor.sh` refused the fold — correctly, since absolute times are a property of the machine. But the **same-machine** control is the finding: run-to-run spread there reaches `2.05×`, matching the `2.01×` measured *across* the two models, with the outliers in the `> 20 ms` lanes rather than in sub-millisecond ones. Machine identity is therefore not this class's dominant variance source, and IF280's re-open trigger ("decide whether that block is a bar or only a trend line") resolves to **trend line**: `ANCHOR_TOLERANCE` is `1.65×`, *below* the class's own noise, so no hosted tolerance is both meaningful and quiet.

**That is survivable only because the release gate is local.** `TimingEnforcement.forAbsoluteAnchor` returns `GATED` solely in the isolated lane on a non-shared machine, and `scripts/publish-release.sh` now runs the perf gate there and aborts on a breach ([RELEASE-SPEC §5a](RELEASE-SPEC.md)). So the bar that stops a release never runs on hosted hardware, and the hosted flag carries no release weight — which is what made "hosted is a trend line" an acceptable answer rather than a loss of coverage.

**Two mechanisms this closed, both invisible until the ceilings were tightened.** A hosted breach recorded `breached`, which `perf-anchor.sh` excludes from a fold — so one noise-driven flag dropped that lane from the artifact and the *next* refresh was refused for incomplete coverage, defeating the documented recovery path with the noise the flag exists to tolerate. It records `flagged` now, which folds. And the margin band's floor derivation could emit a floor just above parity when `best_split` missed a change point, which reads as coverage and can never fire; the emitter now refuses anything below `MIN_FLOOR` (1.5×).

**Residual, recorded rather than hidden:** the block is a **2-run** median, not 3, because the third run measured different silicon. Nothing requires three (no guard asserts it, and the median-of-three prerequisite was justified for the *tightened tolerance on a gated lane*, which hosted is not), and a third matching run is a lottery now that the provider is measured to mix models within the hour. On a lane that only flags, against a class whose own spread is `2.05×`, the precision difference between a 2- and 3-sample median sits far below the noise it lives in.

*Locked by:* `PerfAnchorScriptTest` — the machine-fold refusal that fired here for real, plus the `flagged`-folds / `breached`-excluded pair; `PerfMarginBandTest` and `PerformanceRecordShapeGuardTest` for the band's vocabulary; `PerfTrendScriptTest` for the emitter. The hosted margin band is armed at 68 floors, authored from all six committed hosted runs — margins are ratios measured inside one run, so they divide out machine and contention ([IF281](#if281)) and remain comparable across both.

### IF294: a release refusal that nothing can drive is not a refusal — extracting it found one that never worked (2026-08-13; closes IG133) <a id="if294"></a>

The release performance gate refused a run carrying no evidence in five ways, all written as branches inside `publish-release.sh`. Nothing could execute them: that script builds native images and pushes to a public mirror, so its refusals were verified by running their arithmetic **by hand, one layer down** — the pipeline in a shell, not the branch in the script.

**That is exactly where the defect hid.** Under the script's own `set -euo pipefail`, `SKIPPED="$(grep … | grep … | awk …)"` **aborts at the assignment** when the XML glob matches nothing, so the `NO RESULT XMLs` diagnostic below it was unreachable (executed 2026-08-13 against an absent directory: exit 1, nothing printed). The release still halted, so this was a diagnostic hole rather than an escape — but the check that shipped was not the check that was advertised, and the hand-run pipeline could never have shown it, because the abort is a property of the *enclosing script*, not of the pipeline. **A refusal's oracle is the refusal executing in its own context.**

**The fix is structural, not another branch.** The logic moved to [`scripts/release-perf-gate.sh`](../scripts/release-perf-gate.sh), which takes its gate command, log path and result directory as seams so a self-test can fabricate each condition, and the sum became a single `awk` over the files — one command, always exit 0, so the *branch* decides rather than `set -e`. `publish-release.sh` now calls it and `ReleasePerfGateRefusalTest` additionally refuses a second copy of the messages there, since an inlined re-implementation would be untested again the moment it drifted.

**Freshness became structural too, and an mtime check was written first and rejected.** The lane's result directory is deleted *before* the gate runs, so any XML read back was necessarily written by that invocation. Comparing each file against a reference file's mtime looked more direct and is a false-red generator: `-nt` can compare whole seconds, so a gate writing its XML inside the same second as the reference would be refused as stale — on a release path, for a condition no test reproduces reliably. What the timestamp check was reaching for is covered one layer out, where it belongs: a build-cache restore is excluded by `perfGateTest` being non-cacheable and always-executing, pinned by `GateTierWiringTest`.

**The control's control.** Nine refusal cases are satisfied by a script that refuses everything, so the tenth is a clean run that must **pass**. Three mutants were executed against the suite: dropping the clear-then-run, restoring the pipefail-prone pipeline, and an unconditional refusal — each caught by the case built for it.

*Locked by:* `ReleasePerfGateRefusalTest` (hosting `scripts/selftest-release-perf-gate.sh`: failed, vacuous ×2, absent, empty, stale, skipped, summed skips, no-`skipped`-attribute, clean) in the input-declared `deliveryGuardTest`; the delegation assertion pins that `publish-release.sh` calls the guarded gate and holds no second copy of its messages.

### IF295: an elapsed-time assertion is a bar only where it can be isolated — `:interpreter` now holds none (2026-08-13; closes IG132) <a id="if295"></a>

`:interpreter` carried four elapsed-time assertions, and the module ran in **no CI lane at all**: `./build.sh --dry-run` for `test`, `:adapter:test` and `:noBackendTest` selects no interpreter task, and `ci.yml` named none. So the **JS/Node target was covered nowhere in CI**, which is the larger half of what this closed and is not a performance question.

**The census had to be exact, and the first one was not.** Three classes were found by sweeping `nanoTime`/`currentTimeMillis`/`Date.now`/`performance.now`; a cold review's anchored clock-API enumeration added `kotlin.time` and found a fourth, `PartialRelevanceDepthTest` (`elapsed < 300`). The complete list over `interpreter/src` is nine files, of which `Model.kt` mentions a clock only in prose and three are worker/footprint **reporters** that measure and write a sample while asserting nothing — leaving exactly four bars. An absence claim needs its enumeration stated with its recall limits, which is what the first sweep skipped.

**Two of the four were catastrophe fuses that measured nothing the estate did not already measure better.** `DocumentScaleTest` (`elapsed < 20.seconds`, twice) and `InterpreterMultiplatformTest.scalesToManyRepetitionRows` (`elapsed < 5.seconds`) exist to trip on a reintroduced O(N²). On **JS that bound is dead weight** — Mocha's hard 2000 ms per-test cap fires an order of magnitude earlier — and on the JVM the same regression is measured properly, as a paired N/4N ratio under isolation, by 19 sub-quadratic scenarios in `:adapter`'s routine tier. Both assertions are deleted; the elapsed time is still **printed**, because a number a human reads costs nothing and decides nothing. `scalesToManyRepetitionRows` was additionally self-contradictory: its comment claimed "no hard bound" while the method ended in one.

**The other two were real bars in the wrong place, and moved.** `PartialRelevanceDepthTest`'s cost half became `adapter/…/perf/RelevanceQueryDepthPerfTest`, and `StarRelevanceReductionGrowthTest` became `StarRelevanceReductionPerfTest` — both `@Tag("perf")`, both now inside the isolated single-fork lane with the shared machine-conditional enforcement, so a hosted breach flags rather than fails like every other growth floor. The depth guard was **strengthened** by the move: `elapsed < 300` ms answers "was this machine fast enough", where the claim is "cost does not grow with depth", so it is now a growth ratio across depth 12 → 24 — machine-independent, and sitting ~450x below an exponential's `2^12`. The star reduction's hand-rolled retry loop collapsed into `assertGrowthBelowCatastropheFloor`, which is the same behaviour plus the shared enforcement. Both carry a **vacuity guard**: a ratio over two measurements that round to zero passes whatever the engine did.

**The lane is `:interpreter:allTests`, not `:interpreter:check`.** `check` additionally pulls browser-consumer preparation, ABI dumps, the TypeScript API tool, Dokka and Maven/npm staging — release machinery, on every push. `allTests` is `jvmTest` + `jsNodeTest` + the module doc guard, measured at **33 s** locally (`jsBrowserTest` is SKIPPED — no browser target is declared).

*Locked by:* `GateTierWiringTest.pushCiOwnsCorrectnessAndPersistsTheGradleCache` pins `:interpreter:allTests` in `ci.yml` (mutant executed: removing it fails the guard); `PerfTestClassificationTest` holds the two moved classes to the name/tag convention; the two moved bars run in `:adapter:perfGateTest` (verified: 1 test each, 0 skipped, in the tag-selected lane).

### IF296: a measurement that records nothing is invisible to every trend instrument — the seam, not the lane, was the defect (2026-08-13; closes IG130) <a id="if296"></a>

Seven of the nine routine-tier classes left **zero** rows in the committed record: all 138 observations of the first tag-selected gate run came from `PerfGateTest` (36 `accepted`) and `KernelParityGateTest` (102 `unasserted`). They executed and could fail their own growth floors, but `perf-history.sh`, `perf-trend.sh`'s change-point detection, the cross-engine margin band and the absolute anchor are all built on the record, so a drift in those lanes stayed invisible until it crossed a catastrophe floor 6×–1000× away.

**The root cause is one layer up from the lanes.** The record-and-arm step lived inside `PerfGateTest`'s **private** `assertSubQuadratic` helper, so it was not reachable from any other class — every other lane called the raw `assertGrowthBelowCatastropheFloor`, which asserts a ratio and records nothing. Fixing it lane by lane would have been eight patches of the same defect; the fix is `PerfHarness.assertSubQuadraticLane` (growth floor + record + anchor) with `PerfGateTest`'s helper reduced to a delegate, plus the same step inside `KernelAdvantage.assertBeatsKernelAndScales` for the cross-engine entry-point lanes. **`PerfAnchorCoverageGuardTest.noRoutineTierClassBypassesTheRecordingSeam` refuses the raw primitive in any `@Tag("perf")` class**, which is what stops the class recurring.

**Why the cross-engine lanes need an anchor too, though both their bars are relative.** `assertBeatsKernelAndScales` compares the interpreter to itself at two scales and to the kernel in the same run. Both are ratios, so a slowdown that moves every arm together passes them unchanged — the same blindness the growth floors have, one construct over.

**Two mechanism defects surfaced only by USING the arming path**, and neither is visible from source:

- **The refresh picked the wrong environment.** `find perf-results … | sort | tail -1` sorts whole **paths**, so the environment directory sorts ahead of the timestamp and `linux-amd64-ci/…` beats every `darwin-aarch64/…` however old. A fresh local gate run was ignored and the refresh rewrote the **linux** block from a stale record, downgrading a committed 2-run median to a 1-run block measured on different silicon. Sorting on the basename fixes it; `RESULTS_DIR` now exists so the default-input path — the one a maintainer actually types, and the only one with no test — is executable by `PerfAnchorScriptTest`.
- **Arming a lane cost two gate runs, and a two-lane class four.** `assertWithinBudget` records an unanchored observation and rethrows, so a lane whose small scale was unanchored never recorded its large one, and the refresh can only author keys the record contains. Both endpoints now record before either is allowed to fail — the same exception still fails the same test.

**One estimator was the documented anti-pattern.** `KernelAdvantage.timeMs` timed `repeats` calls in a single region and divided, which is a **mean**, and a mean is dragged by one contended slice. Its two lanes had the widest 3-run spread of all 64 anchored keys (`computeField` **1.89×** on `[3.13, 5.84, 3.09]`, `applyComputations` 1.82×) — wider than the 1.65× anchor tolerance itself, so an armed lane sat one unlucky run from a false red. It is a median now, and its growth bar re-measures before failing like every other growth floor in the estate; it was the only one that did not, and it produced a false red (`computeField` 6.74× against a 6.0× bar inside a full gate at load ~9, then 4.4× in isolation on the same code).

*Measured after:* **166** observation rows across **33** lanes from **11** classes, against 138 rows from 2. Six consecutive isolated gate runs green, 0 unanchored, 64 armed keys. Per-key 3-run spread: median **1.14×**, and four keys still reach the 1.65× tolerance on a machine running gates back-to-back — recorded as its own gap, since "armed" and "the tolerance clears this machine's noise" are different claims.

*Locked by:* `PerfAnchorCoverageGuardTest` (the seam holds the anchor call; no routine-tier class bypasses it; every anchored runner class carries the same lane set) and `PerfAnchorScriptTest.theDefaultInputIsTheNewestRunByStampAcrossEveryEnvironment` (mutant executed: restoring the path sort fails it).

### IF297: the lane boundary needs two mechanisms, because neither can see what the other does (2026-08-13; closes IG131) <a id="if297"></a>

`PerfTestClassificationTest` enforces the convention at its two syntactic ends — a `*PerfTest` name implies a lane tag, a lane tag implies a measuring name — and is deliberately unable to see the third case: a clock-reading assertion under a **neutral name with no tag**. A cold review found one already in the estate and constructed the bypass that defeats the obvious fix.

**Two mechanisms, and the pair is the point.** A runtime seam alone is defeated by a test that never calls the harness; a source census alone cannot see a measurement taken through a helper whose own source holds the clock.

- **`PerfLaneGate`** sits on `PerfHarness`'s four measuring entry points (`measure`, `measureCurrentThreadCpu`, `timeInterpreter`, `timeInterpreterCpu`) and refuses when the calling class is in no lane. It walks the stack out of the harness's own infrastructure, so call depth and lambda nesting do not matter. The **asserting** helpers are deliberately not gated: `assertGrowthBelowCatastropheFloor` and `assertWithinBudget` take their estimate as a value, and five harness unit tests call them on **scripted** numbers — gating the closure-executing measure functions instead of the bar-asserting ones is exactly what separates measured from scripted without guessing.
- **`RawClockCensusTest`** reads the sources for the clock APIs themselves, closing the direct-`System.nanoTime()` bypass.

**The exemption rule is derived, not enumerated**, so it cannot rot: a performance lane tag, an `@EnabledIfSystemProperty` gate (a diagnostic that returns on its first line unless asked for by name), named infrastructure, or an explicit entry carrying its reason. Both halves use the same rule, so they cannot drift into disagreeing about what a lane is. The allowlist has an anti-decay guard of its own — an entry whose file no longer reads a clock fails the build — and it fired immediately, on a `PerfAnchors.kt` entry that was already obsolete when written.

**Three defects in the mechanisms, each caught by their own controls rather than by review:**

- **The owner-class resolution was lenient.** `name.substringBefore('$')` was meant to strip Kotlin's synthetic lambda classes and also stripped genuine nesting, so every nested helper resolved to its enclosing class and inherited that class's lane — a tagged outer class would have laundered an untagged inner one. Walking out only of synthetic/anonymous/local classes is the correct form.
- **The census matched prose.** `PerfLaneGate` and the census itself name `System.nanoTime()` in their own documentation to explain the bypass they close, and both were duly reported as offenders. Comments are stripped now; the census's self-match on its own pattern literal is an explicit allowlist entry, the same shape as `KernelParityCoverageGuardTest` matching every helper-name detector ever tried.
- **A method-level gate is not a class-level one.** `ModelReportSinglePassTest`'s single timed method is `@EnabledIfSystemProperty`-gated in Java, i.e. indented, and the class-level pattern does not match it. Rather than weaken the pattern — which would exempt a class that mixes a gated timing method with an ungated one — it is an explicit entry with that reasoning.

*Locked by:* `PerfLaneGateTest` (six controls: an untagged class refused, both lane tags admitted, a property-gated diagnostic admitted, the one allowlisted class admitted, and an end-to-end case that drives a real measuring entry point from an unlaned caller — without which a predicate nobody calls would satisfy the other five) and `RawClockCensusTest` (a mutant executed: a neutrally named test reading `System.nanoTime()` directly is reported by file and line excerpt).

### IF298: a routine-tier lane's run-to-run noise is a function of its MAGNITUDE — batch it out of the sub-10 ms band (2026-08-14; closes IG134) <a id="if298"></a>

Arming every routine-tier lane ([IF296](#if296)) exposed the next question: for several keys the `1.65× + 1 ms` anchor tolerance was not a bar but a formality, held up by the slack term rather than by margin over the noise. **Both the denominator and the mechanism were wrong on the first pass**, and each was corrected by an oracle rather than by argument.

**The denominator.** Four keys were named from the `max/min` of three runs. `perf-trend.sh` already computed run-to-run noise over the whole record, and over 174 keys it said: estate median **±7.3%**, with **14** keys at or above ±20% over at least 7 runs. Three of those were never among the four; two of the four were not among the worst. `max/min` of three samples routinely reaches 1.5–1.7× on a ±20% distribution, so the statistic picked almost the wrong set — the record's own instrument was already measuring the right quantity.

**The mechanism, with the hypothesis it replaced.** The obvious reading was that wall-clock lanes are contention-exposed where CPU-clock lanes are protected. **Falsified:** wall lanes median **±6.9%** (n=124), CPU lanes **±9.4%** (n=50), and CPU carried 8 of the 14 noisy keys from a quarter of the rows. The discriminator that holds is **magnitude**: bucketed by median, `0-2 ms` 1 noisy of 34, `2-5 ms` 6/37, `5-10 ms` 7/29, `10-30 ms` **0/41**, `30 ms+` **0/33**. Every noisy key in the estate was under 10 ms; nothing above it was noisy.

**Why more sampling cannot fix it, measured.** The lanes already ran `warmup=5, iters=7` inside `pairedGrowth`'s 5 pairs — a median of 35 samples. Raising warmups and iteration counts moved the count of over-tolerance keys from 5 to **7**, i.e. not at all: the variance lives *between* JVM runs, not within one. Only lifting the measured median out of the band moved it.

**The corrective, and the trap in it.** A batch inside the timed block multiplies the median and cancels out of the growth ratio, which is a quotient. But a batch **changes what one observation means**, so the key must move with it — done by hand, the first batched lane was re-keyed and the second was not, and its next gate run breached its own stale anchor. `assertSubQuadraticLane` and `assertBeatsKernelAndScales` therefore take `batch` and derive the key from it, while sampling parameters stay out of the key by the same rule.

**A second, distinct mechanism surfaced at the end.** With everything batched, one key remained at 1.68× on `[28.73, 18.36, 17.06]` — an 18 ms median, far outside the magnitude band. One high reading followed by two consistent ones is the first-run-after-rebuild shape, and it takes a deeper warm-up, not a bigger batch. Naming the two separately is the point: the same symptom had two causes and one corrective would have been credited with both.

*The staged measurements — five stages, three sequential isolated gates each — are in [INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE](INTERPRETER-PERFORMANCE.md)'s results log under the 2026-08-14 checkpoint. The shape they establish: over-tolerance keys 5 → 7 (more sampling) → 2 → 1 → **0 of 64**, median spread 1.19× → 1.10×.*

*Locked by:* `PerfAnchorResourceTest` (the key grammar a batched identity must still satisfy) and the gate itself, which refuses an observation whose key has no committed anchor — so a batch that changed the work without changing the key fails rather than passing quietly. The ongoing instrument is `scripts/perf-trend.sh`, which reports per-key noise over the accumulated record and needs no three-run ritual.

### IF299: the field-list quantifiers' group operand was already correct — the shared-checker inference held, and measuring it is what makes that knowable (MEASURED 2026-08-14; closes IG135) <a id="if299"></a>

[KF173](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf173) records that `FirstFilledValue` routes through the same shared entity-list checker as `FieldValuesNotUnique`, whose group-operand contract is fully measured ([IF287](#if287)). IG135 refused to accept that as evidence, on the grounds that routing through one checker is a **source-level reading, not a measured mechanism** — and that the `FieldValuesNotUnique` history is the reason to distrust it, since the same shape produced two silent wrong answers in this evaluator, each found only by a differential. Measured, on both kernel strategies: **the inference was right this time.** No code changed.

**Why the negative is worth its two files.** IF287 is thirteen instances of this exact class, and *four* of that session's predictions were structurally sound and did not manifest. A shared-checker argument that happens to hold is indistinguishable, before measurement, from the ones that didn't — so what this closes is not "the code is fine" but "we now know which of the two it was."

**`FirstFilledValue` is the family member a boolean lock cannot cover, and that is why it was measured first.** It answers a **value**, so a wrong expansion ORDER returns the wrong cell's contents *while still firing* — invisible to any fires/does-not-fire assertion. Both axes agree with the kernel: the only filled cell in nested row 2 is found (the row-1-pin discriminator), and the direct field is encountered **before** the nested rows, with the rival answer asserted false on the same document so the claim is two-sided rather than one.

**All seven fill quantifiers were then measured over the extent none of their existing locks reaches.** The estate's group-operand fill locks all place the repeatable level AT the operand or under a single row; IG136 records why that is blind ("every current group-scope fixture is flat or guards on a non-repeatable field, so the plan binds `Once` and nothing is pinned"). Over a NON-repeatable group holding one direct field and a nested repeatable subgroup, all seven agree on both spellings and all three engines. Two of the seven are the actual discriminators — `MoreThanOneFieldFilled` and `NotExactlyOneFieldFilled` are the only ones whose answer changes between "two filled cells" and the one filled cell a row-1-pinned expansion would see; the other five agree under either reading and are carried as controls that the quantifiers evaluated at all.

*Locked by:* `adapter.laws.FirstFilledValueGroupOperandDiffTest` (7 rows: the nested-row-2 pin, the encounter-order pair, an explicit-list control on each axis, a negative, and an empty control) and `adapter.laws.NestedGroupFillQuantifierDiffTest` (the seven quantifiers × group + explicit-list spelling × three engines, asserted as one table so a divergence names which quantifier moved, plus a guard that the table never loses its discriminating rows).
